Indutrade · INDT-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Indutrade memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Indutrade was written: 54,147 words, against 11,150 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 26,379 words · written 22 October 2026, 17:17 to 17:40 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Indutrade (INDT-SE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Investment Analyst, written 2026-10-22. Scores: 100 most positive for an investment case, 1 most negative, 50 neutral.

Reader's note: the facts the Consumer must carry into the model, and the traps in the files

  1. Timing. Today is 22 October 2026. The Q3 2026 report is due tomorrow, 23 October 2026. Everything below is written before that print; the latest reported period is Q2 2026 (1 April to 30 June 2026, reported 16 July 2026). The instruction's caution about the latest Earnings Review is resolved: that review states the period it covers, and it is the June 2026 quarter.
  2. The two-year shape. Organic sales growth by quarter: Q1 2025 0%, Q2 2025 -4%, Q3 2025 -1%, Q4 2025 0%, Q1 2026 0%, Q2 2026 +5%. Organic order growth: Q1 2026 +1%, Q2 2026 +8%; book-to-bill 107% and 105%; backlog +8% year on year at June 2026. Reported EBITA margin: FY2023 15.0%, FY2024 14.4%, FY2025 13.8% (14.1% excluding SEK 125m of UK project write-downs), Q1 2026 13.3%, Q2 2026 14.7% (14.9% excluding a SEK 14m earn-out remeasurement). Reported basic EPS: FY2023 7.86, FY2024 7.55, FY2025 7.03, H1 2026 3.79 against 3.46 (+9.5%), with Q2 2026 2.15 (+23%).
  3. The EPS consensus is a blend of two bases, and the headline growth rate is an artefact. FactSet's FY2026 median EPS of SEK 10.01 (mean 9.57) sits against a FY2025 "actual" of 7.03, implying +42%. That is false. Three visible brokers forecast on a reported basis (Pareto 8.46, Goldman Sachs 8.71, a restricted broker 8.40; Redeye separately 8.52) and three on an adjusted basis that adds back amortisation of acquired intangibles (DNB Carnegie 10.40, Nordea 10.30, a restricted broker 9.84). Amortisation of acquired intangibles is about SEK 650 to 700m a year (company EBITA SEK 4,450m against FactSet adjusted EBIT SEK 3,774m in FY2025), roughly SEK 1.4 to 1.5 per share after tax, so FY2025 adjusted EPS was about SEK 8.4 to 8.5. Like for like, both camps expect FY2026 EPS growth of roughly 18 to 24%, and FY2027 of about 13%. The Consumer should model EBITA and EPS on one stated basis and never read the FactSet "+42%" as growth. The same artefact explains the FY2025 consensus "falling 16% over twelve months" (the base switched from adjusted to reported at actualisation).
  4. Other figure conflicts in the files, carried rather than reconciled: FY2025 organic sales growth is stated as -1% and -2% (the annual report says comparable units -2%); the UK write-down as SEK 125m and SEK 128m; the trading-versus-proprietary mix as "60% technical trading, 40% proprietary" (CEO, Capital Markets Day November 2025; Q2 2026 review: 58%/42%) and, in the Business Foundations digest, the reverse (57% proprietary). I use the company's own statement: roughly 58 to 60% technical trading, 40 to 42% proprietary products. Gross margin is 35.4 to 36.0% on the company's definition and 33.1 to 33.4% on FactSet's standardised line; use one.
  5. Leverage definitions: FactSet net debt SEK 9,504m at June 2026 (includes leases and earn-outs), 1.65x EBITDA; the company's financial net debt to EBITDA excluding earn-outs and leases is 1.2x; net debt to equity 45%; undrawn facilities SEK 6.3bn; S&P BBB- with a positive outlook.
  6. The prior memo's conditions have been met. The August 2026 Triaging memo asked for two quarters of positive organic order intake (delivered: Q1 +1%, Q2 +8%) and a permanent chief financial officer (Anna Vilogorac, from Ratos, in post by 14 January 2027). The dashboard ratings as at 2026-10-21: sell-side momentum A, technical momentum B, valuation C; price SEK 267.2, 52-week high 272.2, 18.8% above the 200-day average.

External change forces

C5510 questions
C-0158C-0248C-0352C-0452C-0558C-0665C-0742C-0860C-0948C-1055

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external picture has moved from "demand down, rates easing" to "demand turning, rates and yields rising" within the last six months. For an operating model, the demand turn is the more important fact: it is broad, order-led, backed by completed destocking and by the structural Life Science and energy strands, and it runs against easy comparisons through Q4 2026. The Consumer should model organic growth of roughly 4 to 6% for H2 2026, 3 to 5% for 2027 and 3 to 4% thereafter, with Life Science above and Infrastructure & Construction below the group, and should hold the gross margin flat. For the financial model, the macro regime is the larger fact: currency translation is unhedged and large, and the sector de-rates on yields regardless of a company's own delivery, so reported growth and the multiple can both lag constant-currency operating progress. Nothing external is strong enough to drive a step change in the four-year numbers; the external forces set a moderate tailwind on earnings and a moderate headwind on valuation.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical change, and visibility

C58

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement. Demand has turned cyclically upward from a two-year trough, confirmed by one quarter of hard data, with one genuinely structural strand (Life Science) and one quasi-structural strand (electrification, data-centre cooling, water) inside it. Visibility is two to three quarters, not two years, so under the instruction's own rule the cyclical swing does not yet qualify as a visible multi-year swing. The Consumer should model a cyclical recovery that is real but front-loaded against easy comparisons, and should not extrapolate the Q2 2026 organic rate across the whole four-year horizon.

The reasoning. The last 12 to 18 months were a flat-to-negative organic trough (Q2 2025 to Q1 2026: -4%, -1%, 0%, 0%). The last six months changed that in a way that is broad enough to be believed: organic orders +8% in Q2 2026 against organic sales +5%, four of five business areas growing organically, more than half the subsidiaries reporting positive development, and a book-to-bill above 100% for the third consecutive quarter. The structure of the turn matters for the model. Life Science (23% of sales; medtech and pharma 21% of the customer mix) grew 13% organically with orders +16% to +23%, and did so after deliveries to Novo Nordisk normalised, which converts what was a customer-concentration worry into evidence of breadth (hospitals, laboratories, Poland, Norway, Sweden). Process, Energy & Water grew about 6% organically with orders 7% above sales and a record order book, on electrification, hydrogen handling, water and data-centre cooling, where lead times of six to twelve months mean the revenue sits in H2 2026 and 2027. Industrial & Engineering, the short-cycle two-thirds of the group, grew 5% on replacement and maintenance ordering rather than greenfield capex, with customers still running short order books and no safety stock. Infrastructure & Construction (15%) remained negative (-3% sales) with orders bottoming at -1%; the sector digest says European building recovery is deferred into 2027 under rate pressure. Technology & Systems Solutions (14%) grew only 2% with project delays and UK weakness.

Visibility is short by construction: these are mostly transactional, point-of-delivery businesses with backlogs of a few months, and the company gives no numerical guidance. The leading indicator the cohort uses (the weighted Swedish and eurozone manufacturing PMI, which crossed 53 in June 2026 and historically leads organic acceleration by two to five quarters) argues for continued improvement into 2027. Against that, the macro regime has deteriorated since the Q2 print (Fitness for Fighting digest, 16 October 2026: a discount-rate shock, Riksbank hike likely in November, Sweden among the most rate-sensitive economies), which threatens the construction leg first and the short-cycle industrial leg second. Two-thirds of sales remain cyclical. The structural strands (Life Science, electrification, water) are perhaps 35 to 40% of sales and are the part the Consumer can carry at mid-to-high single-digit organic growth with some confidence; the cyclical 60% should be modelled as recovering from a trough in 2026 and 2027 and then reverting toward the group's 3 to 4% long-run organic norm.

C-02

Competitive intensity: direction and extent of change

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement. Competitive intensity in the end markets is unchanged and benign; it is rising in the market that matters most to this company's growth algorithm, the market for acquiring small private technical companies. The net direction is mildly negative, and it shows up in price paid rather than in lost deals.

The reasoning. In the end markets, the evidence is that no competitor is pushing price: gross margin rose through the 2024 to 2025 volume decline (34.6% in 2023 to 35.4% in 2025 and 35.6% in Q2 2026), peers behave the same way, and the top five serial acquirers together hold under 5% of a market of local family firms. In the M&A market, the number of public and private serial acquirers has increased materially over 12 to 18 months (new Nordic platforms InstallatørGruppen, Tången, Auroora, Röko, Idun; EQT activating OEM International; Addtech lifting its pace to 15 to 20 deals; Lagercrantz to 8 to 12; Lifco to 17), and an expert says Nordic EBITDA multiples have moved from 4 to 6x toward 8 to 10x at the larger end. Two facts cut the other way: deals below EUR 20 to 50m remain largely outside private-equity interest, and Indutrade's own realised multiples are reported as 6 to 7x (CEO, April 2026) and a median of about 7x on the nine H1 2026 deals (DNB Carnegie). But DNB Carnegie's cross-cohort table records Indutrade's median acquisition EV/EBITA for 2023 to 2025 as 8.6x against 6.6 to 6.8x for Addtech, Lagercrantz and Lifco, and Redeye's historical baseline for Indutrade is 7 to 9x. The Consumer should assume Indutrade pays at the top of the cohort range (7.5 to 8.5x including earn-outs), which lowers the return on each krona deployed relative to peers and is the quantitative expression of rising intensity. A second, slower competitive force is the manufacturer going direct through digital channels, which the company now names explicitly in its 2026 MTN prospectus as a high competitive risk; it acts on the 58 to 60% technical-trading base, and it is not visible in the numbers yet.

C-03

Disruption forces: strengthening or disproved, and direction

C52

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement. Two disruption forces are at work in opposite directions and neither has strengthened decisively in six months. Digital disintermediation of technical trading is acknowledged, slow and so far disproved by the margin data; the electrification, data-centre and sovereign-infrastructure build-out is a positive disruption that the company is buying into rather than leading. Net effect: roughly neutral now, with the positive strand more visible in the next six months than the negative one.

The reasoning. The negative force is the structural one: digital product transparency lets component manufacturers sell direct, and AI procurement agents could in time let OEM purchasing teams bypass local distributors for standard components. The company's own risk disclosure and the Business Foundations research both treat it as real but subdued, because the subsidiaries sell application engineering, local stock and multi-brand bundling rather than catalogue price, and because once a valve or sensor is specified into a customer's design the distributor is locked in. The evidence that it is not biting is the gross margin rising to a record while volumes fell, and expert testimony that direct-to-customer sales in complex B2B components repeatedly fail on logistics. The force is strongest in standard electronics and automation products, which sit in Technology & Systems Solutions, the one business area with weak margins; the Consumer should carry a slow gross-margin erosion risk there rather than group-wide. The positive force is explicit in the numbers: Process, Energy & Water grew 12% in total on cooling, grids, hydrogen and water; the July 2026 acquisition of Controlin (SEK 130m sales; micro-grids, data centres, solar) adds exposure; Lagercrantz and Addtech are the purer plays. The Key Question report rates this strand medium confidence because the multi-year validation is thin and the AI build-out's financing cracked in October 2026 (the FfF digest), which is a reason to treat the data-centre element as optionality rather than base case.

C-04

Political and regulatory change, and its bearing

C52

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement. Regulatory change is broad, incremental and roughly cost-neutral for the group; no single item moves the four-year model by more than a few tenths of a margin point, and the one that could (tariffs) is indirect. Bearing: slight net cost, slight net barrier-to-entry benefit in Life Science and electrification.

The reasoning. United States tariffs touch under 6% of sales directly; their effect has been indirect hesitation in project-oriented Technology & Systems Solutions subsidiaries and China's buy-local policy, both already in the 2025 numbers. European reporting rules (CSRD, ESRS, export-control and sanctions complexity across 220 companies) raise compliance cost and create a diffuse control risk, which is the regulatory analogue of the UK concealment problem; the delayed CSRD timetable gives smaller subsidiaries a cushion. On the helpful side, tightening Medical Device Regulation raises qualification barriers in the 23% Life Science business, grid and energy-efficiency mandates pull demand through Process, Energy & Water, and proposed "Buy European" procurement revisions favour localised suppliers. Pillar Two is negligible (about SEK 4m). The planned Euromed acquisition in Poland needs competition-authority approval, a reminder that deal timing is partly outside management's control. None of this changes the trajectory; it changes the overhead needed to run 220 companies and raises the bar for the smallest competitors slightly.

C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: headwind or tailwind

C58

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement. A modest tailwind now, turning toward neutral. Supply chains normalised during 2025, input costs are flat to modestly up, the strong krona lowers landed costs for import-and-resell subsidiaries, and supplier concentration is negligible. The risk is that pass-through is becoming partial as customer bargaining power returns.

The reasoning. The last 18 months removed the post-pandemic bottlenecks: lead times are back to pre-pandemic norms, inventories at comparable units fell 5% in 2025 and were flat in Q2 2026, and the invoicing delays that once held back up to SEK 200m of sales have gone. The gross margin at a record 35.6% in Q2 2026 while freight, raw-material, semiconductor and component increases were passed through is the hard evidence of a tailwind. The krona's appreciation, which hurts translation, helps the trading subsidiaries that buy in euros and dollars and sell in kronor; management said this contributed to the record Q1 and Q2 gross margins. Supplier concentration: thousands of mostly European suppliers, no single one material at group level, with a small number of major principals serving several subsidiaries (the one place a concentration risk hides). The warning signs for the next six months are management's own flag that higher oil prices pressure plastics and other materials, a Q3 2026 automation-channel expert saying suppliers are recovering only 2 to 3% of 3 to 4% cost inflation because availability has restored customer leverage, and energy-sector freight surcharges. For the model: hold gross margin flat at about 35.5% on the company basis, with the downside risk concentrated in standardised electronics and automation lines.

C-06

Where the company's own revenue and demand cycle is, and whether it is turning

C65

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement. The company is one quarter past its trough and turning up. The trough ran from Q2 2025 to Q1 2026 (four quarters of organic sales between -4% and 0%); Q2 2026 was the first positive quarter, orders led sales by 3 percentage points, and the comparison base stays easy through Q4 2026 (Q3 2025 -1%, Q4 2025 0%). The turn is early-cycle and should be modelled as continuing for at least three more quarters before the base normalises.

The reasoning. Three features make this a turn rather than a bounce. First, orders have exceeded sales for three consecutive quarters (book-to-bill 100%, 107%, 105%), and the backlog is 8% higher than a year earlier; in a business that recognises most revenue at delivery, that is as much forward visibility as exists. Second, the turn is broad across four of five areas and across geographies (Nordic PMIs stronger than the eurozone, with the UK, Germany and Finland leading in Industrial & Engineering). Third, it coincides with the customer destocking cycle ending (biopharma single-use channels and industrial verticals both reported as complete), which removes the mechanism that made 2024 to 2025 worse than underlying demand. The counter-evidence is also real: the Q2 comparison was the softest quarter of 2025 (-4% organic, margin down 110 basis points), Infrastructure & Construction has not turned, and the regime shift in October 2026 (yields, Swedish rates) could delay the construction and short-cycle legs. The implication for the model is a 2026 second half with organic growth of about 4 to 6%, a 2027 of 3 to 5% as the base normalises and construction recovers, and a long-run organic norm of 3 to 4%; the cone is wider on the downside in 2027 than in H2 2026 because the backlog covers the next two quarters better than the next six.

C-07

Macro force: on the company, the sector, the industry, and where it bites hardest

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement. The macro force is a rates-led late-cycle squeeze with re-accelerating energy-led inflation, and it bites hardest at the sector level, through the multiple that premium Nordic compounders carry, rather than at the company's earnings. At the company level it acts through three channels, in descending order of size: currency translation, two-thirds cyclical exposure, and interest cost. At the industry level it is a mixed picture (grid and data-centre spending firm, European short-cycle production weak but improving, construction deferred).

The reasoning. The sector digest's rule is that "distributors move with factory orders one to two quarters behind; compounders move on their multiple, and de-rate together when yields rise." The US ten-year yield at about 5.1% (highest since 2007), the ECB and Riksbank both leaning toward hikes, and Sweden's mortgage-reset sensitivity make the discount rate the live macro variable; the Swedish compounder sector's 2026 P/E multiples contracted 6 to 7% over the last 12 months even as earnings momentum improved, and the FfF digest moved Sweden from Overweight to Neutral this month. For the company's earnings the largest channel is currency: about 80% of sales and SEK 17,564m of net investments are outside Sweden, translation is unhedged by policy, a 1% krona move changes net sales by SEK 243m and net profit by SEK 18m (about 0.7% of net profit), FX took 5% off Q1 2026 reported sales and 3% off EBITA, and consensus (EUR/SEK toward 10.40 to 10.50) implies a 2 to 3% annual drag if it materialises. Q2 2026 was FX-neutral, which flattered the reported growth rate. The second channel is volume: a 2 percentage point organic swing moves EPS about 3%. The third is interest: a 1 point rate rise adds about SEK 61m of expense (about 1.5% of pre-tax profit), which is minor at 1.2x financial leverage. The investment-case consequence is that the earnings recovery can proceed while the share price is held by the sector's multiple; the Consumer's model should carry a currency line explicitly and separate constant-currency delivery from reported figures.

C-08

A key industry or sector driver accelerating the company's demand

C60

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement. Partly. The two sector-level drivers that are accelerating demand are healthcare demand (medtech distribution and single-use pharma components) and the electrification, grid and data-centre build-out; they reach the company through Life Science and Process, Energy & Water, together about 46% of sales, and they are the reason organic growth turned before the industrial cycle did. The cohort's purest beneficiaries (Addtech's Energy and Electrification, Lagercrantz's Electrify, Diploma's Windy City Wire) carry a larger share of these drivers than Indutrade does.

The reasoning. Life Science grew 13% organically in Q2 2026 with orders +16% to +23% and an 18.0% EBITA margin, from a broadening base (hospitals, laboratories, Polish healthcare investment, Norway, Sweden) after the Novo Nordisk expansion deliveries normalised in late 2025. Consensus has the segment at SEK 8,630m in 2026, 9,520m in 2027 and 10,091m in 2028 (about +15%, +10%, +6%), with acquisitions (Axotan, PHACOTEC, Albiox, SLT, ATM, Sinmed, Euromed) adding to it. The energy strand is visible in orders rather than sales yet (Process, Energy & Water orders 7% above sales, record order book, six-to-twelve-month lead times), which makes it a 2027 revenue driver. The Key Question research rates the healthcare and electrification strands high plausibility but notes that the data-centre leg has limited multi-year validation and faces permitting and financing constraints. The reason this is "partly" rather than "yes": the drivers accelerate perhaps 40% of the group, and management explicitly refuses to raise the margin target because it wants segment leaders to keep buying lower-margin, high-return trading businesses, so the mix will not tilt toward these drivers as fast as at Lagercrantz.

C-09

Other material external dynamics

❌ D48

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement. Three other dynamics matter, two negative and one two-sided. Negative: the cohort's exposure to a short-seller campaign (The Analyst's January 2026 note on Lifco took Lifco down 16% and opened a 21% discount; the note's contents could not be obtained by local brokers), which shows that an unreadable attack on a decentralised acquirer's accounting can move the whole cohort, and the private-equity activation of OEM International and Beijer Ref, which raises the cohort's deal pace and the competition for targets. Two-sided: the krona. The consensus expects krona strength (a drag); the October 2026 risk-off regime, if it brings safe-haven dollar flows, would weaken the krona and flip translation into a tailwind, as it did in 2023 (+5% sales, +4% EBITA).

The reasoning. The short-seller dynamic is relevant because Indutrade's accounting is clean on every forensic test the Risks report ran (point-in-time revenue recognition, unqualified audits, no restatements, short interest under 0.2% of float) but it shares with Lifco the features an attacker targets: acquisition intangibles (about 45% of assets), earn-out and Level 3 liabilities, no internal audit function, and a thin centre. The dynamic to watch is therefore not Indutrade's own disclosure but the cohort's. The private-equity dynamic matters because the Key Question report finds sponsors accelerate programmatic consolidation and re-ratings, and the sector digest's preference is for "newly lit engines" (OEM International, Bufab) over "mature engines that need the re-rating," a category in which it places Indutrade; that is an allocation headwind for the stock even when the earnings are fine. The krona is the largest single external swing factor for reported numbers and the one the Consumer should scenario explicitly.

C-10

Thematic drivers in turn, and the overall external change picture

C55

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement. Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn: (1) the short-cycle industrial turn, a tailwind at the value-chain and industry level, six to twelve months old, confirmed in one quarter; (2) healthcare and medtech demand, a structural tailwind at the company level, strong; (3) electrification, grids and data centres, a structural tailwind at industry level, reaching the company through about a quarter of sales, with the data-centre leg's financing now in question; (4) construction and building, a headwind at the value-chain level, bottoming but deferred into 2027; (5) the discount-rate regime, a headwind at the sector level, the largest force on the share price; (6) currency, a headwind on the consensus path, a potential tailwind on the risk-off path; (7) M&A competition, a mild headwind acting on the price paid; (8) digital disintermediation, a slow headwind acting on the trading half of the group; (9) supply normalisation and pricing, a mild tailwind. Netting these: at the value-chain level the tailwinds outweigh the headwinds moderately (demand turning, pricing holding, supply normal); at the industry level roughly balanced (grid and healthcare up, construction and German manufacturing down); at the sector level the headwind dominates because the sector is priced on its multiple and the multiple is priced on yields. The overall external change picture is moderately positive for earnings and moderately negative for the rating, which is the configuration in which earnings growth must outrun a de-rating. The last 12 to 18 months were worse on demand and better on rates; the next six months are the mirror image.

Internal change forces

C6013 questions
C-1162C-1266C-1345C-1456C-1550C-1660C-1760C-1868C-1955C-2055C-8642C-8750C-2160

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The internal change forces are real, consistent with one another, and aimed at the right constraint (deal capacity, which is what limits a compounder of this size), and they have begun to show in the numbers in the one place they should show first (deal count and acquired EBITA contribution). They are not transformational: the targets are unchanged, the margin ambition is deliberately capped, the organic engine is unaddressed, and governance scaffolding was not added after the UK concealment. The Consumer should build the operating model around a programmatic M&A engine delivering 6 to 7% of sales a year at accretive margins, a cost base that now delivers operating leverage when organic growth is positive, and a leadership team in transition in finance until January 2027. The positive internal change is strong enough to sustain the historical algorithm (10 to 12% EBITA growth when organic growth is 3 to 4%) and not strong enough to lift the company into the margin and return tier of Lifco or Lagercrantz.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-11

Changes in the chief executive, key leaders and key actors, and what they signal

C62

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement. The chief executive, the board and the anchor shareholder are unchanged; the layer beneath has been refreshed more than at any time in Bo Annvik's nine-year tenure. The signal is a deliberate re-tooling of the deal engine and the weakest operating areas, not a loss of control, and the net effect is positive for the three-year case at the cost of handover risk in the next six months.

The reasoning. Six changes in 15 months: Gustav Ruda promoted to head of acquisitions (March 2025, succeeding Jonas Halvord); Peter Laveson, founder of a mini serial acquirer and former NOTE chief executive, to head Technology & Systems Solutions (September 2025, after Patrik Stolpe's departure); Per Lidström (ex Cibes Lift, Seco Tools, Sandvik) to Process, Energy & Water (April 2026, after Per-Olow Jansson's retirement at 30 years); Håkan Svensson (ex Dahrén Group, Södra, and formerly managing director of Bengtssons Maskin, Indutrade's first acquisition) to Infrastructure & Construction (August 2026, with Juha Kujala stepping back to a segment-leader role); and the chief financial officer sequence, Patrik Johnson out after eight years (to Husqvarna), Jan Öhman back as interim from 20 July 2026 (he was chief financial officer 2014 to 2018, then at Industrivärden), and Anna Vilogorac, chief financial officer of Ratos with prior Sandvik and Nuent finance and M&A roles, in post by 14 January 2027. Beneath them, 35 business-segment leaders (average 18 years at Indutrade) now carry both coaching and deal sourcing. The pattern is consistent: external operators with turnaround or acquirer backgrounds into the two weakest areas (Technology & Systems Solutions, Infrastructure & Construction) and a finance chief with M&A credentials into the seat that will scale deal volume. The leadership research is right that insider ownership is weak (the chief executive holds 83,138 shares plus synthetic options; no open-market purchases in 2025) and that the board declined to create an internal audit function after the UK concealment, so the changes strengthen capability more than alignment. No activist, no change at Lundbergs (26.6%), all eight directors re-elected in 2026. For the model: assume continuity of strategy and financial policy; allow for a quarter or two of disclosure noise across the three-person finance handover (Q3 2026 presented by the interim).

C-12

Change in strategy or strategic priorities, and how well articulated and resourced

C66

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement. The strategy has not changed direction; its operating architecture and its priorities have. The November 2025 Capital Markets Day moved the company from "implementation" to "delivery" of a five-area, 35-segment structure whose explicit purpose is to double the group again without adding central bureaucracy, with internal lead generation replacing broker-led deal flow and absolute profit growth replacing margin as the executive priority. It is well articulated, lightly but adequately resourced, and already producing results in the one dimension (deal volume) where results should come first.

The reasoning. The articulation is specific: each segment leader is responsible for coaching 6 to 8 companies (against capacity of 10 to 15) and for mapping and courting targets, with a network the company says covers up to 1,500 candidates and a stated aim of at least one acquisition per segment per year; eight regional holding companies (Stockholm, Manchester, Vantaa, Copenhagen, Uithoorn, Rheinfelden, Karlsruhe, Milan) provide legal and tax support; a six-category portfolio model (cash generators, growth, stable, growth candidates, loss-makers, problem companies) governs capital and management attention; AI screening tools support white-spot analysis; the Italian scouting network has evaluated more than 50 target NDAs. Resourcing is lean by design (24 people at the parent), which is both the model's strength and the control risk the UK episode exposed. Evidence of delivery: nine acquisitions in H1 2026 adding SEK 1.5bn of annual sales, against 13 deals and SEK 1.3bn in all of 2025; Q2 2026 M&A added 6% to sales and 8% to EBITA. The priority shift to absolute profit growth is coherent with the refusal to raise the 14% margin target (C-13) and with variable pay tied to subsidiary and group profit growth. What is not articulated is any organic growth mechanism beyond coaching and knowledge-sharing; the organic leg remains the strategy's missing building block, and the expert critique that the segment layer is creeping centralisation ("organisational silos", "least interesting compounder") is the risk to the culture that produces the deal flow.

C-13

Guidance raised, or mid-term targets or Capital Markets Day ambitions set or raised

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement. No. The financial targets were reconfirmed, not raised, at the Capital Markets Day (at least 10% average annual sales growth, at least 14% EBITA margin over a cycle, at least 20% return on capital employed, net debt to equity normally below 100%, a 30 to 50% payout), and management explicitly declined to raise the margin target when pressed. The only new ambition is qualitative: to double sales and profit again over five to eight years, which is arithmetically the existing 10% growth target. There is no short-term guidance to raise.

The reasoning. The decision not to raise the 14% margin floor was explained by the chief executive as protecting deal velocity: a higher group margin target would disqualify high-return, lower-margin technical-trading targets. That is a coherent capital-allocation argument, but for the investment case it means two things. First, there is no target-based catalyst; the company is at or above its margin target (14.6% average 2021 to 2025, 14.7% in Q2 2026) and below its growth and return targets (sales -1% in 2025, ROCE 18%), so the targets function as a floor on margin and an aspiration on growth. Second, it signals that management prefers absolute EBITA growth with margin held near 14 to 15% over a Lagercrantz-style margin journey to 20%; the Consumer should not model mix-driven margin expansion beyond about 15 to 15.5% unless the proprietary share rises faster than management intends. Peers differ: Lagercrantz raised its EBITA margin target to 20% and its return-on-working-capital target to 60%; Addtech reiterated 15% earnings growth; Diploma raised full-year guidance three times in 2026. Indutrade's posture is the most conservative in the set.

C-14

Standards improving in execution, operations, governance or cost discipline

C56

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Improving from a low point, with the clearest evidence in cost discipline and the weakest in governance scaffolding. Execution improved in the last six months (Q2 2026 showed sales +11% against SG&A +8%, Infrastructure & Construction margin up 170 basis points on restructuring and divestment, no recurrence of the UK write-downs); governance did not, because the board chose not to add an internal audit function after a concealment that ran undetected for multiple periods.

The reasoning. The 2024 to 2025 period was a standards failure on two fronts: SG&A grew ahead of demand that never arrived (Q1 2025 EBITA 11% below consensus, Q1 2026 adjusted EBITA 5 to 6% below), and two UK Technology & Systems Solutions subsidiaries sharing senior managers hid project cost overruns until SEK 125m was written off in Q4 2025. The responses are concrete: about 200 employees removed from demand-challenged entities over 12 months, the UK managers terminated and replaced, structured monthly scorecards through the new subsidiary boards, portfolio pruning of SEK 200m of dilutive revenue, and the chief executive's public language ("completely unacceptable", "not satisfied with the first quarter"). The evidence that discipline is now holding is the Q2 operating leverage, the gross margin at a record, and S&P moving the BBB- outlook to positive. The evidence that it is not yet institutional is the Capital Markets Day admission that the former span of control (20 to 25 companies per business-area head) had become unmanageable, the continued weakness of the UK project companies, the chief executive's stated reluctance to cut costs indiscriminately in lean trading companies, and the absence of an internal audit function across 220 companies (the whole-board audit committee relies on segment controllers and selective KPMG validation). The standards trend is positive; the structural control gap is unchanged.

C-15

Reducing the perimeter or composition: divestments, simplification, exits

C50

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement. A small, completed pruning rather than a reduction programme. Four non-core or loss-making units were sold in 2024 to 2025 (Scanmaskin, Plooijer Zaandam SEK 93m, Novisol SEK 50m, Modernum SEK 17m; roughly SEK 200m of revenue), the Infrastructure & Construction margin benefited visibly, and the chief executive said in January 2026 that no further divestment agenda is planned. Divestment proceeds in H1 2026 were zero. Composition is being changed by addition, not subtraction: the portfolio is tilting toward Life Science and electrification through acquisitions.

The reasoning. The relevance to the model is twofold. The completed pruning explains part of the Infrastructure & Construction margin step (12.2% in Q2 2026 from 10.5%) and should be treated as a one-time structural gain, not a trend. And the "own forever" posture, shared with Lifco, means that problem subsidiaries (one of the six portfolio categories) are turned around rather than exited, which keeps a tail of low-return capital in the group and is one reason return on capital employed sits at 18% against the 20% target. The simplification that did happen is organisational (eight regional areas to five sector areas), which makes segment disclosure more useful to the Consumer but does not narrow the business.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly

C60

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement. Steady, adjacency-based entry through acquisition, all of it sensible on the core-versus-adjacency test (shared customers, channels or skills) and none of it large enough to change the group's risk profile. The most strategically telling moves are Spain (ATM, late 2025), the Italian scouting network, Poland in Life Science (Sinmed, and Euromed announced 19 October 2026, conditional on competition approval), Dutch electrification (Controlin), German composites for medical instruments (Createc, SEK 250m) and marine flow aftermarket (Valveco, SEK 345m).

The reasoning. Each entry is a known model (technically specialised B2B niche, owner-managed, exclusive supplier relationships or proprietary product) in a new geography or sub-vertical, bought at the company's standard 5 to 8x, with the local management retained. The geographic broadening is also a response to Nordic auction congestion: the Nordics are the most competed market for targets, Germany is hard to source (owners reluctant to sell to foreign acquirers), Southern Europe and Poland are less contested. The two things to watch are the larger average deal size (SEK 130 to 345m against a historical SEK 50 to 100m), which management says is not a change of strategy but which increases integration exposure per deal, and the chief executive's own earlier hesitation about the UK, where the control failure occurred. Organic entry into new product markets is not a feature of this model; it happens inside subsidiaries and is not disclosed.

C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent

C60

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement. Real but modest, and demonstrated rather than claimed. The company does not split price from volume, so the evidence is indirect: the company-basis gross margin rose from 34.6% in 2023 to 35.4% in 2025 and 35.6% in Q2 2026 while organic volumes fell, through a period of freight, metals, semiconductor and wage inflation, and management attributes it to value-based pricing and pass-through. That is a real-terms rise at the margin line. It is not a pricing engine of the Lifco kind (3 to 7% price rises in the first year of ownership), and the latest external evidence is that customer bargaining power is returning as availability normalises.

The reasoning. Three facts support "real": the gross margin rose in every year from 2023 to 2026 on the company's definition and in most years on FactSet's (32.9% to 33.4%), which cannot happen under pure cost-plus pass-through when volumes fall; the operating culture ("the core DNA of an Indutrade managing director is to protect the gross margin") is stated and enforced through local EBITA-based bonuses; and Q2 2026 SG&A rose 8% (wage inflation) while gross margin still rose 30 basis points. Two facts support "modest": the gain is 1 percentage point over three years, and the trading half of the group faces more price transparency than the proprietary half. The forward view is that price contributes perhaps 1 to 2% of organic growth and holds gross margin flat rather than expanding it; the Consumer should not model gross-margin expansion as a driver, and should treat any expansion that occurs as mix (proprietary and Life Science share) rather than price.

C-18

Stepping up mergers and acquisitions: extent, sense, discipline, integration

C68

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement. A clear step-up, strategically sensible, with discipline that is intact on the stated rules and open to question on the price actually paid, and integration that is light by design. Nine deals in H1 2026 adding SEK 1.5bn of annual sales, plus Controlin (SEK 130m) in July and Euromed in October, against 13 deals adding SEK 1.3bn in all of 2025 and 16 adding SEK 1.6bn in 2024; acquired contribution to EBITA rose to 7% in Q1 and 8% in Q2 2026 from 4 to 5% in 2024 and 2025 against the ten-year norm of 7 to 8%. Redeye models 18 deals for 2026. This is the most important internal change force for the four-year model.

The reasoning. The step-up is structural, not cyclical: it follows the delegation of sourcing to 35 segment leaders and coincides with lower European rates unfreezing seller conversations. Acquired businesses are higher quality than the group on the quarterly bridge (acquired EBITA margins 19.5% in Q1 and 17.6% in Q2 2026 against group 13.3% and 14.7%), which makes the step-up margin accretive at the group level. Discipline on the stated rules holds: initial consideration at 5 to 8x EV/EBITA (about 1.2x sales), earn-outs averaging 0.4x sales over one to three years (SEK 272m initially recognised, maximum SEK 436m, on the H1 deals), a 20% return on capital employed within three to five years, EPS accretion required. The discipline question is the price including earn-outs: DNB Carnegie's cohort table puts Indutrade's 2023 to 2025 median at 8.6x against 6.6 to 6.8x for Addtech, Lagercrantz and Lifco, and the Business Foundations research warns that any drift above the historic 6 to 7x range mechanically dilutes through-cycle return on invested capital. The sense of the deals is good: Life Science, electrification, marine aftermarket, composites for medical instruments, all adjacencies with regulatory or specification barriers. Integration is intentionally minimal (local management retained, no synergies), which protects the sellers' culture and removes integration cost but means a weak acquisition is not fixed by integration; the larger average size (SEK 130 to 345m) raises the stakes per deal. For the model: SEK 2.0 to 2.5bn of acquired annual sales a year (6 to 7% of sales), at 16 to 18% EBITA margins, for SEK 1.8 to 2.5bn of cash consideration a year including earn-out settlements.

C-19

Using the balance sheet more, or stepping up shareholder returns

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used more for acquisitions and not at all for returns. Net debt to EBITDA (FactSet, including leases and earn-outs) rose from 1.3x at March 2025 to 1.65x at June 2026 on acquisition outflows of SEK 1.6bn in H1 2026, still inside the policy (net debt to equity 45% against a 100% ceiling) and with SEK 6.3bn undrawn. The dividend is progressive (SEK 3.10 for 2025, +3%; consensus SEK 3.30 for 2026 and 3.80 for 2027) at a 40 to 45% payout. There has never been a buyback, the chief executive has said dividends will rise in absolute terms each year, and nothing in the files suggests a change in either.

The reasoning. DNB Carnegie calculates headroom to fund up to SEK 3bn of acquired revenue a year without breaching the net-debt-to-equity ceiling; Redeye models leverage falling to 0.8x by 2034 even with 18 deals a year and a 40% payout. The capital structure is therefore not a constraint on the M&A step-up and the step-up is self-funded: FY2025 operating cash flow SEK 3,990m, capex SEK 502m, dividends SEK 1,091m, acquisitions SEK 1,765m. The share count has been 364.3m for four years; there is no dilution and no repurchase. For the model: debt rises modestly with the deal pace, interest cost falls with rates (FY2025 net interest SEK 470m against SEK 592m in FY2024), the dividend grows with EPS at a 40 to 45% payout, and buybacks are zero. The absence of buybacks is a mild negative for EPS compounding relative to Diploma-style capital returns, but a rational choice while acquisitions return 12 to 17% pre-tax on entry multiples of 6 to 8x.

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces present or plausible

C55

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement. Four, all incremental. The demonstrated return of operating leverage after the 2023 to 2025 SG&A build (organic EBITA +12% on organic sales +5% in Q2 2026), which converts the cost base built for growth into a margin lever if volumes hold. The S&P outlook move to positive on BBB-, which lowers the marginal cost of the M&A funding. The segment-leader pipeline and AI-assisted target screening, which are the plausible route to the 20 to 30 deals a year the structure was built for. And the cohort-wide bottoming of destocking, which removes the 2024 to 2025 mechanism that made reported volume worse than end demand.

The reasoning. None of these is a discrete catalyst and none is new since the Q2 print; they are the conditions under which the existing strategy compounds. The plausible further positives are a formal organic-growth initiative (none yet), a raised margin target if the proprietary share rises (management resists), and a transition of the UK project companies from drag to contribution (management says it will take time). The Consumer should treat operating leverage as the one positive force with near-term, quantifiable effect: about 2 to 2.5 points of organic EBITA growth per point of organic sales growth above zero.

C-86

Negative corporate change developments present or plausible

❌ D42

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement. Five, of which two are live and three are plausible. Live: the three-person chief-financial-officer handover within six months during a period of accelerating deal volume and earn-out accounting, and the unresolved weakness of the UK project-oriented subsidiaries in Technology & Systems Solutions, whose margin fell to 13.7% in Q2 2026 from 14.4%. Plausible: creeping centralisation through the segment layer eroding the subsidiary autonomy that attracts sellers (the expert-network critique); integration or overpayment risk as deal size rises and the count scales toward 18 to 30 a year with a 24-person centre and no internal audit; and working capital rising with growth (net working capital 27.9% of trailing revenue at June 2026, up from 24.2% in March 2025, as receivables grew with the Q2 invoicing surge) after the 2024 to 2025 inventory releases that flattered cash conversion are exhausted.

The reasoning. The first two are visible in the numbers and will be tested at the Q3 and Q4 2026 prints. The third is the one that would damage the model most if true, because the deal flow (80% proprietary at the best peers) depends on reputation as a permanent home; the counter-evidence is the H1 2026 deal count itself. The fourth is the Risks report's concern that rapid segment proliferation compromises target quality if central monitoring breaks; the 8.6x median multiple is the early-warning statistic to watch. The fifth matters for the free cash flow line: the Risks report's "false friend" hypothesis is precisely that 2024 to 2025 cash conversion above 130% was an inventory unwind masking volume decay, and management itself said in 2025 that further working-capital releases would be marginal. For the model, cash conversion should be assumed to fall toward 80 to 90% of net income in 2026 (consensus FCF/net income 0.8x) before recovering, not held at the 2024 to 2025 level.

C-87

Controlling actors indicating scope for change in strategy or approach

C50

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement. None. Lundbergföretagen (26.6% of capital and votes; chair Katarina Martinson, with three Lundberg-affiliated directors) is a permanent, patient owner with no history of forcing change at Indutrade, and no activist is present in the register or in the research. AMF (8.0%) and Fidelity (8.6%) are passive. One-share-one-vote means a change could in principle come from outside, but the 26.6% block and the absence of any short-seller or activist interest make that academic. The relevance to the Consumer is stability: strategy, financial policy and the dividend are unlikely to be reset by an owner, for better or worse.

C-21

Taken together: how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes

C60

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement. Moderately strong, reasonably broad, lightly but adequately resourced, and already partly delivered. The positive changes form a coherent chain: a structure built to scale deal flow (35 segments, internal sourcing, regional holding companies), a step-up in deals that has already restored the acquired EBITA contribution to its historical 7 to 8%, a bench refreshed with operators and an M&A-literate finance chief, cost discipline that converted the first quarter of volume recovery into 100 basis points of margin, and a balance sheet with SEK 6.3bn of unused capacity. The weaknesses in the chain are that none of the targets were raised, the organic engine has no new mechanism beyond coaching, insider ownership is low, the control scaffolding was not strengthened after a real failure, and the price paid for acquisitions is at the top of the cohort. Compared with the last 12 to 18 months (restructuring in progress, deal flow slow, costs ahead of demand, write-downs), the last six months are a clear improvement, and the next six are about proving that the improvement repeats (Q3 2026 tomorrow, Q4 2026 in January, the chief financial officer in post by mid-January). For the model, the internal changes support 6 to 7% acquired sales growth a year, a group EBITA margin of 14.5 to 15.5%, and no equity dilution; they do not support a margin journey toward peers.
