Industry and Value Chain

C6016 questions
C-2455C-2555C-2660C-2758C-2872C-2970C-3168C-8455C-3258C-3365C-3465C-3560C-3650C-3755C-3852C-3962

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry structure is the most reliably supportive part of this investment case and the part the Consumer can model with the most confidence. End-market demand is fragmented, specified and rationally priced; competitors put return on capital before volume and did not cut price through a seven-quarter downturn; the route to market is the company itself; suppliers are diversified; regulators are mildly helpful on demand and mildly costly on compliance; substitutes and orthogonal entrants act slowly and on the standardised minority of the trading half. Those facts justify a flat company-basis gross margin of about 35.5% and an EBITA margin floor of 14% in the operating model. The value chain's one fragile link is the acquisition market, which is this company's real supply chain: more buyers, higher multiples at the top of the range, and Indutrade already paying more than its best peers. The empirical precedent for the sector is that the compounding arithmetic produces outperformance when both engines run, and that an organic stall or a yield-driven de-rating produces two years of sideways returns; Indutrade has just come out of the first and is entering the second. The Consumer should carry a stable value-chain margin structure and an explicit sensitivity on the price and pace of acquisitions.

C-24

Rank by market share or size, and distance from number one or two

C55

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement. Two answers are needed, because the company competes in two markets. As a platform that buys and owns technical B2B niche companies, Indutrade is the largest Nordic serial compounder by revenue (SEK 32.2bn in 2025, against Beijer Ref 32.0bn, Lifco 28.3bn, Addtech 22.7bn, Lagercrantz 10.6bn) and is therefore number one by size; but size confers little here, and on the metrics that drive the multiple (margin, organic growth, return on capital) it ranks fourth of the four Nordic "proven compounders". In its end markets, the group has no measurable share: the European technical-trading market is made of thousands of local product markets, the top five serial acquirers together hold under 5%, and each subsidiary's claim is to be number one to three in a micro-niche that is not disclosed. The ten largest subsidiaries are only 19% of group sales (30% ten years ago).

The reasoning. For the model, the operative rank is the cohort one. Indutrade's EBITA margin (13.8% in 2025, 14.7% in Q2 2026) sits below Lifco (22 to 23%), Lagercrantz (18.1%), Addtech (16.0 to 16.6%) and Diploma (24.5%); its decade organic growth (about 4%) sits below Addtech's 6.5% and Lifco's 6%; its ROIC (median 14% over 2014 to 2024) sits below Addtech's 18.7% and Lifco's 21%. The distance to the number one or two position in the cohort is therefore not a share gap but a quality gap of 2 to 9 margin points and 2 to 3 points of organic growth, which is the whole of the 15 to 20% valuation discount. Size is a mild disadvantage: at SEK 32bn, each SEK 150m acquisition is 0.5% of sales, so the company needs more deals than smaller peers to grow at the same rate.

C-25

Market concentration: top five above 30 percent, steepness of the distribution

C55

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement. Extremely low concentration in the end markets and in the acquisition market; the top five do not hold 30% of anything, and there is no steep share distribution at the group level. Inside individual niches, concentration can be high (a subsidiary with a leading local position and high switching costs), which is where the pricing power comes from. This is a fragmented-market model where the consolidator's advantage is the runway of targets rather than oligopoly pricing.

The reasoning. The target pool is roughly 1.4 million small industrial SMEs and more than 210,000 mid-sized firms in Europe; the company's own mapping covers about 1,500 candidates; the top four distributors in Addtech's framing control under a third of the technical-trading market, and the Business Foundations research puts the top five acquirers under 5%. The consequence for the Consumer is that market structure neither caps growth (the runway is decades long) nor supports price discipline at the industry level; price discipline comes from product specification and niche position, subsidiary by subsidiary. Fragmentation also cuts the other way for a buyer of this size: as the Wisdom Library entry on structural fragmentation warns, fragmented markets often stay fragmented because the economics are local, so the roll-up thesis has to be tested one niche at a time.

C-26

Stability of market shares, and share taken by new entrants over three to five years

C60

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement. End-market shares are stable to the point of being unmeasured; the company reports no share data and the research finds none, which is itself consistent with micro-niches where positions move slowly. New entrants have taken no visible share in the end markets. In the acquisition market, new entrants are numerous (new listed Nordic platforms, private-equity-backed platforms, EQT at OEM International) and have taken a share of the deal flow and pushed up multiples at the larger end, but have not stopped Indutrade from completing a record first half.

The reasoning. The structural reason end-market shares are stable is specification lock-in: once a component is designed into a customer's system, replacement requires re-qualification; combined with local stock, technical support and exclusive regional distribution rights, this makes entry a slow business. The three-to-five-year change that matters is on the supply side of deals, not of products. The Consumer should model no share loss in end markets and should model acquisition pricing at the top of the historical range as the cost of new entrants.

C-27

Consolidation toward the largest and the most advantaged, and whether the company is among them

C58

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement. Yes, the industry consolidates steadily toward serial acquirers, and the most advantaged acquirers are not the largest but those with proprietary deal flow, a reputation as a permanent home, the discipline to pay 6 to 8x, and the internal capacity to run many parallel small deals. Indutrade is among the consolidators by scale and by capacity (35 segment leaders, SEK 6.3bn of headroom, the largest deal count in the cohort in H1 2026), but it is not clearly among the most advantaged: it pays more than the best peers (8.6x median against 6.6 to 6.8x), its organic growth is lowest, and the cohort's investors prefer the "newly lit" engines.

The reasoning. The mechanism of consolidation is arithmetic (buy at 6 to 8x, trade at 17 to 26x) and the constraint is organisational, not financial; the Key Question report and the sector digest both find that two-thirds of a top compounder's earnings growth comes from unannounced deals that consensus does not model, so the advantage accrues to whoever can convert free cash flow into closed deals fastest at disciplined prices. Indutrade's segment structure is designed exactly for that, and H1 2026 is the first evidence that it works (nine deals, SEK 1.5bn). The gap to "most advantaged" is the price paid and the organic engine; the first erodes the return per deal, the second means the consolidation does not compound as fast. For the model, Indutrade participates in the consolidation at the cohort's average pace with below-average returns per deal.

C-28

Competitors' attitude to competition: margin or volume first, benign or loss-tolerant, prices when volume falls

C72

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. Margin and return on capital first, benign, not loss-tolerant, and prices do not fall when volume falls. This is one of the strongest facts in the file and the foundation of the margin stability the Consumer can model.

The reasoning. The direct evidence is the group's own gross margin through the 2024 to 2025 downturn (34.6% to 35.4% while organic sales fell), which could not have happened if competitors cut price for volume. The cohort evidence is the same: Addtech's gross margin rose 330 basis points from 2022 to 2025 and 100 basis points in Q1 2026/27 on flat organic sales; Lagercrantz's gross margin is over 39%; Bufab and Bossard expanded gross margins in 2026 on value-based pricing; a Morgan Stanley survey found 46% of distributors strengthened pricing in late 2026. The peers state their priorities explicitly (Addtech and Lifco target ROIC above 15%; Lagercrantz aims at 20% EBITA margins and a return on working capital above 60%; "ignore market share and maximise absolute profit" at Lifco) and target different niches, so overlap is limited; OEM International says it meets Indutrade only at the local product level. The exception the research names is the generalist and small local distributor, who could discount in a prolonged downturn; that risk sits in standard components, not in specified ones. The one place behaviour is not benign is bidding for acquisitions, where the Lifco playbook (3 to 7% price rises in the first year) shows what a more aggressive owner extracts, and where new acquirers compete on price paid rather than price charged.

C-29

Stability of that attitude over 18 months to three years; O'Leary instability; shark attacks

C70

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. Stable. There is no evidence of reactive, situation-dependent pricing behaviour from any named competitor over the last three years, and no sign that any would attack industry pricing if they sensed weakness; the structural reason is that no competitor is large enough in any niche to gain from a price war and all are measured on return on working capital. The only instability over the period is in the acquisition market, where private-equity and new-platform bidding has pushed multiples at the larger end from 4 to 6x toward 8 to 10x, and that has stabilised below EUR 20 to 50m deal sizes.

The reasoning. The 2024 to 2025 downturn was the test: volumes fell for seven quarters across the cohort, and gross margins rose at Indutrade, Addtech and Lagercrantz. That is as strong a demonstration of stable, non-predatory conduct as a sector can give. The latest Q3 2026 expert evidence that suppliers are recovering only 2 to 3% of 3 to 4% cost inflation in automation channels is a sign of returning customer leverage, not of competitor aggression. For the model: no price-war scenario is needed; the margin downside scenario is volume-driven operating deleverage and partial pass-through, not price collapse.

C-31

How supportive the industry structure is, and how fragile that support

C68

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. Supportive, and the support is robust in the end markets and moderately fragile in the acquisition market. The end-market structure (fragmented, specification-locked, locally served, rationally priced, with secular GDP-plus drivers) is the reason a 35% gross margin and a 14 to 15% EBITA margin have held across cycles since the 2005 listing. The acquisition-market structure (a vast pool of family sellers, few buyers for the smallest deals, stable sub-EUR-20m multiples) is the reason the compounding arithmetic works; its fragility is the rising count of acquirers and the drift of multiples at the top of the range.

The reasoning. The fragility test is what would have to change for the support to fail. In the end markets it would take digital disintermediation of specified components, which the evidence says is slow and partial, or a European industrial recession deep enough to make generalist distributors discount, which 2008 to 2009 (sales -7.5%) showed the group survives. In the acquisition market it would take multiples moving from 7 to 8x toward 10x for the deals Indutrade does, which would compress the return on new capital from 12 to 17% pre-tax toward 10%; the Risks and Earnings Review reports both name this as the pivotal structural threat, and DNB Carnegie's 8.6x median says Indutrade is already closer to the edge than its peers. The Consumer can model stable end-market economics and should run a sensitivity on the acquisition multiple.

C-84

Historical precedents and empirical evidence on how this sector outperforms and underperforms, and the company's fit

C55

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. The empirical pattern for Nordic serial acquirers is well documented in the files and Indutrade fits the first half of it and has recently fallen out of the second. Outperformance comes from the multiple-arbitrage engine (buy at 6 to 8x EBITA, trade at 17 to 26x), run at a deal pace that adds 7 to 12% to EBITA a year, financed internally with no dilution, on an asset-light base (capex 1 to 2% of sales) with high return on working capital, in businesses with specification-based pricing power; the best of them (Lifco, Addtech, Lagercrantz, Diploma) have compounded EBITA at 17 to 20% for a decade or two and doubled earnings every five years, and the share price has followed earnings plus a re-rating from about 11x to above 20x. Underperformance comes in three forms: a stall in organic growth that exposes the 2:1 dependence on acquired growth (Addtech's eleven flat quarters; Lifco's 1.2% in Q1 2026); a de-rating when yields rise, which hits the whole cohort regardless of delivery (2021 to 2022; the sector's 6 to 7% P/E contraction over the last year); and idiosyncratic shocks, a control failure in a decentralised model (Indutrade's UK write-down), an unreadable short-seller note (Lifco, -16%), a leveraged roll-up breaking (Storskogen), a large acquisition failing (Beijer Alma's Alcomex).

The reasoning on fit. Indutrade fits the outperformance template on mechanics (15% EPS CAGR from the 2005 listing to 2023; 12% sales CAGR; self-funded; 5 to 8x entry; capex 1.8% of sales; no equity issued in a decade) and has fallen out of it on three counts since 2024: organic growth at the bottom of the cohort, acquired contribution falling to 4 to 5% in 2024 to 2025, and a control failure. The result was a de-rating from about SEK 330 in early 2025 to SEK 170, a 10% five-year EPS CAGR, and a 15 to 20% discount to the proven peers. The precedent the sector digest draws for the next phase is that "the newly lit engines" (OEM International, Bufab) outperform the "mature engines that need the re-rating" (Indutrade, Addtech, Lagercrantz) in a rising-yield regime, because their earnings can outrun a de-rating. Indutrade's H1 2026 deal pace is an attempt to relight its own engine; the fit with the outperformance pattern over the next 18 months depends on whether acquired EBITA contribution stays at 7 to 8% and organic growth stays positive, which together would deliver the 10 to 12% EBITA growth that historically supported the multiple. The base rate for a diversified Nordic compounder failing outright is near zero; the base rate for one trading sideways for two years after an organic stall is high.

C-32

Customer demand: support for the thesis and fragility of that support

C58

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. Supportive since Q2 2026 and fragile for three reasons: it rests on one quarter against the softest comparison of the cycle; it is concentrated in Life Science and energy while construction is still contracting and project-oriented technology subsidiaries are subdued; and the macro regime turned against it in October 2026 before a second quarter could confirm it.

The reasoning. The supportive facts are the +8% organic orders, +5% organic sales, 105% book-to-bill, 8% higher backlog, four of five areas positive, destocking complete, and the PMI lead indicator. The fragile facts are that Q2 2025 was -4%, that Infrastructure & Construction (15%) remains negative with European building deferred to 2027, that Technology & Systems Solutions grew 2% with UK project weakness, that the energy backlog converts on six-to-twelve-month lead times (so an order slowdown would erode it rather than be visible immediately), and that no price-volume split is disclosed so the recovery cannot be proven to be physical volume. Peer evidence corroborates the turn (Lagercrantz +6%, Sdiptech +11%, Lifco +4.7% in Q2; OEM International +7%; Bufab +5.3%) while Addtech's 0% shows how uneven it is. Q3 2026 tomorrow is the first test; the working assumption should be that demand supports organic growth of 4 to 6% in H2 2026 and that the probability of a relapse to 0 to 2% in 2027 is material, perhaps one in three, under the current rates regime.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and fragility

C65

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement. Helping, structurally. Indutrade's subsidiaries are the route to market: they sell directly to OEMs, installers and end users with local stock, application engineering and multi-brand bundling, which is the source of the 35% gross margin and the reason customers return after trying direct purchase. The fragility is upstream, not downstream: the trading half of the group depends on exclusive regional distribution rights from international principals, and the named risk is a principal going direct or switching distributor.

The reasoning. The route-to-market advantage is confirmed by the research on specialty distribution generally (principals cannot economically replicate local technical support; direct B2B sales in complex components fail on logistics) and by the company's own margin record. The fragility is contained by diversification (thousands of suppliers, none material at group level), by the shift toward proprietary products (now about 40 to 42% of sales, up from about 40% a decade ago on the company's framing, which removes supplier flight risk where it applies), and by the technical content that makes the subsidiary the specifier rather than the reseller. The place where the route is weakest is standard electronics and automation components in Technology & Systems Solutions, where digital transparency and platform procurement are furthest advanced. The Consumer can model stable channel economics with a slow drift in the trading half's gross margin as the only structural leak.

C-34

Existing competitors: behaviour, effect on the company, fragility of position

C65

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement. Existing competitors are benign and rational, which helps the company at the gross-margin line, and the company's position against them is robust in end markets and moderately fragile in the one arena where they compete head-on, the purchase of private companies. The cohort peers (Lifco, Addtech, Lagercrantz) target different niches and rarely meet Indutrade in an auction (a former Lifco acquisition head recalled meeting it twice in eight years); the broader set (Beijer Ref, Bufab, OEM International, Sdiptech, Diploma, IMCD, Azelis) overlaps little in product. The behaviour that hurts is indirect: the peers' superior organic growth and margins set the valuation benchmark against which Indutrade is discounted.

The reasoning. Where Indutrade is weaker than existing competitors is not in winning customers but in extracting returns from the same type of asset: Lifco mandates 3 to 7% price rises post-acquisition and runs 29% EBITA margin targets; Lagercrantz has lifted its proprietary share to 80%; Addtech's return on working capital is 81%. Indutrade's refusal to centralise pricing or to raise its margin target is a deliberate choice to protect deal velocity, and it leaves the company structurally behind on the metrics the market rewards. The position is fragile to the extent that a competitor out-bids it for the best targets; H1 2026 says that has not yet happened.

C-35

New entrants: adjacent, standard and orthogonal, and the threat of each

C60

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement. Three types, none a material threat to end-market revenue, one a material threat to acquisition returns. Standard entrants (new listed Nordic compounders such as InstallatørGruppen, Tången, Auroora, Röko, Idun) copy the playbook, buy small targets at about 5x, and compete for the same sellers; their threat is to the price of deals, not to customers. Adjacent entrants (private-equity platforms, EQT at OEM International and Beijer Ref, the larger listed peers expanding into continental Europe) compete for deals above EUR 20 to 50m and raise multiples there; Indutrade's sweet spot (SEK 30 to 250m sales, about EUR 15m revenue) sits mostly below that line. Orthogonal entrants (B2B marketplaces, direct-to-manufacturer e-commerce, AI procurement agents, integrated distributors such as OEM International with digital ordering and shared logistics) threaten the standardised part of the trading half over a longer horizon; the evidence to date is that specified, engineered components resist them.

The reasoning. The threat ranking for the model is: adjacent (price of deals) first, because it acts on the return on new capital now; orthogonal second, because it acts on the trading gross margin slowly; standard third, because the targets are too numerous for new small platforms to crowd out a buyer with 1,500 mapped candidates. The structural entry barriers in the end markets (machinery and tooling, specialist training, long R&D accumulation, ERP connectivity, authorised-territory policies, regulatory qualification) remain intact.

C-36

Substitutes and alternatives: threat and direction

C50

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement. A moderate, slow-moving threat confined to the standardised end of the trading half, and not changing quickly. The substitutes are: buying direct from the manufacturer; buying through a digital platform or marketplace; and, for the proprietary half, product substitution as customer systems change (industrial IoT, additive manufacturing, new materials, 800VDC data-centre architectures displacing low-voltage equipment). None is new, and the company's own evidence (record gross margin, customers returning from direct purchase, specification lock-in) says the first two have not progressed in the measurable period.

The reasoning. The company disclosed the direct-sales risk in its annual report and, more prominently, in its June 2026 MTN prospectus; the Earnings Review treats it as the single most significant structural disruption risk to the trading base, with application engineering and local integration as the defences. The direction of change is twofold: AI procurement tools make comparison cheaper (a negative for standard components) and the company's own mix is moving toward proprietary products and regulated Life Science niches (a positive). The Consumer should carry no explicit substitution loss in the base case and a 20 to 50 basis point gross-margin erosion in the trading half in a bear case, which is the magnitude the margin history can accommodate without breaking the 14% EBITA floor.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: help or hurt, and fragility

C55

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement. Mild net help on demand, mild net hurt on cost, low fragility. Helpful: Medical Device Regulation and pharma qualification standards raise barriers in Life Science; grid, water, decarbonisation and energy-efficiency mandates pull demand through Process, Energy & Water; EU utility certification standards block uncertified imports; "Buy European" procurement proposals favour local suppliers; validated SBTi targets support institutional customer screening. Hurtful: CSRD and ESRS data burdens across 220 companies, eco-design and chemical (SVHC, REACH) compliance, export-control and sanctions complexity, and United States tariffs acting indirectly on project demand. Neutral: Pillar Two (about SEK 4m), competition approvals on deals (Euromed in Poland).

The reasoning. The fragility is not that a regulator turns hostile but that a decentralised group fails to comply uniformly, which is the same control problem as the UK write-down expressed in regulatory form; the probability is low and the cost of a single failure would be local. For the model: no regulatory line is needed beyond modest compliance cost growth in SG&A the regulatory tailwinds are embedded in the Life Science and energy growth assumptions.

C-38

On whom in the value chain the disruption forces act, how each acts, and the fragility they leave

C52

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement. Disruption acts on four actors, in this order of importance for Indutrade. On the acquisition market (the "supplier" of the company's growth): private-equity and new platforms raise the price of targets and the speed at which sellers are approached, which erodes return per deal; fragility moderate. On suppliers (principals): digitalisation lowers the cost of going direct or of switching distributors; fragility low to moderate, concentrated in standard components. On customers: AI-assisted procurement and B2B platforms lower search and comparison cost, which pressures the trading half's gross margin over years, and connected factories shift some value from components to software and data services; fragility low for specified products, moderate for catalogue-like ones. On competitors: integrated distributors with digital ordering and logistics (OEM International style) could gain a cost-to-serve advantage that a 220-company decentralised model cannot match; fragility low today, rising with scale. On regulators and political actors: no disruptive force beyond the compliance load.

The reasoning. The company's defences are structural (specification lock-in, local technical service, proprietary products at 40 to 42% and rising with acquisitions, regulated niches in Life Science) and the evidence is that they hold at the gross-margin line. The one place where the company's own structure is a disruption liability is the slow discovery of local deterioration (the UK case) and the fragmentation of IT and cyber controls across subsidiaries. For the model, disruption is a bear-case gross-margin drift in the trading half and a sensitivity on the acquisition multiple, not a base-case driver.

C-39

Suppliers: a support or a source of risk

C62

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement. A support, with one named and contained risk. Supplier relationships are long-standing, mostly European, exclusive by territory where it matters, and so fragmented that no single supplier is material at group level (Addtech's largest is 1% of purchasing; Indutrade discloses no concentration but states no predominant dependency). Suppliers passed cost increases through in 2024 to 2026 and the subsidiaries passed them on. The named risk is a principal setting up direct sales or appointing another distributor, which the annual report lists among its most material risks; the mitigant is the subsidiary's superior local market access and the gradual shift toward proprietary products, which removes the dependency altogether where it applies.

The reasoning. Two second-order points for the Consumer. First, a small number of major principals serve several Indutrade companies, so the group-level fragmentation can hide a cluster exposure; it is not quantified anywhere in the files. Second, the supplier relationship is an asset the company buys with every trading acquisition and audits in due diligence (durability of the supplier relationship is a named diligence item), so the risk is managed at entry rather than at group level. Supply availability is normal, freight and energy surcharges are the live cost item, and working-capital discipline on safety stock is a stated tension between the centre and subsidiary managing directors.

Company - Competitive advantage

C587 questions
C-4160C-4255C-4370C-4458C-4565C-4635C-4760

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The company's competitive advantage is genuine at the subsidiary level, moderate at the group level, and converts into financial outputs through a mechanical acquisition arithmetic rather than through any market-share or scale dynamic. The subsidiary moats (specification lock-in, local service, exclusive rights, proprietary products) are evidenced by a gross margin that rose through a two-year volume decline and by a customer base with no concentration; they justify a stable gross margin in the model. The group moat (permanent-home reputation producing proprietary deal flow at disciplined multiples) is evidenced by the H1 2026 deal count and qualified by the price paid and by the risk that the segment layer centralises the culture that produces the flow. The addressable market is effectively unbounded for a decade. What the company lacks is any evidence of leading customer utility or of share gain, any scale economy, and the pricing and margin ambition of its best peers; those are the reasons it is the fourth-ranked of the four proven compounders and why the Consumer should model parity in the end markets, 6 to 7% acquired growth, and margin improvement from mix rather than from advantage.

C-41

Sustainable relative advantages or moats, their strength, and whether widening or narrowing

C60

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement. Indutrade holds two tiers of moat and they must be assessed separately. At subsidiary level: customer-related (specification lock-in, local technical service, exclusive territorial distribution rights; strong, stable) and production-related for the proprietary 40 to 42% (customised products with safety and performance validation; moderate, slowly widening as acquisitions add proprietary businesses). At group level: a culture-and-knowledge moat (the reputation as a permanent, hands-off home for family sellers, which produces proprietary deal flow at 5 to 8x; moderate, under test as the segment layer centralises) and a scale-based platform moat (35 segment leaders, eight regional holding companies, BBB- funding, SEK 6.3bn of capacity; moderate, widening with the 2024 restructuring). There is no regulatory or political moat except the qualification barriers inside Life Science. Net: moderate moats, widening at the platform, stable at the subsidiary, and narrowing relative to the best peers on the metrics that convert moats into returns.

The reasoning. The subsidiary moats are the best evidenced: gross margin rising through a volume downturn, no customer above 3% of sales, customers that "prioritise component reliability over unit cost" and return after trying direct purchase, 2008 to 2009 sales down only 7.5%. The group moat is the one that matters for a serial acquirer and the one where the evidence is mixed: H1 2026's nine deals say sellers still choose Indutrade; the 8.6x median multiple (against 6.6 to 6.8x at peers) says it pays for the privilege; and the expert critique that the company is centralising and building silos is a direct attack on the moat's source. The Wisdom Library's "flywheel is not a moat" entry applies: the acquisition flywheel is a process advantage that needs refuelling every year, not a barrier that defends itself. Against Lifco (pricing playbook, 29% margin targets, 132% ROCE ex-goodwill) and Lagercrantz (80% proprietary, micro-niche number-one positions), Indutrade's moats are of the same kind and of lesser depth; relative to generalist distributors (Bufab, RS Group, Rexel) they are deeper.

C-42

Scale relative to competitors, and what it confers

C55

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement. Large relative to the cohort (SEK 32.2bn of sales, about 9,800 employees, 220-plus companies across 30 countries, against Lifco SEK 28.3bn and 275 companies, Addtech SEK 22.7bn and 150, Lagercrantz SEK 10.6bn and 85, Diploma GBP 1.5bn and 17 businesses) and negligible relative to the end markets it serves. Scale confers four things: funding (investment-grade, SEK 1.3bn five-year bond at STIBOR plus 113 basis points, SEK 6.3bn undrawn); deal capacity (the ability to run nine deals in a half-year and to absorb a SEK 125m write-down without strategic consequence); a reputational position with sellers; and diversification that caps any single failure at under 1% of revenue. It does not confer purchasing power, pricing coordination or cost synergies, because the model forbids them, and it imposes a cost: the group needs 15 to 30 deals a year to grow at the same rate a smaller peer achieves with 8 to 12.

The reasoning. On the Wisdom Library's "scale needs a bounded market" test, Indutrade fails every condition for a scale economy (no throughput unit, no indivisible fixed cost, no bounded market) and should not be modelled as having one. The scale that matters is financial and organisational, and the 2024 to 2025 restructuring was explicitly a response to the organisational limit of the previous span of control. The ten largest subsidiaries at 19% of sales (30% a decade ago) show the group is getting more granular as it grows, which raises resilience and lowers oversight.

C-43

Size of the realistic addressable market relative to the company, and the mid-term growth it allows

C70

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement. Very large relative to the company, on both engines. For acquisitions: roughly 1.4 million European industrial SMEs and more than 210,000 mid-sized firms, of which the company has mapped about 1,500 candidates in its segments; at SEK 2 to 2.5bn of acquired sales a year the company consumes well under 0.1% of the pool annually, and Europe outside the Nordics (Germany, Benelux, Italy, Spain, Poland, the UK) is barely consolidated. For organic growth: the end markets grow at European GDP (about 2% real) plus structural overlays (healthcare demographics, electrification, water, automation, outsourcing of technical procurement), which the research puts at GDP-plus, and the group's decade organic average was about 4%. The addressable market therefore allows the 10% sales growth target (6 to 7% acquired plus 3 to 4% organic) to be sustained for at least the four-year horizon and plausibly a decade; the constraint is organisational capacity and price discipline, not market size.

The reasoning. The two things the market size does not allow are faster organic growth than the end markets (the company is a price-taker on volume) and acceleration of acquired growth without a corresponding rise in deal count; DNB Carnegie's 7% acquired EBITA growth to 2031 and Redeye's 18 to 24 deals a year by 2033 both sit inside the runway. Life Science's consensus trajectory (SEK 7.5bn in 2025 to 10.1bn in 2028) is the best example of a sub-market growing faster than the group.

C-44

Surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings: leading, or at least not inferior

C58

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement. Demonstrably not inferior; not demonstrably leading. The disproof of inferiority is strong: customers accepted price rises through a volume downturn (gross margin 34.6% to 35.6%), no single customer exceeds 3% of sales yet the base is sticky enough that five-to-ten-year framework contracts exist in technical service, components are designed into customer architectures, and Life Science order growth broadened after its largest pharma customer normalised. The absence of proof of leadership is equally clear: the company discloses no retention, win-rate, net promoter or share data, organic growth over the decade (about 4%) is below Addtech's 6.5% and Lifco's 6%, and Diploma's 99% retention at Windy City Wire or Lagercrantz's number-one-or-two rule have no Indutrade analogue in the files.

The reasoning. On the Sophos or Bytes standard the question sets, the evidence clears the bar of "not inferior" at the gross-margin line and fails to establish "leading" at the volume line. The honest model assumption is parity: subsidiaries hold their customers and their price, and grow with their niches rather than taking share. Where utility is highest (specified components in regulated or safety-critical applications, Life Science, flow technology) the organic numbers are best; where it is lowest (standard electronics and automation components, project-oriented systems businesses) the organic numbers and margins are weakest, which is consistent with the thesis.

C-45

Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of surplus utility, by application rather than conjecture

C65

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Judgement. Demonstrated at a moderate level. The application evidence is the gross margin: 34.7% held in 2023 and 2024 through supply disturbances, 35.4% in 2025 through a 2% organic decline, 36.0% in Q1 2026 and 35.6% in Q2 2026 while freight, metals, semiconductor and component costs rose, with management attributing the gain to pass-through and value-based pricing. The erosion test is volume: organic sales fell 2% in 2025 and were flat in Q1 2026, which could in principle reflect customers resisting price, but the Q2 2026 rebound to +5% organic with gross margin still rising says utility was not sacrificed. Peer benchmarking supports the read (Addtech, Lagercrantz, Bufab, Bossard all expanded gross margin in 2025 to 2026).

The reasoning. What limits the score is the quality of the evidence rather than its direction: no price-volume split, no disclosure of realised price increases, and a model that deliberately does not run a group pricing programme (unlike Lifco's mandated 3 to 7%). A Q3 2026 expert in automation channels reports suppliers recovering only 2 to 3% of 3 to 4% cost inflation now that availability has normalised, which is the first sign of the pricing window narrowing in the standardised end. For the model: price holds the gross margin flat; it is not a source of expansion.

C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers, stated or de facto

❌ D35

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement. Not at all, and by design. The model has no scale economies to share (no central purchasing, no shared logistics, no pricing coordination), the stated strategy is value-based pricing that charges for technical content rather than passing cost savings through, and the culture instructs managing directors to protect the gross margin under all circumstances. The nearest thing to de facto sharing is the local stock-holding and engineering support that reduce the customer's own cost of failure and inventory, which is a service bundle, not a scale economy passed through. This is the opposite of the Costco or Amazon pattern the question describes; the strength of the model lies in not competing on cost.

The reasoning. For the Consumer the implication is that there is no reinforcing loop of the "lower price, more volume, lower unit cost" kind to model; the reinforcing loops here are the acquisition flywheel and the mix shift, not customer-side scale sharing. The score reflects the absence of the attribute, not a defect in the business.

C-47

Gravitational or mechanical improvement in advantage and financial outputs each period, versus heroic dynamism

C60

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement. Partly mechanical. Three forces improve the financial outputs without heroic effort: the acquisition arithmetic (each deal bought at 6 to 8x EBITA and funded from cash adds EBITA at a 12 to 17% pre-tax yield against an interest cost of 3 to 4%, so EPS compounds as long as deals close); the mix shift (acquired companies at 16 to 19% EBITA margins against a 14% group, and Life Science at 18% growing fastest, both lift the group margin by roughly 10 to 20 basis points a year without any operating improvement); and operating leverage from the cost base built in 2023 to 2025 (organic EBITA grows at two to two and a half times organic sales while the base is under-absorbed). Two forces require effort: organic growth (a price-taker on volume, dependent on coaching and the cycle), and the control of 220 companies (the UK case shows what happens when the centre is passive).

The reasoning. The counterweight is that the mechanical forces run only at the pace of deal flow, which is organisationally bounded, and that the acquisition multiple paid is the variable that can turn the arithmetic from gravitational to effortful. Relative to peers, Lifco's and Lagercrantz's arithmetic is stronger (lower multiples, higher margins), Addtech's is similar, Diploma's is stronger but at a cyclical margin peak. For the model: build the mechanical forces explicitly (deal pace times entry multiple times acquired margin; mix uplift; leverage factor) and treat organic growth as the exogenous input.

Company - Economic strength

C5811 questions
C-4958C-5068C-5152C-5260C-5358C-5462C-7560C-7670C-7745C-7862C-7945

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Economically this is a sound, self-funding, asset-light compounder of the second rank. Returns on capital are above the cost of capital but below target and below the peers (ROCE 18%, post-tax ROIC 11 to 14%); margins are at the bottom of the cohort and recovering (EBITA 14.7% in Q2 2026 against 22 to 24% at Lifco and Diploma); cash conversion is high in normal years but flattered in 2024 to 2025 by inventory releases that are now exhausted; financial leverage is low and not a constraint; operating leverage is moderate and currently working in the company's favour; the cost base is not cuttable and does not need to be; and the earnings base is clean once the amortisation of acquired intangibles is handled consistently. The economics support an algorithm of 9 to 11% sales growth (6 to 7% acquired, 3 to 4% organic), an EBITA margin rising from 14.1% underlying in 2025 to 14.5 to 15.0% in 2026 to 2027 and 15.0 to 15.5% by 2028, EBITA growth of 10 to 13% a year after the 2026 rebound, EPS growth 1 to 2 points above that as interest falls, free cash flow near 90 to 100% of net income after a weaker 2026, and no dilution. The two economic facts most likely to break the model are the price paid for acquisitions (8.6x median against 6.7x at peers) and a reversal of organic growth, because the structure has no cost lever to offset either.

C-49

EPS growth being generated, with what revenue growth, and its durability

C58

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement. EPS growth has just turned from negative to strongly positive and is about to be flattered by two artefacts the Consumer must strip out. Reported basic EPS: FY2023 7.86 (+7%), FY2024 7.55 (-4%), FY2025 7.03 (-7%), H1 2026 3.79 (+9.5%), Q2 2026 2.15 (+23%). Revenue: FY2023 +17.8%, FY2024 +2.2%, FY2025 -1.0%, Q2 2026 +10.7% (organic +5%, acquired +6%, currency 0%). Five-year EPS compound growth to 2025 is 8.9% on reported figures (4.60 to 7.03) and about 10% on the brokers' adjusted basis, against 14 to 15% over the five and ten years to 2024 and 15% from the 2005 listing to 2023. For FY2026 the like-for-like expectation is roughly 18 to 24% EPS growth on either basis (reported-basis brokers at SEK 8.4 to 8.7; adjusted-basis brokers at SEK 9.8 to 10.4 against an adjusted FY2025 base of about SEK 8.4 to 8.5), then about 13% in FY2027 and 8% in FY2028 on consensus. The FactSet headline of +42% for FY2026 is the basis artefact described in the reader's note and must not be used.

Durability. The underlying algorithm is 6 to 7% acquired sales growth at accretive margins, 3 to 4% organic growth with a 2 to 2.5x leverage factor to EBITA, and a flat-to-falling interest line, which yields 10 to 13% EBITA growth and 10 to 14% EPS growth in a normal year; 2026 is above that because of the easy base, the restored deal pace and the return of operating leverage, and the FY2027 and FY2028 consensus (+13%, +8%) is a reversion toward the algorithm. Durability depends on the deal pace (the variable most under management's control) and the organic cycle (the variable least under it); the 2024 to 2025 experience shows that when organic growth goes to -2% and acquired contribution to 4%, EPS falls. Peer comparison: Lifco and Addtech have compounded EPS at 15 to 20%; Indutrade's durable rate is 10 to 12%.

C-50

Immediate scope to deploy capital or opex at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what it means for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple

C68

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement. Large and immediate. The company generates SEK 2.4 to 3.5bn of free cash flow a year (FactSet definition SEK 2,397m in FY2025; the company's free operating cash flow SEK 3.5bn), pays SEK 1.1bn of dividends, and can therefore deploy SEK 1.5 to 2.5bn a year into acquisitions at 6 to 8x EBITA (a 12 to 17% pre-tax EBITA yield on cash consideration, before earn-outs) with a 20% return on capital employed target within three to five years, without raising leverage; DNB Carnegie's headroom calculation allows up to SEK 3bn of acquired revenue a year inside the leverage policy. Opex deployment is small (capex 1 to 2% of sales; the 2023 to 2025 SG&A build is done). The reinvestment rate has averaged 71% of cash generation.

What it means. Mid-term potential: at SEK 2bn a year of cash consideration at 7.5x EBITA, acquired EBITA grows by about SEK 270m a year, 6% of the FY2025 base, before earn-outs and before margin accretion; that is the mechanical floor under EBITA growth. Long-term potential: the runway (C-43) supports this for a decade, and Redeye's SEK 22.6bn of acquired sales by 2034 is a plausible envelope. Fair multiple: a business that can reinvest 70% of cash at 13 to 15% post-tax returns for a decade with 1.2x financial leverage deserves a premium to the market (the cohort has traded at 17 to 26x EV/EBITA); Indutrade's own ten-year 24-month-forward P/E median is 22.4x and its five-year median 25.1x. The qualification is the return on new capital, which at an 8.6x median multiple (DNB Carnegie's figure, versus 6.6 to 6.8x at peers) is 12% pre-tax rather than 15%, and which earn-outs reduce further; that justifies a multiple below the cohort's leaders but above a mature industrial.

C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed, and on tangible capital

C52

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Judgement. Good in absolute terms, below target, and bottom-quartile in the cohort. Return on capital employed (company definition) averaged 21% over 2020 to 2024, 19% in late 2025 and 18% at June 2026, against the 20% target; post-tax return on invested capital (median 2014 to 2024) is 14%, against Lifco 21%, Addtech 18.7% and Lagercrantz about 13%; return on tangible capital (excluding goodwill and acquired intangibles, which are about 45% of assets) is very high, with the Risks report's invalidation trigger set at "return on capital ex intangibles falling below 50%", consistent with an asset-light model whose capital is almost entirely the price paid for acquisitions. Post-tax return on new invested capital at the point of acquisition is about 9 to 12% (a 12 to 17% pre-tax EBITA yield at 6 to 8x, taxed at 22 to 24%, before earn-outs and before the three-to-five-year improvement toward the 20% ROCE target).

The reasoning. The structure of the returns tells the Consumer three things. First, the group-level ROCE will stay in the 18 to 20% range while acquisitions run at 6 to 7% of sales, because each new deal enters below the group average and takes three to five years to reach it. Second, the lever for raising returns is the entry multiple, not operations: moving the median from 8.6x toward the peers' 6.7x would lift the return on new capital by about 3 points. Third, the FactSet-basis post-tax return on invested capital (adjusted EBIT 3,774m taxed at 23% over equity plus net debt of roughly SEK 26bn) is about 11%, the figure to use when comparing with the cost of capital; it is above the cost of capital and below the peers.

C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow, and the behaviour of net working capital and similar items

C60

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement. High in normal years, volatile with the working-capital cycle, and about to be tested by growth. FactSet free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) as a percentage of reported net income: 67% (2019), 143% (2020), 89% (2021), 38% (2022, an inventory build), 106% (2023), 95% (2024), 94% (2025); the ten-year median of net income to lease-adjusted free cash flow to equity is 102%. The company's own "cash conversion" measure has exceeded 130% for eleven consecutive quarters, but that measure benefited from inventory reductions of 5% in 2025 and management said in 2025 that further releases would be marginal. Consensus has free cash flow at SEK 2,566m in 2026 (0.8x net income) rising to SEK 4,046m in 2027 and 4,581m in 2028 (1.1 to 1.2x), which assumes working capital normalises after a 2026 build.

Net working capital behaviour. Receivables plus inventories less payables were 24 to 28% of trailing revenue across the last eleven quarters, rising to 27.9% (SEK 9,231m) at June 2026 from 24.2% in March 2025 as the Q2 invoicing surge lifted receivables; days: inventory 88 (from 95), receivables 54, payables 32 in 2025. The model should carry working capital at about 26 to 27% of incremental sales, which means roughly SEK 0.8 to 0.9bn of cash absorbed for each SEK 3bn of sales growth, and should expect 2026 conversion near 80 to 90% of net income before recovering toward 100% in 2027 to 2028. Lease repayments (SEK 570m in 2025) and earn-out settlements (maximum SEK 436m on the H1 2026 deals) are below the FactSet free-cash-flow line and reduce the cash available for new deals. Capex is 1.5 to 2% of sales and needs no special treatment.

C-53

Gross and operating margins, and their trend

C58

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Judgement. Gross margin: 35.4 to 36.0% on the company's basis (33.1 to 33.4% on FactSet's), the highest in the company's history and rising slowly; stable and defensible. Operating margin: EBITA 15.0% (2023), 14.4% (2024), 13.8% reported and 14.1% underlying (2025), 13.3% (Q1 2026), 14.7% reported and 14.9% underlying (Q2 2026); on FactSet's adjusted EBIT (after amortisation of acquired intangibles) 12.8%, 11.9%, 11.7%; EBITDA 17.7%, 17.1%, 17.1% and 17.9% in Q2 2026. Consensus has the brokers'-basis EBIT (effectively EBITA) margin at 14.5% in 2026, 15.0% in 2027 and 15.0% in 2028, and EBITDA at 17.5%, 17.8% and 17.7%. The trend has turned from three years of compression to recovery, with Q2 2026 the first quarter above the 2023 level.

The reasoning. The margin bridge over 2023 to 2025 was entirely below gross profit: gross margin rose about 80 basis points while EBITA margin fell 120 basis points, because SG&A was built for growth that did not come and the UK write-down landed in Q4 2025. The recovery bridge is the mirror image: flat gross margin, operating leverage on SG&A (sales +11% against SG&A +8% in Q2), the Infrastructure & Construction pruning (segment margin 10.5% to 12.2%), and mix (Life Science 18.0%, acquisitions 17 to 19%). The ceiling is set by management's refusal to raise the 14% target and by the trading half's structural margin; the Consumer should model 14.5 to 15.0% EBITA in 2026 to 2027 and 15.0 to 15.5% thereafter, with the segment dispersion (Life Science 17 to 18%, Technology & Systems Solutions 13 to 15%, Infrastructure & Construction 11 to 12%, Industrial & Engineering 14%, Process Energy & Water 15 to 16%) as the building blocks.

C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base: adjusted versus reported, one-offs, capitalised costs, tax and other leakage

C62

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement. Reasonably clean, with one large structural gap and two recurring small ones. The structural gap is amortisation of acquired intangibles, about SEK 650 to 700m a year (EBITA SEK 4,450m against FactSet adjusted EBIT 3,774m in 2025; consensus depreciation and amortisation SEK 1,075m for 2026 against FactSet's total of 1,725m for 2025), which is non-cash, grows with every deal, and is the entire difference between the two EPS bases in consensus. The recurring small items are earn-out remeasurements (positive SEK 27m in Q1 2025, negative SEK 14m in Q2 2026) and one-off project write-downs (SEK 125m in Q4 2025), which the company presents transparently and which net to a modest drag over time. There is no capitalised development to speak of, no capitalised R&D, and point-in-time revenue recognition for almost all sales. Tax is stable at 22 to 24% (22.8% in 2025, 24% in Q1 2026) and Pillar Two is negligible. Net interest was SEK 470m in 2025 (12% of EBITA) and is falling with rates. Below-the-line, lease liabilities of SEK 1.55bn and contingent consideration (up to SEK 436m on H1 2026 deals) are the obligations that do not appear in EBITA.

The reasoning. Two consequences for the Consumer. First, choose one EPS basis (I recommend reported, because the share price is set on a P/E that FactSet computes on a blended consensus and the trading history is on reported EPS) and build amortisation explicitly as 1.5 to 2% of sales growing with deals, so that the EBITA-to-EPS bridge is transparent. Second, treat goodwill and acquired intangibles (about 45% of assets, never impaired) as the balance-sheet item an attacker would target, as at Lifco; there is no evidence of a problem, but the earnings base is only as clean as the acquisitions behind it.

C-75

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how much a change in revenue moves operating profit, and the range it creates

C60

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement. Material but moderate, and asymmetric in timing. On the evidence of Q2 2026, organic sales growth of 5% produced organic EBITA growth of about 12%, a leverage factor of roughly 2.4x; on the 2024 to 2025 evidence, organic sales of -2% to 0% produced EBITA margin compression of 120 basis points because costs rose while volume did not. The company's own sensitivities: a 1 percentage point change in revenue growth is about SEK 322m of sales and SEK 0.10 of EPS; a 2 percentage point change in organic volume moves EPS by SEK 0.32 (about 3%); a 50 basis point change in EBITA margin moves EPS by SEK 0.34 (about 3.3%). The structure behind this: a 35.5% gross margin on incremental organic sales, against SG&A of roughly 21 to 22% of sales that is mostly people and largely fixed over a year.

The range it creates. Over a twelve-month horizon, organic sales between -3% and +6% (the cone the last three years have shown) implies organic EBITA growth between about -7% and +14%, or an EBITA margin range of roughly 13.3% to 15.3% before mix and acquisitions. That is a two-point margin range on a 14 to 15% base, which is wide relative to the earnings but narrow relative to a manufacturer: the asset-light, people-light structure prevents severe deleverage, and the decentralised model lets managing directors cut local cost without central instruction, though slowly (the chief executive refuses blanket cuts to protect scarce expertise). The asymmetry is that leverage arrives fast on the way up when the cost base has been pre-built (2026) and slowly on the way down because cost is cut with a lag (2024 to 2025). For the four-year model, use a leverage factor of 2 to 2.5x on organic growth while the base remains under-absorbed (2026 to 2027) and 1.5x thereafter.

C-76

Financial leverage or gearing: amplification of EPS and risk, and comfort

C70

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement. Low and comfortable; it amplifies little. Net debt (FactSet, including leases and earn-outs) SEK 9,504m at June 2026, 1.65x trailing EBITDA; financial net debt excluding leases and earn-outs 1.2x; net debt to equity 45% against a 100% policy ceiling; equity ratio 51%; S&P BBB- with a positive outlook; SEK 6.3bn of undrawn facilities; a SEK 1.3bn five-year bond at STIBOR plus 113 basis points. Net interest expense was SEK 470m in 2025 (12% of EBITA, 14% of pre-tax profit), down from SEK 592m, and a 1 percentage point move in rates changes expense by about SEK 61m (1.5 to 2% of pre-tax profit). The peers sit at 1.0x (Diploma), 1.3 to 1.5x (Addtech, Lifco) and 1.8 to 2.4x (Lagercrantz).

The reasoning. The relevance to EPS is small and positive: as deals are funded from cash and rates fall, the interest line shrinks relative to EBITA, adding perhaps 1 point to EPS growth a year; consensus has pre-tax profit growing faster than EBIT (SEK 3,999m to 4,700m to 5,157m, +18% then +10%, against EBIT +12% and +5%). The relevance to risk is that leverage could rise to 2.0 to 2.5x without stress if the deal pace doubled, and that the Riksbank's likely hikes are a minor cost. The hidden leverage is earn-outs and minority obligations, which are small here (maximum SEK 436m on the H1 2026 deals) compared with Lifco's SEK 3bn of put options.

C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible

❌ D45

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement. Little, by design, and slowly. The cost base is roughly: cost of goods sold SEK 20.8bn (64.6% of sales, variable, not cuttable except through sourcing and pricing); SG&A and other operating costs about SEK 6.5 to 7bn (20 to 22% of sales, mostly local salaries, premises and logistics across 220 companies, cuttable only unit by unit); depreciation and amortisation SEK 1.7bn (fixed, non-cash, rising with deals); central costs for 24 people (not material). The demonstrated cuttability is about 200 employees (roughly 2% of the workforce) removed from demand-challenged units over 12 months, the Infrastructure & Construction restructuring and divestments, and the closure of the UK projects; the demonstrated non-cuttability is the 2024 to 2025 period, when SG&A grew while volume fell and the chief executive explained that lean trading companies lose scarce expertise if cut indiscriminately.

The reasoning. The Consumer should model cost as flexing with a lag of two to four quarters on the way down and as largely fixed within a year, and should not model a cost programme: there is none and the model does not permit one. The reducible fraction of SG&A in a prolonged downturn is perhaps 3 to 5%, concentrated in the problem-company category of the portfolio model, which is the Addtech precedent (unprofitable Automation sites closed at a SEK 10m cost). The strength of this structure is that costs do not have to be cut for margins to hold in a mild downturn (2024 to 2025 margins fell 120 basis points on a 2% organic decline); its weakness is that there is no cost lever to pull if the recovery fails.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, scope to improve financial outputs over the mid term; peer precedent; few big areas or many small; congruence with strategy

C62

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement. Moderate scope, from many small areas, achievable on peer precedent, and only partly congruent with the stated strategy. The areas: operating leverage on the pre-built cost base (worth 50 to 100 basis points of EBITA margin if organic growth runs at 3 to 5% for two years); mix from acquisitions at 16 to 19% margins (10 to 20 basis points a year); the Life Science share rising (18% margin, growing fastest); Infrastructure & Construction recovery from 11 to 12% toward its pre-2024 level; Technology & Systems Solutions recovering from 13.7% toward 15 to 16% as the UK project companies are fixed; working-capital discipline through the segment layer; lower interest cost. Together these support an EBITA margin of 15 to 15.5% by 2028 against 14.1% underlying in 2025, and Redeye's 17% by 2034 is the outer envelope. Peer precedent exists for each: Addtech lifted EBITA margin from 11.6% to 16.0% in six years on mix and pricing; Lagercrantz from 16.6% to 18.1% in three years on proprietary share; Lifco's Dental from 20% to 23% on manufactured mix.

Congruence. Only partly. Management has said it will not raise the 14% margin target, prefers absolute profit growth, wants segment leaders free to buy lower-margin high-return trading businesses, and will not centralise pricing or purchasing. The margin improvement that occurs will therefore be a by-product of mix and leverage, not a programme, and the proprietary share will rise only as fast as acquisitions tilt it. That is why the mid-term upside is bounded at about 15.5% rather than the 17 to 20% of the better peers; the Consumer should model the improvement as many small, mechanical increments rather than a step.

C-79

Narrowness of the business perimeter, its change, and what that means for predictability

❌ D45

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement. Very wide and widening: 220-plus companies in more than 30 countries, five business areas, 35 segments, more than 12 customer segments, roughly 80% of sales outside Sweden (Sweden 22%, UK and Ireland 12%, Benelux 10%, Denmark 9%, North America 5%, Asia 4%), and a trading-proprietary mix of about 58/42. Every acquisition widens it further, and the recent deals add geographies (Spain, Italy, Poland) and sub-verticals (marine aftermarket, composites, electrification). This is the opposite of a narrow-perimeter case; the earlier Triaging memo graded the simplicity guardrail D for exactly this reason.

What it means for predictability. Two opposite effects, and the Consumer should model both. At the group level, the perimeter makes the aggregate more predictable than any part: no customer above 3% of sales, no subsidiary above 1% of revenue at risk from a single manager, sales down only 7.5% in 2008 to 2009, and a 14 to 15% EBITA margin across cycles since 2005; the law of large numbers works for the top line and the margin. At the level of disclosure and control, the perimeter makes the detail unpredictable: segment-level figures are the finest the company gives, no backlog by area, no price-volume split, and a concealment in two UK companies went undetected for several quarters. The practical consequence is that group-level forecasts of revenue and EBITA margin are reliable within one to two points, while quarter-level surprises (write-downs, earn-out revaluations, project timing, currency) are frequent and of the order of 5 to 10% of a quarter's EBITA. Predictability of the whole is high; predictability of the parts is low.
