Company - Management

C5511 questions
C-5552C-5658C-5752C-57b62C-5862C-5952C-6058C-6155C-6240C-6355C-6455

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Management is disciplined, honest and capable, and it has chosen a ceiling for itself. The chief executive has doubled the company, kept the allocation rules intact through a downturn, rebuilt the organisation for scale, hired well into the weak spots, and communicated plainly about failures; he has also presided over two years of stagnation, a control failure, a drift of SG&A ahead of demand, the cohort's highest acquisition multiples and lowest margins, and has declined both to raise the margin ambition and to add the audit scaffolding the failure called for. Insider ownership is weak throughout. The culture is coherent and the recent hires improve the bench. For the Consumer, this management supports a model of continuity (stable rules, stable policy, no dilution, programmatic deals, margin held near 14 to 15%) and does not support a model of transformation; the probability of a positive surprise from management action is low, the probability of a negative surprise from management action is also low, and the residual risk is control at subsidiary level.

C-55

Size of the company's ambition or vision, particularly against peers

C52

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement. Moderate, and deliberately so. The stated ambition is to double sales and profit again over five to eight years (the arithmetic of the existing 10% growth target), to scale from about 15 to 30 acquisitions a year, and to keep the 14% margin floor rather than raise it. That is a real but conventional ambition for a serial acquirer, and it sits below the peers on the dimension the market rewards: Lagercrantz has committed to a 20% EBITA margin within two to three years and a 60% return on working capital; Addtech to 15% earnings growth every year and doubling every five; Lifco to 15 to 20% EBITA growth; Diploma raised its guidance three times in 2026. Indutrade's chief executive frames the goal as "sustainable absolute profit growth" and has said raising the margin target would harm deal velocity. The force is growth as a priority through acquisition, not out-competing or innovating.

The reasoning. The ambition is credible because the structure to deliver it was built before the target was restated and because the doubling has been done once already under this chief executive (SEK 14.8bn in 2017 to 32.2bn in 2025). It is modest because it asks nothing new of the organic engine, accepts a cohort-low margin as permanent policy, and expresses no intent to close the return gap to peers. For the model, the ambition translates to a 10% sales growth target that the company has missed for two years and will meet in 2026 largely on the easy base; it should not be modelled as a stretch.

C-56

Quality and boldness of the strategy: widening the moat, growth priority, dynamic across environments

C58

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement. Sound, incremental, and adequately dynamic; not bold. The strategy has four strands: keep the companies autonomous and the centre tiny; buy technically specialised B2B niche leaders at 5 to 8x from founders who want a permanent home; build an internal sourcing and coaching layer (35 segments, eight regional holding companies, AI screening) so that deal volume can double without central bureaucracy; and tilt the mix toward proprietary products and structurally growing niches (Life Science, electrification, water) through what is bought. It widens the group moat (deal flow) and preserves the subsidiary moats (autonomy, local pricing) without attempting to deepen them centrally. It is dynamic across environments in the sense that the capital-light, diversified base survived 2008 to 2009 with sales down 7.5% and 2024 to 2025 with margins down 120 basis points, and the balance sheet lets the company buy through a downturn.

The reasoning. What makes it less than great is what it leaves out: no mechanism for organic growth beyond coaching, no pricing playbook of the Lifco kind, no proprietary-share target of the Lagercrantz kind, no plan to lift returns on new capital (the entry multiple is the highest in the cohort), and no governance investment after a control failure. Against the Wisdom Library's "name the marathon" test, the marathon here is capital allocation at scale, and the strategy names it correctly; it simply runs it at the cohort's average pace and price. For the Consumer, the strategy supports a stable algorithm; it does not create an inflection.

C-57

The chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture

C52

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

Judgement. Competent, disciplined, transparent and not exceptional; an enabler who has not ossified the company but has not driven it to outlier standards either. The actions on the record over nine years: more than doubled sales (SEK 14.8bn to 32.2bn); kept the acquisition multiple at 5 to 8x and leverage at 1.1 to 1.7x; never issued equity or bought back shares; executed two reorganisations (2024 to five sector areas, 2025 to 35 segments) without a central cost build; removed the UK managers promptly and said the deviations were "completely unacceptable"; cut about 200 jobs in weak units; pruned four businesses; refreshed three of five business-area heads and the finance chief with external operators; and reconfirmed rather than raised the targets. Against that: EBITA margin peaked at 14.7% in 2021 and fell to 13.8% in 2025; five-year EPS compound growth slowed from 14 to 15% to about 10%; acquired contribution fell to 4 to 5% in 2024 to 2025; SG&A was allowed to run ahead of demand for 18 months; and the UK concealment happened on his watch under a governance model he chose not to strengthen afterward.

The reasoning. The prior-company evidence (Haldex 2012 to 2017: margins from 5.3% to 6.6% with a 9.3% peak, a 437% five-year return, a factory relocation, partly a cyclical tailwind) shows an operator who can fix a cost base and sell a company well, consistent with the Indutrade record of discipline rather than dynamism. His share ownership (83,138 shares, about one to two times salary, plus 300,000 synthetic options with no downside; no open-market purchases in 2025) is the weakest alignment signal in the cohort. On the Wisdom Library's operator dimensions, he scores well on capital discipline and communication, adequately on execution, and poorly on outlier standards. For the model, expect continuity and prudence; do not expect a step change in ambition from this chief executive.

C-57b

Priority on hiring well, and the quality of the leadership team built

C62

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement. A visible priority, and the recent hires are the best evidence of it. In the last 20 months the chief executive has appointed an external finance chief with M&A credentials (Anna Vilogorac, Ratos, ex Sandvik and Nuent), an external founder of a serial acquirer to run Technology & Systems Solutions (Peter Laveson, ex NOTE and Svevik Industri), an external industrial operator to Process, Energy & Water (Per Lidström, ex Cibes Lift, Seco Tools, Sandvik), a returning former subsidiary head with large-company experience to Infrastructure & Construction (Håkan Svensson, ex Dahrén, Södra), an internal promotion to head of acquisitions (Gustav Ruda, with the company since 2015), and a bridge interim finance chief who held the role before (Jan Öhman). The segment-leader layer is internal (average 18 years at Indutrade), more than 400 managers have been through the group leadership programmes, 43 went through them in 2025, and a dedicated chief-financial-officer development programme was added for subsidiaries.

The reasoning. The team is now stronger in finance and in the two weakest business areas than it was 18 months ago, which is where strengthening was needed. The limits: the bench is thin at the top (the model depends on the chief executive and a small group), insider ownership is low throughout (the Process, Energy & Water head holds no shares), and the people-quality record at subsidiary level is uneven (two UK managers colluded to hide overruns; employee turnover rose to 13.7% in 2025; survey coverage and development dialogues are improving). Compared with Addtech (entirely home-grown, 15-year tenure) and Lagercrantz (three-person group management, no successor to a 20-year chief executive), Indutrade's willingness to hire outside is a differentiator and a modest cultural risk. For the model: the finance handover is the only near-term execution risk from the team.

C-58

How well resourced and invested the whole business is

C62

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Judgement. Well invested for its model, and slightly over-invested in the one place that now helps. Capital intensity is 1.5 to 2% of sales (SEK 502m in 2025) because the subsidiaries assemble and trade rather than manufacture heavily; the inventory base was rebuilt to normal after the pandemic and then reduced 5% in 2025; the holding-company network was expanded to eight hubs; segment leaders carry 6 to 8 companies against a capacity of 10 to 15, which is deliberate slack; AI screening and a leadership academy exist; and SG&A was built in 2023 to 2025 for volume that is only now arriving, which is why operating leverage appeared in Q2 2026. The balance sheet is a buffer in itself: SEK 6.3bn undrawn, 1.2x financial leverage, investment grade.

The reasoning. The under-investment is in control, not in operations: no internal audit function across 220 companies, annual control questionnaires validated selectively, and a reliance on segment controllers that the UK case showed can be defeated by two colluding managers. The Risks report's "false friend" narrative that subsidiaries might starve capex to flatter margins is not supported by the numbers (capex to sales 1.5 to 2%, consistent with history, and the company's own warning threshold is 0.5%). On the ant-colony test the business is resilient (no single point of failure, cash funded, diversified) and lightly governed. For the model: no catch-up investment is needed; the built cost base is the operating-leverage asset for 2026 to 2027.

C-59

Holistic congruence of the culture across goals, strategy, execution and decisions

C52

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Judgement. Congruent in its core and strained at its edges. The core is a single idea applied everywhere: the subsidiary managing director owns the income statement, balance sheet, pricing and customers; protects the gross margin "under all circumstances"; is paid on local EBITA growth; and is left alone by a 24-person parent. The strategy (buy founders who want that), the sourcing model (segment leaders who were themselves managing directors), the financial policy (self-funded, no synergies, no central purchasing), the targets (growth and margin over a cycle, no quarterly guidance) and the capital-allocation rules (5 to 8x, 20% ROCE, EPS accretion) all follow from it. The edges are where the culture is being adjusted: a mid-layer of 35 segment leaders, structured monthly scorecards, portfolio categories, business controllers and sustainability support, which the company calls "support" and a former Lifco acquisition head calls centralisation and silos. The UK concealment is the cultural failure mode made visible: autonomy plus EBITA-linked bonuses plus thin oversight gave two managers the means, motive and opportunity to hide deviations.

The reasoning. On the Lifco or Avanza standard of a total system where every part reinforces every other, Indutrade is close but not there: Lifco's version is purer (no HR, no procurement, managers told to ignore share and maximise absolute profit, 50% owned by management and board), and it shows in margins (22 to 23%) and returns (132% ROCE ex-goodwill). Indutrade's version is the same model with more structure and less skin in the game, which explains the gap. For the model, the culture supports stable margins and steady deal flow; it does not support a Lifco-style margin step.

C-60

Clarity, straightforwardness and helpfulness of communication, including on the driver transmission

C58

Question asked How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

Judgement. Clear and honest about direction, deliberately unhelpful about quantities. The company reports five business areas with sales, organic growth and EBITA margin, gives order intake and book-to-bill, names acquisitions with their sales and margins, discloses the growth bridge (organic, acquired, currency), holds a Capital Markets Day every three years with a transparent account of the model, and explains deviations plainly (the chief executive called the UK deviations unacceptable and said he was not satisfied with Q1 2026). It gives no numerical guidance, no backlog by business area, no price-volume split, no segment disclosure below the five areas, no acquisition multiples per deal, and it offered "weather" as part of the Q1 2026 explanation, which the Q2 construction numbers undermined.

The reasoning. The transmission mechanism is communicated in principle (deal pace at 5 to 8x plus organic growth plus 14% margin equals double-digit profit growth) and the building blocks are stated (segment leaders, one deal per segment, 20% ROCE in three to five years), so the Consumer can build it; what the company does not supply is the quarterly path, which is why consensus dispersion is wide (FY2026 EPS 8.40 to 10.40 across bases, targets SEK 260 to 322). Brokers rate the communication highly (DNB Carnegie gives a 4.0 of 5.0 target-fulfilment credibility score; both brokers call management an honest tracker). Relative to Lifco ("we don't forecast the future") it is the same posture; relative to Diploma (guidance raised three times) it is far less helpful for short-term modelling.

C-61

Record of acting, not just talking: recent delivery and mid-term track record, whatever the environment

C55

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement. A strong mid-term record, a weak recent record, and a strong latest quarter. Mid-term: 15% EPS compound growth from the 2005 listing to 2023, 12% sales growth, an average EBITA margin of 14.6% over 2021 to 2025 against a 14% target, sales down only 7.5% in 2008 to 2009, 16 and 13 acquisitions in 2024 and 2025 through a downturn, and a doubling of the group under the current chief executive. Recent (late 2024 to Q1 2026): sales growth of 2% and -1% against a 10% target, EBITA margin of 13.8% against 14%, acquired contribution of 4 to 5% against the 7 to 8% norm, three consensus misses in four quarters including a 14% adjusted EBITA miss in Q4 2025 and 5 to 6% in Q1 2026, EPS down two years running, and a SEK 125m control failure. Latest (Q2 2026): organic +5% against 1.7% expected, EBITA 11 to 12% above consensus, margin +100 basis points, nine deals in a half-year, and the restructuring producing the deal flow it was built for.

The reasoning. The question asks about delivery whatever the environment, and the honest answer is that Indutrade delivered on margin through the downturn (the floor held at 13.3 to 13.8%) and did not deliver on growth or on control; Addtech, in the same environment, delivered 12% EBITA growth on flat organic sales. The record of action rather than justification is good: managers removed, costs cut, businesses sold, structure rebuilt, deals closed. For the model, the mid-term record justifies the 14% margin floor and the 5 to 8x discipline as durable; the recent record justifies modelling the growth target as aspirational.

C-62

Outlier standards: higher, faster, better, with a record of out-executing competitors

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

Judgement. No. On the standards the cohort measures itself by, Indutrade is bottom-quartile: EBITA margin 14.7% in Q2 2026 against Lifco 23.1%, Lagercrantz 17 to 20%, Addtech 16.6%, Diploma 24.5%; decade organic growth about 4% against Addtech 6.5% and Lifco 6%; return on capital employed 18% against a 20% target and Lifco's 20.5% including goodwill; acquisition multiples at the top of the cohort; five-year EPS growth 10% against 17 to 24% at Lagercrantz. The two places where it is an outlier are positive and narrow: cash conversion (ten-year median 102% of net income to free cash flow to equity; 137% rolling on the company's measure in Q2 2026, top 5% of the peer set) and the scale of its decentralisation (220 companies against 50 to 150 at most peers).

The reasoning. This matters for the investment case because the sector's outperformers are the companies that out-execute on margin and return, and the market prices the gap (a 15 to 20% discount on EV/EBITA, a P/E of 23x against 27 to 28x for Addtech, Lagercrantz and Diploma). Management does not claim outlier standards and has explicitly chosen not to pursue the margin standard. For the model, assume Indutrade executes at the cohort's median pace and at its own historical quality; the discount is earned, and closing it would require standards the company has not set for itself.

C-63

Shareholders first, ahead of management, and prudence of governance scaffolding

C55

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement. Shareholders are treated fairly and conservatively; the governance scaffolding is prudent in form and has one demonstrated gap in substance. The evidence for shareholders first: one share, one vote with no voting limitations (unlike the dual-class structures at Lifco, Addtech and Lagercrantz); no equity issuance in a decade and no dilution (364.3m shares for four years; incentive programmes 0.12% of shares); a progressive dividend inside a 30 to 50% policy; self-funded growth within a stated leverage ceiling; modest executive pay (chief executive SEK 25.8m in 2024, 45 times the median employee, against SEK 84.5m and 123 times at Lifco) with three-year EPS-linked performance shares requiring co-investment; arm's-length related-party dealings; clean audits through a mandatory rotation from PwC to KPMG; no activist and no short-seller interest. The evidence against: an anchor shareholder (Lundbergs, 26.6%) with three of eight board seats, a chair who also chairs the remuneration committee and sits on the audit committee, five of eight directors independent of major shareholders (the Code minimum is two), female representation down to 38%, very low director share ownership, and, decisively, a board that reviewed a concealment which ran for several quarters and concluded that no internal audit function was needed.

The reasoning. The question asks for scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed; here scaffolding was not added even where it was shown to be needed, which is the opposite. The compensation design has a second-order risk the leadership research flags: three-year accumulated EPS hurdles can reward debt-funded acquisitions, though the leverage ceiling and the entry-multiple rule contain it. The LTIP conditions have been met consistently, which suggests achievable hurdles. On balance the governance is honest and shareholder-aligned on the big decisions (no dilution, no empire deals, no excessive pay) and under-built on control, which is a risk to earnings quality rather than to shareholders' primacy.

C-64

Enlightened capital allocation and dynamic repurchasing, with a record of allocating at high returns

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement. A disciplined allocator and not a repurchaser. The allocation record over a decade: about 70% of cash generation reinvested in acquisitions (SEK 9.1bn over five years; SEK 1.5 to 2.1bn a year in 2024 to 2026) at 5 to 8x EBITA with a 20% ROCE requirement within three to five years, producing a median post-tax return on invested capital of 14% and a group ROCE of 18 to 21%; the remaining 30 to 45% paid as a growing dividend; no equity raised; no buyback ever; leverage held at 1.1 to 1.7x. The quality of the allocation is good but not best-in-class: the realised median multiple of 8.6x is the highest among the four proven compounders, earn-outs add about 0.4x sales, and returns on capital trail Lifco and Addtech by 4 to 7 points. The 2024 to 2025 slowdown in deal flow was a capacity problem that the restructuring addressed, not a discipline problem.

The reasoning. The absence of buybacks is a considered policy rather than an omission: at 19 to 23x the shares are more expensive than the targets, so reinvestment at 6 to 8x is the better use of cash, and management says dividends will rise in absolute terms every year. The test of enlightenment is whether the company would buy back shares if the deal market became expensive; nothing in the files suggests it would, which is a mild negative for optionality. For the model: cash goes to acquisitions first, dividends second, debt reduction third, with no repurchase line.

Risks

C504 questions
C-8055C-8160C-8245C-8340

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The fundamental risks are moderate and diversified; the sentiment risks are larger and concentrated in the next six months. The engine has just come off its slowest twelve months since listing and has one quarter of recovery behind it; the sudden fundamental risks (another concealment, a krona spike, a European recession, a large deal going wrong) are each plausible and each capped by the structure at a few percent of earnings; the sentiment risks (a yield-driven cohort de-rating, a short-seller attack on the cohort, a Q3 disappointment into a 52-week-high share price) could take 15 to 25% off the shares with no change in the four-year earnings path. The fair-trough calculation gives about 25% downside to 17 to 18x and 35 to 40% in a combined earnings-and-multiple bear case, against about 18% upside to a defendable multiple. For the Consumer, the operating model's downside cases should be built around organic growth returning to 0% and the acquisition multiple rising, not around a collapse; the financial model should carry the multiple as the dominant risk variable over 18 months.

C-80

Deceleration of the core growth engine over the last twelve months: below the normal rate, and is the input force slowing

C55

Question asked To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Judgement. The engine decelerated below its normal rate for the four quarters to March 2026 and re-accelerated in the quarter to June 2026; the input force (European industrial demand) was slowing through mid-2025, bottomed around the turn of the year, and is now turning up, but the macro conditions that drive it deteriorated again in October 2026. Over the last twelve reported months (Q3 2025 to Q2 2026), total revenue growth was -1.6%, -1.3%, +0.3% and +10.7%; organic -1%, 0%, 0% and +5%; the normal rate for this company is 10 to 12% total and 4 to 5% organic. On a trailing-twelve-month basis the company is still below its normal rate (about +2% total); on a run-rate basis it is above it.

The reasoning. The two engines decelerated for different reasons and are re-accelerating for different reasons. The acquired engine slowed because the restructuring absorbed management attention and sellers hesitated at high rates (contribution 4 to 5% in 2024 to 2025); it re-accelerated because the segment structure matured and rates fell (7 to 8% in H1 2026). The organic engine slowed with European industrial production, destocking and construction; it re-accelerated with Life Science, energy and the end of destocking. The input force for the acquired engine (seller willingness, financing cost) is now at risk again from rising Swedish and European rates; the input force for the organic engine (PMI, industrial production) is improving but construction is deferred to 2027. For the Consumer: the last twelve months show the engine at its slowest in the company's listed history, with a one-quarter recovery; the base effect alone guarantees above-normal growth through Q1 2027, after which the normal rate is the right assumption.

C-81

Fundamental risks that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability: plausibility and size of each

C60

Question asked What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

Judgement. Six, each individually contained by diversification, and ranked by expected damage. (1) Another concealed deterioration in one or more subsidiaries, of the UK kind: plausible (the governance gap is unchanged), size SEK 100 to 300m of EBITA in a quarter (the Risks report's re-pre-mortem trigger is cumulative write-downs above SEK 250m); the most likely to recur. (2) A sharp krona appreciation of 10%: plausible on the consensus path, size about SEK 180m of net profit (7%) and SEK 2.4bn of reported sales, with no hedge. (3) A European industrial recession with construction and project cancellations: lower probability, size organic sales -5 to -8% and EBITA margin down to 12 to 13% (2009 precedent: sales -7.5%), with order backlogs in energy eroding rather than cushioning. (4) A large acquisition failing or a cluster of larger deals (SEK 250 to 345m targets) under-delivering, with goodwill impairment: low probability per deal, rising with size, size SEK 200 to 500m. (5) A Life Science customer or reimbursement shock (Novo Nordisk deliveries have already normalised; Polish public procurement is a new dependency): low probability, size 1 to 2 points of group organic growth. (6) A cyber or compliance failure across the decentralised IT estate, or a principal going direct in a cluster of subsidiaries: low probability, local in size.

The reasoning. None of these is large enough to threaten solvency (1.2x financial leverage, SEK 6.3bn undrawn, no customer above 3% of sales, no subsidiary above 1% at risk) and the historical base rate of a diversified Nordic compounder failing outright is near zero. The risk that matters for the four-year model is the first, because it strikes at earnings quality and at the multiple at once, and because the mitigant the company relies on (segment leaders detecting deviations faster) is unproven.

C-82

Perception- or sentiment-driven risks that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere

❌ D45

Question asked What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement. Four, and together they are the larger near-term risk to the share price than the fundamentals. (1) A cohort de-rating on yields: the sector digest's rule that compounders de-rate together when yields rise, the US ten-year at 5.1%, Riksbank and ECB hikes likely, Sweden moved to Neutral in the Fitness for Fighting view, and the sector's P/E multiples already down 6 to 7% in a year; magnitude 15 to 25% of the share price without any change in earnings. (2) A short-seller campaign on a peer or on Indutrade: The Analyst's unreadable January 2026 note cost Lifco 16% and opened a 21% discount; Indutrade shares the features an attacker would cite (acquired intangibles about 45% of assets, earn-outs, no internal audit, a thin centre, a recent concealment); magnitude 10 to 20%. (3) A private-equity exit or overhang in the cohort (EQT at OEM International and Beijer Ref) or a failed roll-up (Storskogen precedent) resetting how the market prices decentralised acquirers; magnitude 5 to 10%. (4) The Q3 2026 print tomorrow disappointing against a share price at a 52-week high (SEK 267 against 272), 18.8% above the 200-day average, with sell-side momentum rated A and 70% buy ratings; a miss of the Q1 2026 kind would take 10 to 15% off quickly, as the 40% rebound after Q2 shows the sensitivity in both directions.

The reasoning. The common thread is that Indutrade's rating is set relative to a cohort and to a discount rate it does not control; its own fundamentals set the floor (the 15 to 20% discount to peers is already in the price) but not the ceiling. The Consumer's financial model should separate the earnings path from the multiple path and should treat the multiple as the larger source of 18-month variance.

C-83

How far the shares could fall to a fair trough multiple

❌ D40

Question asked How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

Judgement. About 25% to a fair trough, about 35% to a crash trough, from SEK 267 today. The reference points: the 24-month-forward P/E is 22.9x today (29th percentile of its five-year range of 17.9 to 38.2x, median 25.1x; 41st percentile of two years); the ten-year percentile bands used by the dashboard are 32.7x (mania), 27.3x (defendable), 22.4x (median), 20.0x (conservative) and 15.3x (crash); the Swedish industrial small-cap universe trades at about 13x forward; DNB Carnegie's bear case applies a 15% EBITA cut and a 15% multiple contraction (19x to 16.15x EV/EBITA) for SEK 170; Redeye's bear case is SEK 160 (6% sales growth with margin compression); the 2025 low was SEK 170 to 181.

The arithmetic. On consensus 24-month-forward EPS of about SEK 11.5 to 11.7 (blended basis), a fair trough multiple of 17 to 18x (the five-year low and the ten-year 20th to 25th percentile) gives SEK 195 to 210, a fall of 21 to 27%; a crash multiple of 15x gives about SEK 175, a fall of 35%; and if the EPS also falls 15% as in the bear case, the crash level is SEK 150 to 170, a fall of 36 to 44%, which matches the brokers' bear cases. The two-year-ago precedent (SEK 330 to 170, down 48%, on an organic stall plus a write-down plus a de-rating) shows the full combination is possible. The upside to a defendable multiple (27x on SEK 11.6) is about SEK 315, +18%, which is where the most bullish broker sits (Nordea SEK 322). The skew from today's price and multiple is roughly symmetric on the multiple alone and negative once the earnings cone is added.

Guidance

❌ D486 questions
C-6850C-7040C-7152C-7245C-8558C-7350

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The company's guidance is a set of through-cycle definitions, not forecasts, and it will not be the source of any catalyst or any disappointment: the targets sit where consensus sits for 2026 and 2027, they have never been raised, and they are unlikely to be raised before 2028. The informal commentary is the only forward signal and it is constructive but qualitative, already reflected in consensus, and pre-dates the October macro turn. The building blocks of the algorithm are stated clearly enough to model; the quarterly path is not, and the quarterly numbers carry unguided noise. For the Consumer, the formal guidance supplies a margin floor (14%) and a growth ceiling (10%) and nothing in between; the model must be built from the blocks and the run-rate, and the predictability of the forecasts over 18 months will rest on the company's delivery against consensus rather than against anything it has promised.

C-68

Company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this year and next, and why

C50

Question asked To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement. The company gives no numerical guidance for any period, so the only comparison is the through-cycle targets against consensus, and on that comparison the targets and consensus sit on top of each other for 2026 and 2027: the 10% sales growth target against consensus +9.7% (FY2026) and +8.5% (FY2027); the 14% EBITA margin floor against consensus 14.5% and 15.0%; the 20% ROCE target against an actual of 18%. The sales target is marginally above consensus (and consensus itself requires H2 2026 revenue growth of about 14%, which is demanding); the margin target is below consensus, as it is a floor the company has exceeded on average since 2021; the return target is above delivery. There is therefore nothing in the formal guidance for the company to beat or miss, and no gap for the Consumer to exploit in either direction.

The reasoning. Why the targets are where they are: management treats them as a definition of the model (10% growth times 14% margin equals double-digit profit compounding) rather than as a forecast, and declined at the November 2025 Capital Markets Day to raise the margin floor so as not to constrain segment leaders' choice of targets. Why consensus is where it is: it is built bottom-up from the Q2 2026 run-rate, the announced acquisitions and a gradual organic recovery, which is why it lands on the targets. The implication is that the formal guidance is uninformative for the next 18 months and the informal commentary (C-85) carries the signal.

C-70

Record, nature and behaviour in upgrading guidance, beyond meeting or beating

❌ D40

Question asked Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

Judgement. The company does not upgrade guidance, because it does not give any; its targets have been reconfirmed, never raised, at the 2022 and 2025 Capital Markets Days and at every quarterly report, and the chief executive refused an explicit invitation to raise the margin target in November 2025. The qualitative behaviour is that of an honest tracker: when the quarter is weak the company says so ("not satisfied", "completely unacceptable") and points to the order book; when it is strong it says so and points to the backlog and pipeline; it never pre-announces and it never guides to a number. The only upgrades that occur are the sell-side's.

The reasoning. For the investment case this is a negative on the narrow question asked (there is no upgrade cycle for the company to run) and neutral on the wider one (management's credibility score of 4.0 of 5.0 at DNB Carnegie rests on not over-promising). Peers behave similarly at Lifco ("we don't forecast the future") and Addtech; Lagercrantz raised its margin and return targets in 2025; Diploma raised guidance three times in 2026. The Consumer should expect no target changes over the four-year horizon unless the margin runs above 15.5% for an extended period, in which case the floor might be lifted to 15%, and should treat the 2028 Capital Markets Day (the cadence has been three years) as the only plausible occasion.

C-71

Conservatism of current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five, adjusted for the difference in change forces

C52

Question asked How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

Judgement. Appropriately set rather than conservative. Against the last five years, the targets were comfortably met on margin (average 14.6% over 2021 to 2025 against a 14% floor) and met on growth only in the boom years (sales +13%, +24%, +18% in 2021 to 2023, then +2% and -1%); EPS compounded 9 to 10% against the 15% long-run history. Against the next three years, consensus has growth of 9.7%, 8.5% and 5.0% and margins of 14.5 to 15.0%, so the 10% growth target is a stretch in 2028 on current deal pace and the 14% margin floor is slack by 50 to 100 basis points. Adjusting for the change forces: the organic input is weaker than in 2021 to 2023 (GDP-plus demand without the post-pandemic restock), the acquired input is stronger than in 2024 to 2025 (the segment structure now works) but faces higher competition and higher multiples, and the currency input turns from tailwind to headwind on the consensus path. Netting those, a company that grew sales 12% a year from 2005 to 2023 and targets 10% from here is asking for slightly less than its history delivered, in an environment that is somewhat less favourable; that is neither sandbagging nor over-reach.

The reasoning. The one target that is conservative is the margin floor, which the company has deliberately kept low to preserve deal flexibility and which the mix shift will lift mechanically; the one that is ambitious is the 20% ROCE, which the current 8.6x entry multiple makes hard to reach at the group level. For the model: use the margin floor as a floor and the growth target as a ceiling for the acquired-plus-organic algorithm beyond 2027.

C-72

Specificity of the guidance: stated building blocks, or vague

❌ D45

Question asked How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

Judgement. The building blocks are stated; the path is not. The company discloses the components of its algorithm with precision: acquisition multiples of 5 to 8x EV/EBITA on initial consideration, earn-outs of about 0.4x sales over one to three years, a 20% ROCE requirement within three to five years, EPS accretion per deal, at least one deal per segment per year across 35 segments, a target size of SEK 30 to 250m of sales, a 30 to 50% payout, net debt to equity under 100%; it reports the growth bridge (organic, acquired, currency) each quarter, order intake, book-to-bill, and acquired companies' sales and EBITA margins. It does not state how the 10% growth splits between organic and acquired, what organic growth the cycle should produce, where the margin lands in any year, or what the backlog and lead times mean for the next two quarters beyond qualitative phrases ("a positive step in Q2 and a larger improvement in Q3", "the pipeline remains exceptionally strong").

The reasoning. The Consumer can therefore reconstruct the medium-term algorithm from stated blocks (which is what the sell-side does, with DNB Carnegie at 7% acquired EBITA growth to 2031 and Redeye at 18 deals and SEK 22.6bn of acquired sales by 2034) but cannot check a quarter against guidance. This is a deliberate choice consistent with the decentralised model: the centre does not know the subsidiaries' quarters either. Relative to the cohort it is standard; relative to a guided industrial it is vague.

C-85

Informal and qualitative commentary against formal guidance and consensus, and why

C58

Question asked To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement. Informal commentary is above formal guidance and roughly in line with, or slightly above, consensus. The qualitative messages since July 2026: the order backlog 8% above a year earlier with deliveries extending through Q3 and Q4; the M&A pipeline "exceptionally strong across all five business areas"; demand broadening in Life Science beyond pharma into hospitals and laboratories, with Poland called out as a growth engine; Infrastructure & Construction at an "early operational bottoming"; Technology & Systems Solutions with "no recurring write-downs expected" but recovery "will take time"; costs should not rise proportionately with revenue; and acquired companies are larger on average without a change in strategy. DNB Carnegie's Q3 preview of 5 October 2026 ("we stay on the sideline") kept its Hold and its SEK 260 target, which says the broker reads the commentary as already in its numbers; consensus EPS for 2027 has risen 6% in six months and the buy share has risen 30 points to 70%, which says the market has moved toward the tone.

The reasoning. Why above formal guidance: the formal targets are floors and definitions, and any quarter of 11% growth at 14.7% margin exceeds them. Why in line with consensus: consensus is built from exactly this commentary plus the announced deals, and its H2 2026 implication (revenue +14%, EBITA +16% underlying) already prices a continuation of Q2's momentum. The risk in the commentary is that it is qualitative in the two places where the model is most sensitive (organic breadth beyond Life Science and energy; the conversion of the energy backlog) and that it was given before the October 2026 deterioration in the macro regime. For the model: the commentary supports the consensus path for H2 2026; it provides no information beyond that.

C-73

Other material or uncommon features of the guidance or investor relations

C50

Question asked What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

Judgement. Four features matter for the Consumer. First, the consensus EPS is a blend of two accounting bases, which makes FactSet's growth rates for 2026 meaningless and the P/E (23.2x on the dashboard) sensitive to which brokers contribute; this is uncommon and dangerous in a screening context. Second, the company's acquisition disclosure is frequent and ad hoc (a press release per deal with sales and sometimes margin, no multiple), so the acquired-growth line can be tracked in near-real time, which is unusual and helpful; the sector digest's point that consensus counts only completed deals means each announcement is an upgrade. Third, the quarterly numbers carry recurring small items the company presents transparently (earn-out remeasurements, restructuring costs, the SEK 125m write-down) and one large non-cash item it reports around (amortisation of acquired intangibles), with a definitional habit of quoting "underlying" margins. Fourth, the Capital Markets Day cadence is three years (2022, 2025), the head of investor relations is unchanged through the finance handover, and the company holds no short-term guidance and no quarterly outlook statement beyond tone.

The reasoning. None of these is a red flag; together they mean that the Consumer must build the model on one basis, track announced acquisitions as a leading indicator of the acquired line, and expect quarterly noise of 5 to 10% of EBITA that is not guided.

Sell-side estimates

C503 questions
C-6755C-6950C-7445

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The sell-side has moved from pessimism to cautious optimism in six months, and its forecasts now describe the company's own historical algorithm restored: 9 to 10% growth, a 14.5 to 15% EBITA margin, EPS growth of about 20% in 2026 on an easy base then 10 to 15%, free cash flow recovering, no dilution. The forecasts are credible in shape and in margin, slightly demanding on 2026 revenue and 2027 cash flow, and conservative only in the structural sense that unannounced deals are excluded. The company's record against those forecasts is good over five years, poor over three, and has just produced one strong beat; the estimates are no longer cut or depressed, and the share price has already rewarded the turn. For the Consumer, consensus is a reasonable base case for the operating model, with the downside concentrated in organic growth and working capital and the upside concentrated in deal announcements; the predictability of the forecasts over 18 months is middling until two more quarters confirm the Q2 run-rate.

C-67

The sell-side's forecasts for one to three years against the last one to five, line by line, and the credibility of the gap

C55

Question asked How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

Judgement. Consensus describes a return to the company's historical algorithm after a two-year interruption, with a strong 2026 on an easy base, and it is credible on margin, slightly demanding on 2026 revenue, and generous on 2027 cash flow. Line by line (FactSet median, 7 brokers for revenue and EBITDA, 5 to 6 for earnings):

  • Revenue: SEK 35,364m (+9.7%), 38,375m (+8.5%), 40,308m (+5.0%) for 2026 to 2028, against +13.0%, +24.4%, +17.8%, +2.2%, -1.0% for 2021 to 2025 (five-year average about 11%, last three years about 6%). The 2026 figure requires H2 2026 revenue of SEK 18.3bn, +13.9% on H2 2025, against Q2's +10.7%; with acquired growth at 6 to 7%, currency neutral and organic at 4 to 6%, H2 lands at +11 to 13%, so 2026 revenue has 1 to 2% of downside risk unless the krona weakens or deals close faster. The 2027 figure (+8.5%) is in line with the algorithm; the 2028 figure (+5%) is below it and reflects brokers not modelling unannounced deals, which is the sector-wide pattern and the source of the upgrade stream if deals continue.
  • Segments (2026): Life Science SEK 8,630m (+15%), Industrial & Engineering 8,896m (+11%), Process, Energy & Water 8,257m (+11%), Infrastructure & Construction 4,717m (-2%), Technology & Systems Solutions 4,839m (+6%). The shape is right; Life Science's +15% assumes Polish and other acquisitions plus continued double-digit organic growth.
  • EBITDA: SEK 6,204m, 6,829m, 7,151m (+12.5%, +10.1%, +4.7% on the brokers'-basis 2025 actual of 5,515m), margins 17.5%, 17.8%, 17.7% against 17.9%, 17.5%, 17.7%, 17.1%, 17.1% for 2021 to 2025. Credible: it returns the margin to the 2021 to 2023 range, no higher.
  • EBIT (brokers' basis, effectively EBITA since consensus depreciation and amortisation of SEK 1,075m excludes acquired-intangible amortisation): SEK 5,127m, 5,768m, 6,039m, margins 14.5%, 15.0%, 15.0%, against company EBITA margins of 14.7%, 15.0%, 14.4%, 14.1% underlying for 2021, 2023, 2024, 2025. Credible on the Q2 2026 evidence (14.9% underlying) and on mix; it implies H2 2026 EBITA of about SEK 2.7bn, +16% on the underlying H2 2025 base, which needs organic growth of 4% or more plus the 8% acquired contribution.
  • Pre-tax profit: SEK 3,999m, 4,700m, 5,157m (+20%, +18%, +10%), growing faster than EBIT because net interest falls (SEK 470m in 2025) and amortisation grows more slowly than EBITA. Tax implied at about 23.5%.
  • Net income and EPS: the net income median (SEK 3,060m, 3,587m, 3,960m) is on a reported basis and implies EPS of about 8.40, 9.85, 10.87 (+19%, +17%, +10%); the EPS median (10.01, 11.30, 12.17) blends adjusted-basis brokers in and must not be used as a series. On the adjusted basis DNB Carnegie has 10.40, 11.80, 12.80 (+14%, +8% in the out-years). Either way, 2026 is +18 to 24%, 2027 +10 to 17%, 2028 +8 to 10%.
  • Cash flow: operating cash flow SEK 4,212m, 4,979m, 5,600m; capex 518m, 563m, 598m (1.5% of sales); free cash flow 2,566m (0.8x net income), 4,046m (1.1x), 4,581m (1.2x). The 2026 figure correctly assumes a working-capital build; the 2027 jump of 58% assumes working capital normalises and is the line most likely to be too high.
  • Dividend: SEK 3.30, 3.80, 3.80, a 39 to 40% payout on reported net income.
  • Shares: flat at 364.3m; no issuance or buyback modelled, correctly.
  • Leverage: net debt rises modestly with deals; interest falls; consistent with policy.

The credibility of the gap. The gap between the next three years and the last two (growth of 9.7%, 8.5%, 5.0% against 2.2% and -1.0%; EBITA margin 14.5 to 15.0% against 13.8 to 14.4%) rests on three things the foundations support (deal pace restored to 7 to 8% of EBITA, operating leverage on a pre-built cost base, mix) and one they only partly support (organic growth of 3 to 5% sustained for three years against a decade average of 4% and a weakening macro). The gap between the next three years and the 2021 to 2023 boom (growth of 13 to 24%) is correctly not being closed. The consensus is therefore credible in shape, about 1 to 2% high on 2026 revenue, about right on 2026 to 2027 margin, high on 2027 free cash flow, and structurally low on 2028 because unannounced deals are excluded.

C-69

Record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over one, three and five years, including in comparable conditions

C50

Question asked What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

Judgement. Over five years: a strong record in the boom (2021 to 2023: beats, targets met, EPS +30%, +27%, +7%); over three years: a deteriorating one (2024 to Q1 2026: three consensus misses in four quarters, growth targets missed two years running, margin target missed on a reported basis in 2025); over one year: a mixed one that has just turned (Q3 2025 roughly in line with margin above target but growth below; Q4 2025 adjusted EBITA 14% below consensus on the UK write-down; Q1 2026 adjusted EBITA 5 to 6% and net profit 9 to 11% below; Q2 2026 EBITA 11 to 12% above with organic growth of 5% against 1.7% expected). In comparable conditions (a European industrial downturn), the 2024 to 2025 record is worse than the 2008 to 2009 precedent on growth (then sales fell 7.5% with margins held in double digits; now sales fell 1% with margins down 120 basis points on an avoidable cost overrun) and worse than Addtech's in the same quarters (flat organic sales with 12% EBITA growth).

The reasoning. The pattern for the Consumer is that the company beats when volume arrives and misses when it does not, because the cost base does not flex; the misses were concentrated in cost absorption and in one control failure, not in gross margin, cash or deal discipline. DNB Carnegie's credibility score (4.0 of 5.0) and the two brokers' "honest tracker" label refer to the targets, which were met on average over the cycle (14.6% margin), not to quarterly consensus, where the company's record since 2024 is roughly even. For the third set's predictability test: a single beat does not restore a record; two more quarters of delivery against consensus (Q3 2026 tomorrow, Q4 2026 in January) are the minimum evidence for treating consensus as reliable.

C-74

Have estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has guidance been rebased lower, kitchen-sinked or sandbagged

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

Judgement. No on all three counts, as of today. Estimates were cut deeply through 2025 (DNB Carnegie trimmed 2025 to 2026 adjusted EPS by 9 to 11% after Q1 2025 and cut its target from SEK 300 to 235 over twelve months; the FY2026 consensus mean ranged from 10.83 down to 8.81 over the last twelve months and the FY2027 from 11.88 to 9.90) and troughed around April 2026 after the Q1 miss; since the Q2 print they have been rebuilt (DNB Carnegie +5 to 6% for 2026 to 2028, Redeye +8 to 9%, consensus 2027 EPS +6% in six months, 2028 +25% in twelve months and now at its twelve-month high, target prices +11% in six months, buy share up 30 points to 70%, mean target SEK 285 against a price of 262 at refresh). Guidance has not been rebased because there is none; the targets were held through the downturn, which is the opposite of kitchen-sinking, and the write-down was paired with reaffirmed targets.

The reasoning. For the investment case this is a less favourable setup than it was six months ago: the estimate base is no longer depressed, the share price is at a 52-week high, sell-side momentum is rated A and the dispersion has narrowed to a Hold-Buy split (Pareto, DNB Carnegie, Goldman Sachs Hold at SEK 260 to 265; Nordea and two restricted brokers Buy at 290 to 322). The remaining conservatism is structural rather than cyclical: brokers do not model unannounced acquisitions (worth roughly 2 to 3% of EBITA growth a year if the deal pace holds) and 2028 is modelled at +5% revenue. The Consumer should treat consensus as fairly rebuilt, with the upgrade stream dependent on deal announcements and on Q3 and Q4 2026 confirming the organic recovery, and should not look for a kitchen-sink reset in the numbers.

Stock market narrative

C501 question
C-2250

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The narrative ahead is a sequence of confirmations, dated and well founded in the next six months, cyclical and less certain in the following year, and dependent on a Capital Markets Day the company has not yet announced beyond that. There is no novel positive development in the files that the market does not already know, and the company's communication style ensures there will be none between reports. For the Consumer's purposes this means the share price over 18 months will be driven by delivery against consensus and by the cohort's multiple, not by narrative events, and that the operating and financial model should be built to be judged quarter by quarter rather than against a story.

C-22

The staircase of novel positive developments: within 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; novelty and foundation

C50

Question asked What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

Judgement. The staircase is real but low-rise: every step is a confirmation of a known model rather than a novel development, and the market already knows the shape. Within six months: the Q3 2026 report (23 October 2026, tomorrow) as the first test of whether Q2's organic rate and operating leverage repeat against a -1% comparison; the full-year 2026 report in late January 2027 with a dividend proposal (consensus SEK 3.30) and the count of 2026 acquisitions (Redeye's 18 would be a record); Anna Vilogorac in post by 14 January 2027, closing the finance handover; completion of Euromed in Poland after competition approval; a possible S&P upgrade to BBB from BBB- on the positive outlook; and the Q1 2027 report in April against a 0% comparison. Novelty low; foundation strong, because the dates are fixed and the comparisons easy. In months 6 to 18: full-year 2027 delivery of the algorithm with an EBITA margin at or above 15% for the first time since 2023, return on capital employed back toward 20%, organic growth broadening into Infrastructure & Construction (building recovery deferred into 2027) and Technology & Systems Solutions (UK project companies fixed), acquired sales running at SEK 2.5bn or more a year with a deal count above 20, and the Q2 2027 print lapping the strong Q2 2026 base (a negative step the narrative will have to absorb). Novelty low to moderate; foundation moderate, dependent on the cycle. In months 18 to 36: the next Capital Markets Day on the three-year cadence (2028), where the only plausible novelties are a lifted margin floor (to 15%) if the mix has moved, a formal organic initiative, or a statement on the proprietary share; Life Science approaching SEK 10bn of sales; progress toward the doubling; and, if the deal pace holds, the compounding of unannounced acquisitions into consensus upgrades. Novelty moderate at the Capital Markets Day only; foundation weak beyond 18 months because it rests on the cycle and on management choices it has so far declined to make.

The reasoning. What the staircase lacks is any step that would change the category the market puts the stock in: there is no product cycle, no divestment, no buyback, no guidance to raise, no new end market large enough to re-rate the group, and management has chosen not to supply a margin-journey narrative of the Lagercrantz kind. The sector digest's framing is the one the market uses: Indutrade is a "mature engine that needs the re-rating", and re-ratings are what the current regime refuses. The narrative upside is therefore the steady accumulation of delivered quarters and announced deals, which closes the 15 to 20% discount slowly if at all; the narrative downside is a single disappointing quarter against a share price that has already risen 40% from its 2025 low.

Research requests for the next set

Company: Indutrade AB (INDT-SE), a Swedish decentralised industrial group that acquires and owns more than 220 technical-trading and niche-manufacturing companies across more than 30 countries, reporting in five business areas: Industrial & Engineering, Life Science, Process Energy & Water, Infrastructure & Construction, and Technology & Systems Solutions

Request: T-P1

Peers: Lifco (LIFCO.B-SE, the cohort's margin leader at about 22 to 23% EBITA, same decentralised acquisition model); Addtech (ADDT.B-SE, technical trading, delivered 12% EBITA growth on flat organic sales in its Q1 2026/27 and runs a restructuring precedent in its Automation area); Lagercrantz (LAGR.B-SE, proprietary-product mix shift to 80% and a 20% margin target); Diploma (DPLM-GB, value-add distributor with sector-high margins and a stated opex reinvestment drag); OEM International (OEM.B-SE, centralised technical trader with shared logistics, the contrasting cost model); Bufab (BUFAB-SE, trading-heavy peer with value-based pricing); Beijer Ref (BEIJ.B-SE, regional warehouse centralisation precedent); and the private, family-owned technical traders and small Nordic serial-acquirer platforms (InstallatørGruppen, Tången, Auroora) that compete for the same targets Inputs: What is the fixed and variable split of Indutrade's cost base below gross profit (selling, administrative and other operating expenses spread across more than 220 subsidiaries, per the 2025 annual report), and how did it behave in 2024 to 2025 when organic sales fell 1 to 2% and the reported EBITA margin fell from 15.0% in 2023 to 13.8% in 2025, against Q2 2026 when organic sales grew 5% and the company reported SG&A growth of 8% against sales growth of 11% (Q2 2026 interim report, 16 July 2026)? How much of the 2023 to 2025 SG&A build was capacity for growth that remains under-absorbed, and over how many quarters of organic growth does it absorb? In past up-cycles (2010 to 2011, 2021 to 2023) and down-cycles (2008 to 2009, 2024 to 2025), what was the incremental EBITA margin on organic sales changes, by business area where available, and how did it compare at Lifco, Addtech and Lagercrantz? What cost actions has Indutrade actually taken (the company said about 200 employees were removed from demand-challenged entities over the twelve months to April 2026; divestments of Scanmaskin, Plooijer Zaandam, Novisol and Modernum in 2024 to 2025; management changes at two UK Technology & Systems Solutions companies after SEK 125m of project write-downs in Q4 2025), what did each deliver, and which cost lines in a decentralised technical trader prove cuttable and which do not, given the chief executive's stated reluctance to cut lean trading companies? What do Addtech's Automation restructuring (unprofitable sites closed, a SEK 10m one-off in Q2 2025/26, margin recovered by Q3) and Beijer Ref's warehouse centralisation (SEK 100m targeted savings) show about the achievable size and speed of cost reduction in this business model? How much operating leverage comes from acquired companies entering at higher margins than the group (acquired companies reported at 19.5% and 17.6% EBITA margins in Q1 and Q2 2026 against group margins of 13.3% and 14.7%) and how durable are those entry margins after one to three years in the group? Focus: Whether the Q2 2026 return of operating leverage (organic EBITA growth of about 12% on organic sales growth of 5%) is repeatable across 2026 to 2028 at the organic growth rates the sell-side currently forecasts, what incremental margin a model should assume on the way up and on the way down, and whether any cost reduction beyond ordinary subsidiary-level discipline is feasible or intended in this model.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Lifco (LIFCO.B-SE, which former executives say raises prices 3 to 7% in the first year after acquisition and holds about 43% gross margins); Addtech (ADDT.B-SE, gross margin up 330 basis points from 2022 to 2025 on value-add pricing and mix); Lagercrantz (LAGR.B-SE, gross margin above 39% on 80% proprietary products, with a reported 20 basis point gross margin fall in Q1 2026/27 on raw-material inflation); Diploma (DPLM-GB, cost-of-failure pricing with both contract and spot components); Bufab (BUFAB-SE, gross margin 33.2% in Q2 2026 on value-based pricing) and Bossard (BOSN-CH, 34.4% in H1 2026); RS Group (RS1-GB) and Rexel (RXL-FR) as the generalist distributors whose pricing window is shorter; and the private family-owned technical traders and the international component principals (for example SKF, Danfoss, Bosch) whose direct-sales decisions bound the trading subsidiaries' pricing Operating conditions: a volume downturn with input-cost inflation (2022 to 2023 and 2024 to 2025); a volume recovery with normalised supply and returning customer bargaining power (2026); a strong krona that lowers landed import costs for trading subsidiaries selling in Swedish kronor; an energy-led raw-material shock to plastics, metals and freight; a prolonged European construction and project recession; and a period of digital price transparency or AI-assisted procurement in standard components Inputs: What explains the rise in Indutrade's company-basis gross margin from 34.6% in 2023 to 35.4% in 2025 and 35.6% in Q2 2026 while organic sales fell and then recovered (Indutrade annual report 2025 and Q2 2026 interim report), and how much of it is price, mix between technical trading (about 58 to 60% of sales per the November 2025 Capital Markets Day) and proprietary products, acquisitions, and krona-driven purchasing gains? What realised price increases have Indutrade subsidiaries achieved and held in 2022 to 2026, by business area, and where has price been given back? What are the typical contract structures in technical trading and niche manufacturing (one-year fixed-price agreements in industrial components, currency clauses, framework contracts of five to ten years in technical service, indexation and its lags), and how quickly did subsidiaries pass through freight, metals, semiconductor and component increases in 2021 to 2023 and again in 2025 to 2026? What do customers, competitors and channel experts say now about distributors' ability to pass through cost inflation, given a Q3 2026 automation-channel expert view that suppliers were recovering only 2 to 3% of 3 to 4% cost inflation as availability normalised? How does customer switching cost differ between specified, engineered or regulated components (Life Science, flow technology) and catalogue-type electronics and automation components (Technology & Systems Solutions), and how has gross margin behaved in each? Does the decentralised model (no central pricing programme, subsidiary managing directors "protecting the gross margin") deliver more or less pricing power across conditions than Lifco's mandated post-acquisition price rises, and what does that imply for the group margin ceiling? Focus: Whether Indutrade's gross margin of about 35.5% is a defensible floor through a renewed downturn or an input-cost shock, how much price can contribute to organic growth and margin in 2026 to 2028 across the named conditions, and where in the portfolio pricing power is weakest.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The company's growth algorithm is a set of loops (segment leaders generating deal flow, acquisitions at 6 to 8x funded from cash, operating leverage on a pre-built cost base, and a mix shift toward proprietary and Life Science businesses) whose strength or exhaustion will determine whether EBITA compounds at its historical rate or stalls as it did in 2024 to 2025, and the sell-side models the loops only as far as announced deals. Peers: Lifco (LIFCO.B-SE, the longest-running version of the same acquisition flywheel, with the 2:1 acquired-to-organic growth dependency its brokers note); Addtech (ADDT.B-SE, eleven quarters of flat organic growth alongside five years of margin improvement, and a plan to lift deal pace to 15 to 20 a year); Lagercrantz (LAGR.B-SE, proprietary mix shift as a margin loop); Diploma (DPLM-GB, acquisition-led growth with a stated reinvestment drag); OEM International (OEM.B-SE, a newly activated acquisition engine under EQT); Storskogen and Bergman & Beving as the precedents of a decentralised or debt-funded roll-up whose loops reversed; and private-equity-backed platforms and new listed Nordic acquirers as the competing bidders in the same loop Inputs: The deal-sourcing loop: does delegating acquisition sourcing to 35 segment leaders (each targeting at least one deal a year, with a mapped network the company says covers up to 1,500 candidates, per the November 2025 Capital Markets Day) create more proprietary deal flow and better pricing over time, or does the added structure erode the permanent-home autonomy that attracts sellers, as a former Lifco acquisition head argued in a December 2025 expert call? The arithmetic loop: how far can self-funded acquisitions at 5 to 8x EBITA, with about 0.4x sales of earn-outs (nine H1 2026 deals, SEK 1.5bn of annual sales, contingent consideration SEK 272m initially and SEK 436m maximum, per the Q2 2026 report), keep compounding EBITA at the historical 7 to 8% a year as the group grows past SEK 35bn of sales, and at what deal size or multiple does the loop become balancing (DNB Carnegie records Indutrade's 2023 to 2025 median acquisition EV/EBITA at 8.6x against 6.6 to 6.8x for Addtech, Lagercrantz and Lifco)? The operating-leverage loop: does organic growth restore margin, which funds more deals, which add higher-margin businesses, or does working capital absorb the cash (the company reported total operating working capital rising sequentially in Q2 2026 on higher trade receivables, and said in 2025 that further inventory releases would be marginal) and the earn-outs defer the cost? The mix loop: does the rising share of Life Science and proprietary products lift the group margin mechanically year by year, and has management's decision not to raise the 14% margin target (to keep buying lower-margin, high-return trading businesses) capped it? The balancing loops: scale (each deal matters less), multiple inflation from new acquirers, organisational complexity and control failures (the UK write-downs), and currency translation on an unhedged base of about SEK 17.6bn of foreign net investments. For each loop, the evidence it is running now, the precedent at peers, and what would stall or reverse it. Focus: Whether the deal-sourcing and operating-leverage loops that showed in H1 2026 are self-reinforcing over 2026 to 2029 or are already being offset by the balancing loops of scale, acquisition pricing and complexity, and which observable indicators would show the balance tipping.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The published EPS consensus blends a reported basis and an adjusted basis that differ by the amortisation of acquired intangibles, and the EPS path over 18 months to four years depends on several below-EBITA inputs (amortisation, interest, earn-out remeasurements, one-off project costs, tax, currency translation and a share count that has not moved) that the company does not guide and that the sell-side treats inconsistently. Peers: Lifco (LIFCO.B-SE, which reports EBITA excluding about SEK 1.1bn of acquisition-related amortisation and carries large minority put options); Addtech (ADDT.B-SE, earn-out revaluations in operating profit, a 1 percentage point rate move worth SEK 57m after tax); Lagercrantz (LAGR.B-SE, floating-rate unhedged debt, contingent consideration and minority put liabilities); Diploma (DPLM-GB, a GBP 43.8m bridge between statutory and adjusted operating profit in H1 2026 and Day 1 inclusion of acquisitions in organic growth); Bravida, Beijer Ref and Bufab as Swedish companies that leave currency translation unhedged Inputs: Amortisation of acquired intangibles: Indutrade reported EBITA of SEK 4,450m in 2025 against FactSet adjusted EBIT of SEK 3,774m; how does the amortisation charge grow with the deal pace, what is its typical life, and which brokers add it back in their EPS? Net interest: SEK 470m in 2025 against SEK 592m in 2024 (FactSet), with a SEK 1.3bn five-year bond at STIBOR plus 1.13% issued in Q4 2025 and a company-stated sensitivity of about SEK 61m per 1 percentage point of rates; how does the interest line move with Riksbank and ECB policy and with the acquisition funding mix? Earn-outs and one-offs: the frequency and size of contingent-consideration remeasurements (positive SEK 27m in Q1 2025, negative SEK 14m in Q2 2026) and of project write-downs (SEK 125m in two UK companies in Q4 2025), and how peers treat them in adjusted earnings. Tax: a reported rate of 22 to 24% and the company's statement that OECD Pillar Two has a negligible effect; what drives the rate across the group's 30 countries? Currency: the company's stated sensitivity of SEK 243m of net sales and SEK 18m of net profit per 1% move in the krona, with translation unhedged by policy; what are the consensus krona forecasts (EUR/SEK toward 10.40 to 10.50 by end-2026 according to brokers cited in the research) and what did translation do to reported EBITA in 2023 (positive), 2025 (negative 2%) and Q1 2026 (negative 3%)? Share count and distributions: 364.3m shares unchanged for four years, no buybacks ever, a progressive dividend of SEK 3.10 for 2025 inside a 30 to 50% payout policy; is any change plausible? Working capital and earn-out cash: how much cash do lease repayments (SEK 570m in 2025), earn-out settlements and working-capital growth take below the operating cash flow line, and what free cash flow is available for deals at the current pace? For each input, the company's own record, management's statements, the sell-side's assumptions by broker and basis, and what would move it materially. Focus: A reconciled bridge from EBITA to reported and adjusted EPS and to free cash flow for 2026 to 2029, stating which basis each named broker uses, so that the EPS growth rate can be modelled on one consistent basis and the below-EBITA items can be forecast rather than inherited from a blended consensus.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 12,420 words · written 23 October 2026, 01:52 to 02:11 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Indutrade (INDT-SE): second set of questions, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Investment Analyst, written 2026-10-22, before the Q3 2026 report due 2026-10-23. Scores: 100 most positive for the financial forecasts in an investment case, 1 most negative, 50 neutral.

Reader's note: the conventions and the corrections carried into this set

  1. Bases. Every margin and profit figure below is on the company's EBITA basis (before amortisation of acquired intangibles) unless labelled otherwise. "EBITDA" means the brokers' basis in the financials block (FY2025 SEK 5,515m, 17.1%), which is EBITA plus depreciation of property, equipment, right-of-use assets and non-acquired intangibles (about SEK 1,065m in 2025, 3.3% of sales). "Reported EPS" is basic EPS after amortisation of acquired intangibles (FY2025 SEK 7.03); "adjusted EPS" adds that amortisation back in full, with no tax effect, which is how DNB Carnegie (FY2026 SEK 10.40) and Nordea (10.30) compute it; FY2025 adjusted EPS on that basis is SEK 8.83 (net income 2,560m plus amortisation 656m, over 364.3m shares). The first set's figure of SEK 8.4 to 8.5 for adjusted FY2025 EPS assumed a tax shield on the add-back; the brokers do not apply one, and nor do I from here.
  2. A correction to the first set on cash conversion. The FactSet "free cash flow" line in the financials block (FY2025 SEK 2,397m, "94% of net income") is operating cash flow less capital expenditure less cash dividends: 3,990 less 502 less 1,091 equals 2,397, and the same identity holds for 2023 and 2024. The first set read it as pre-dividend free cash flow and concluded that conversion was about 94% and would fall to 80 to 90%. The pre-dividend figure is SEK 3,488m, 136% of reported net income, or 114% after lease repayments of SEK 565m. The company's own "cash conversion above 130%" is therefore not flattered by a definitional trick; it reflects SEK 1.7bn of non-cash depreciation and amortisation on a SEK 2.6bn net income. The consequence for the model is that free cash flow after leases runs at roughly 95 to 110% of reported net income across the forecast years, not 80 to 90%, and that the acquisition programme is comfortably self-funded. This changes the balance-sheet path materially and I return to it in P-02 and P-08.
  3. A change in the currency facts since the first set. The first set carried the consensus krona forecast (EUR/SEK toward 10.40 to 10.50 by end-2026) as a 2 to 3% drag on reported growth. ING (12 October 2026) puts spot EUR/SEK at 11.20 after a "relief rally" from higher levels, and DNB Carnegie (5 October 2026) describes the weakening krona as a tailwind to its estimates. The translation effect on H2 2026 and early 2027 reported figures is therefore neutral to mildly positive, not negative. I model currency at zero and treat it as the largest unmodelled swing (about SEK 18m of net profit and SEK 243m of sales per 1% move, unhedged).
  4. A change in the rates facts. The first set and the brokers (DNB Carnegie's net financial items falling to SEK -330m in 2027 and -210m in 2028) assumed falling policy rates. The October 2026 regime (US ten-year about 5.1%, a Riksbank hike likely in November, markets pricing up to three ECB rises by mid-2027) reverses that. I model net financial items rising modestly in absolute terms and falling only as a share of EBITA.
  5. Quarterly base I reconstruct for the phasing. EBITA by quarter: Q1 2025 about SEK 1,097m (13.6%, including SEK 27m of positive earn-out remeasurement), Q2 2025 1,113m (13.7%), Q3 2025 about 1,146m (14.6%), Q4 2025 1,094m reported (13.3%; 14.9% underlying before the SEK 125m UK write-down), Q1 2026 1,070m (13.3%), Q2 2026 1,322m (14.7%; 14.9% underlying). H1 2026: sales SEK 17,051m, EBITA 2,392m (14.0%). H2 2025: sales 16,072m, EBITA 2,240m reported, 2,365m underlying. Consensus FY2026 EBIT of SEK 5,127m (the brokers' line is effectively EBITA) therefore requires H2 2026 EBITA of about 2,735m, up 16% on the underlying H2 2025 base, on H2 revenue of 18,313m (+13.9%).

Financial forecasts

C579 questions
P-0162P-0258P-0348P-0455P-0557P-0650P-0852P-0958P-1058

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months to four years describe a company whose two engines have restarted after a two-year stall, running at the cohort's average pace with the cohort's lowest margin and a balance sheet that funds everything without strain. The mechanisms are clear enough to write as rules: EBITA margin moves one-third of a point for each point of organic growth above or below 2%; acquisitions add 6 to 7% of sales a year at 16 to 18% margins for SEK 2.3 to 3.0bn of cash; mix adds 10 to 20 basis points of margin a year; the below-EBITA lines take about a point off EPS growth; free cash flow after leases is about 100% of reported net income and equals the dividend plus the deal programme. The outcome is revenue growth of about 9% a year, an EBITA margin of 15.0% in 2027 and 15.4% by 2030, reported EPS of SEK 8.30 in 2026 rising to 12.71 in 2030 (adjusted 10.28 to 15.46), and leverage falling to 1.0x. Against consensus the forecasts sit 1 to 2% below on 2026 to 2027 earnings (lower H2 2026 revenue, a higher interest line under the October rates regime) and 3 to 5% above from 2028 (unannounced deals). The two facts most likely to break the forecasts are a volume relapse in 2027, which the cost structure cannot offset and which the macro regime makes a one-in-four event, and the price paid for acquisitions, which does not reduce growth but holds returns below target and below peers. The two facts most likely to beat them are a deal pace that uses the balance sheet's spare SEK 6bn, and a krona that stays weak. For an investment case, the forecasts are moderately positive: they support double-digit earnings compounding from an undemanding 2025 base, they rest on strengths the company demonstrably has rather than on strengths it would need to acquire, and they do not require any decision management has refused to take; they do not support a re-rating to the peers' margin tier, and the 18-month path is a sequence of quarterly tests against easy and then hard comparisons rather than a guided glide.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C62

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement.
