Operating leverage is the single largest driver of the difference between the next four years and the last four, and it is a two-way, asymmetric mechanism that should be modelled with an explicit rule rather than a margin assumption. The rule the evidence supports: the group EBITA margin moves by about one-third of a point for each point of organic sales growth above or below a break-even rate of roughly 2%, before mix and acquisitions. On that rule, organic growth of 4 to 5% through Q1 2027 lifts the margin by 70 to 100 basis points year on year, organic growth of 3 to 3.5% in 2027 to 2028 adds 30 to 50 basis points a year, and a relapse to 0% organic growth takes 60 to 70 basis points off within two quarters. The leverage was negative for the whole of 2024 to Q1 2026 and turned positive in Q2 2026; it will be the main reason the EBITA margin moves from 14.1% underlying in 2025 to about 14.5% in 2026 and 15.0% in 2027, and the main reason EBITA grows faster than sales in every year of the forecast in which organic growth is above 2%.

Why the structure produces this rule. The cost base below gross profit is about SEK 7.0bn net (selling costs SEK 5,113m, administrative costs 2,127m, other operating expenses 409m, less other operating income, in 2025), 21.6% of sales, and it is overwhelmingly people and premises in 220 companies: employee benefit expenses were SEK 7,745m in 2025 against 7,496m in 2024, a rise of 3.3% in a year in which organic sales fell 2%. That is the signature of a cost base that grows at wage inflation regardless of volume and flexes only slowly and locally. The gross margin on the company's basis is 35.4 to 36.0% and has been flat to rising for three years, so an incremental krona of organic sales carries about 35.5 öre of gross profit against perhaps 10 öre of genuinely variable selling and logistics cost. The arithmetic is then simple: with SG&A growing 3 to 4% a year at zero volume, the group needs about 2% of organic growth to hold its margin, and each point above that adds roughly 0.33 points of margin (0.355 incremental gross margin less about 0.03 of variable cost, times a 0.9 to 1.0 pass-through, on a cost base that does not move within the year). Two checks confirm the rule: Q2 2026 (organic +5%, margin +100 basis points: 3 points above break-even times 0.33 equals 1.0) and 2024 to 2025 taken together (organic about -2%, margin -120 basis points over two years, against 4 points below break-even times 0.33 equals 1.3). The AlphaSense operating-leverage report's "40 to 60% incremental EBITA margins in up-cycles" is the same arithmetic stated on the incremental organic krona (35 öre of gross profit less a few öre of variable cost, before any cost growth), and is the figure to use for a single quarter; the one-third-of-a-point rule is the figure to use for a year, because it nets the cost inflation that continues underneath.

Converted to EBITA growth, the leverage factor. At a 14.5% margin, organic EBITA growth is approximately organic sales growth plus the margin change divided by 0.145. That gives: organic sales +5%, organic EBITA +12% (factor 2.4x, exactly Q2 2026); +4%, EBITA +8.6% (2.1x); +3%, +5.3% (1.8x); +2%, +2% (1.0x); 0%, -4.6%; -3%, -14%. The first set's "2 to 2.5x on the way up while the base is under-absorbed, 1.5x thereafter" is consistent with this and I keep it, with the refinement that the factor is not a constant but falls as organic growth normalises toward 3%, which is why 2028 to 2030 EBITA growth in my base case is only about one point above sales growth from leverage.

Why the leverage is asymmetric in time. On the way up it arrives within the quarter, because the cost base was pre-built: the 2023 to 2025 SG&A build (net operating costs below gross profit rose from about 19.6% of sales in 2023, the 34.6% gross margin less the 15.0% EBITA margin, to about 21.6% in 2025, 35.4% less 13.8%; selling and administrative costs alone were 22.5% of sales in 2025) was capacity for volume that is only now arriving, management says more than half the companies are growing and it is adding heads selectively in energy and Life Science, and segment leaders carry 6 to 8 companies against capacity for 10 to 15. On the way down it arrives with a two-to-four-quarter lag, because the chief executive will not cut lean trading companies indiscriminately, and the demonstrated cuttability is about 200 employees in a year (2% of the workforce). So the margin cone is wider on the downside over any twelve-month window than the symmetric rule implies: a relapse to 0% organic growth in 2027 would take the 2027 margin to about 14.3% rather than 15.0%, and a -3% year to about 13.3%, which is the Q1 2026 level and the floor the 2024 to 2025 downturn established.

How it shapes each line item, against the last four to six years.

  • Revenue: no direct effect; leverage acts on the margin. The relevant comparison is that organic growth averaged about 4% over 2019 to 2025 but was -2%, 0%, 0% and +5% in the last four reported periods; the forecast assumes 4.5% in H2 2026, 3.5% in 2027 and 3% thereafter.
  • Gross profit: flat margin at 35.5% on the company's basis (33.3 to 33.5% on FactSet's); leverage is below this line.
  • EBITA: the margin path 14.1% underlying (2025), about 14.5% (2026), 15.0% (2027), 15.2% (2028), 15.3 to 15.4% (2029 to 2030), of which leverage contributes about 40 basis points in 2026 (H2 only), 40 in 2027, 20 in 2028 and 10 thereafter, with mix (Life Science share, acquired companies at 16 to 19%) contributing 10 to 20 basis points a year and the Infrastructure & Construction and Technology & Systems Solutions recoveries the rest. Against 2021 to 2023 (14.7%, 14.4%, 15.0%) this is a return to the range, not a step above it; against 2024 to 2025 (14.4%, 13.8% reported) it is a 100 to 150 basis point recovery, roughly half of it leverage.
  • EBITDA: the same path plus about 3.1 points of depreciation (the gap narrows slightly as leases and equipment grow more slowly than sales): 17.7% (2026), 18.1% (2027), 18.3 to 18.5% thereafter, against 17.1 to 17.9% in 2021 to 2025.
  • EPS: leverage is worth about 3 to 4 points of EPS growth in 2026 and 2027 on top of the acquired and organic volume contribution; this is why 2026 and 2027 EPS growth (reported basis about +18% and +14%) exceeds the 10 to 12% algorithm, and why EPS growth reverts to about 10% from 2028 once leverage has done its work.
  • Free cash flow: leverage converts at close to 100% because the incremental cost base is already paid for; the offset is working capital on the incremental organic sales (about 26 öre per krona).

Peers, and why Indutrade's leverage is larger than theirs. Addtech lifted its EBITA margin for a fifth consecutive year to 16.0% on 2% organic growth and Lifco holds 22 to 23% through flat quarters, because both have higher gross margins (Lifco about 43%), active pricing programmes and a proprietary mix that runs 60 to 80%; their margins depend less on volume. Indutrade's lower gross margin and larger trading share make it the most volume-geared of the four proven compounders, which is a disadvantage in a downturn (2024 to 2025 showed it) and the reason it has the most to gain from the current turn. OEM International's centralised logistics model gives it a structurally different, larger operating leverage (EBITA margin 13.2% to 16.5% in five quarters), which Indutrade cannot copy without abandoning its model.

What would break the rule. Three things: SG&A growth re-accelerating above 4% as subsidiaries hire into the recovery (watch the SG&A-to-sales ratio in Q3 and Q4 2026 against 22.5% in 2025); gross margin slipping as customer bargaining power returns (the Q3 2026 automation-channel expert's 2 to 3% price recovery against 3 to 4% cost inflation); and a volume relapse, which the October 2026 rates regime makes the most probable of the three. The Q3 2026 print tomorrow is the first test: an EBITA margin of 15.0% or better on organic growth of 3% or better would confirm the rule is running; a margin below 14.6% (the Q3 2025 base) on positive organic growth would say the cost base is growing into the recovery.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years

C58

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Financial leverage is low, self-correcting and a small negative for EPS over the next 18 months rather than the small positive the brokers assume, because rates are now rising and net debt rises with the deal pace. Over four years it is close to neutral: the acquisition programme at SEK 2.3 to 3.0bn a year is fully funded from free cash flow after dividends, so net debt stays roughly flat at SEK 8.3 to 9.2bn (FactSet basis, including leases and earn-outs) while EBITDA grows from SEK 5.5bn to about 9bn, taking leverage from 1.65x at June 2026 to about 1.0x by 2030 without any policy change. The balance sheet is therefore not a constraint, not a source of EPS acceleration, and not a risk; the only way it becomes any of those is if management chooses to deploy the SEK 6bn of unused capacity into a faster deal pace, which is the upside case, not the base case.

The arithmetic of the interest line. Net financial items were SEK -437m in 2025 (company basis; FactSet net interest -470m), down from -506m, on average net financial debt of about SEK 7 to 8bn and lease liabilities of SEK 1.55bn. The company's sensitivity is SEK 61m of pre-tax cost per percentage point of rates (SEK 48m after tax, SEK 0.13 of EPS), which says that roughly SEK 6bn of the debt is floating. From here, two forces push the line up and one pushes it down. Up: net debt is about SEK 2bn higher than the 2025 average after the H1 2026 deals (SEK 9.5bn at June 2026), worth about SEK 70m a year at 3.5%; and the Riksbank and ECB are leaning toward hikes, with 50 to 75 basis points over the next 12 to 18 months plausible, worth SEK 30 to 45m. Down: the SEK 1.3bn five-year bond at STIBOR plus 113 basis points (November 2025) replaced more expensive bank debt, and the positive S&P outlook lowers the marginal spread. Netting these, I model net financial items of about SEK -410m in 2026, -450m in 2027, -465m in 2028, -480m in 2029 and -495m in 2030: rising 2 to 3% a year in absolute terms, falling from 9.8% of EBITA in 2025 to 8.1% in 2026 and 6.6% in 2030. Consensus implies about -430m for 2026 (EBIT 5,127m less pre-tax 3,999m equals 1,128m, of which about 700m is amortisation) and about -310m for 2027; DNB Carnegie's published path is -395m, -330m, -210m. My 2027 figure is therefore about SEK 140m worse than consensus at the pre-tax line, worth SEK 0.30 of EPS, or 2.5 to 3%. That is the most concrete place where I lean the 18-month forecasts below the sell-side.

The amplification of EPS. With pre-tax profit of about SEK 4.0bn in 2026, a SEK 61m interest swing is 1.5%; the volume and margin sensitivities (a 2 point organic swing is 3% of EPS, 50 basis points of margin is 3.3%) are each twice as large. Financial leverage amplifies EPS by well under 10% of the operating variance, which is the reason it deserves a line in the model but not a scenario. Against the last six years, the interest line was a material drag only in 2023 to 2024 (net interest rising from SEK 117m in 2021 to 592m in 2024 as rates rose on a debt base built by the 2022 to 2023 acquisitions); that episode cost about 2 points of EPS growth a year for two years. The next four years carry nothing of that size: the debt base is not growing and the rate cycle, even on the hiking path, is a fraction of 2022 to 2024.

The balance sheet path, built from the cash flow. Free cash flow after capital expenditure and lease payments (my definition for the cross-check, before acquisitions, earn-out settlements and dividends) is about SEK 2.9bn in 2026, 3.5bn in 2027, 4.0bn in 2028, 4.4bn in 2029 and 4.9bn in 2030. Dividends paid are SEK 1.13bn in 2026 rising to about 1.7bn by 2030 at a 40% payout. Acquisition cash (initial consideration plus earn-out settlements, less disposals, which are nil) is about SEK 2.3bn in 2026 (SEK 1.63bn in H1 on FactSet's line), 2.4bn in 2027, 2.6bn in 2028, 2.8bn in 2029 and 3.0bn in 2030. The sum is approximately zero in every year from 2027: the model is self-funding at a deal pace that adds 6 to 7% of sales a year. Net debt (FactSet basis) ends 2026 at about SEK 8.3bn (H2 is the cash-generative half and the dividend was paid in May), then drifts to SEK 8.5 to 9.2bn as leases grow, which is 1.35x EBITDA at end-2026, about 1.25x at end-2027 and about 1.0x by 2030. Financial net debt excluding leases and earn-outs stays near 0.9 to 1.1x, and net debt to equity falls from 45% toward 30%. S&P's positive outlook on BBB- is more likely than not to become BBB within 18 months on these numbers, which would trim the marginal spread by 10 to 20 basis points.

What financial leverage could do that the base case does not assume. DNB Carnegie calculates headroom to fund SEK 3bn of acquired revenue a year inside the net-debt-to-equity ceiling; Redeye models 18 deals in 2026 and 18 to 24 a year by 2033 with leverage still falling to 0.8x. The balance sheet would support a deal pace 50% above my base case (SEK 3.5bn of cash a year) at leverage rising only to about 1.8x, which would add 2 to 3 points of EBITA growth a year. Management's constraint is organisational, not financial. On the other side, a buyback is implausible (none in the company's history; the chief executive prefers deals at 6 to 8x to shares at 23x), and a dividend cut is implausible short of a 2009-type recession. The hidden leverage is small: contingent consideration on the H1 2026 deals is SEK 272m recognised and SEK 436m maximum, lease liabilities about SEK 1.6bn, no minority put options of the Lifco kind (SEK 3bn there). Pension deficits are SEK 305m.

Against peers. Diploma at 1.0x, Addtech and Lifco at 1.1 to 1.5x, Lagercrantz at 1.8 to 2.4x: Indutrade sits in the middle and has the largest absolute headroom. Addtech's rate sensitivity (SEK 57m after tax per point) and Lagercrantz's (SEK 40m pre-tax) are the same order as Indutrade's, so the cohort's EPS will move together on rates, which matters for the relative case: none of the four will see a differentiated interest tailwind.

For the model. Net financial items as above; no buyback; no issuance; share count 364.3m throughout; dividend per share SEK 3.40 for 2026 rising about 10% a year; leverage falling. The one scenario worth running is the rates path: each 100 basis points on policy rates is SEK 0.13 of EPS and nothing on the balance sheet.

P-03

Cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the main line items, and when

❌ D48

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement. Very little, and almost all of it has already arrived. There is no cost programme, the model forbids a central one, and the three cost actions of the last 18 months (about 200 heads removed from demand-challenged units, the pruning of four businesses with about SEK 200m of dilutive revenue, and the termination of the UK project managers) together explain perhaps 40 to 50 basis points of the group margin recovery, most of it already in the Q2 2026 run-rate. What remains to reach the line items over the next 18 months to four years is of three kinds: the roll-off of the UK project losses in Technology & Systems Solutions (worth 10 to 20 basis points of group margin by 2027 as that area recovers from 13.7% toward 15%); the Infrastructure & Construction restructuring compounding with a cyclical recovery (segment margin from 12.2% toward 13 to 14% by 2028, worth 10 to 15 basis points of group); and the continuing, unquantifiable discipline of 220 managing directors paid on local EBITA, which shows up as the SG&A-to-sales ratio drifting from 22.5% in 2025 back toward 21.5 to 22% by 2028 as volume returns. The efficiency story at Indutrade is therefore a story about absorbing a cost base, not eliminating one; the Consumer should model no cost-out line and should treat any announced programme as a change of model, which would be news.

Why the scope is small by design, and why that is the right design. The cost base is 220 local income statements. Central costs are 24 people. There is no shared purchasing, logistics, IT platform or back office to consolidate; the eight regional holding companies provide legal and tax support, not shared services. The AlphaSense report's two precedents of cost elimination in this business model make the point: Addtech's Automation restructuring (unprofitable sites closed, SEK 10m one-off, margin recovered within three to six months) is small and local, which is exactly the kind of action Indutrade's portfolio model ("problem companies" as one of six categories) is built to take, subsidiary by subsidiary; and Beijer Ref's warehouse centralisation (SEK 150m one-off for SEK 100m of annual savings by mid-2026) is a structural programme available only to a company that has chosen integration, which Indutrade has not and will not. Lifco's former acquisition head argues that the segment layer is already creeping centralisation; the counter-evidence from Univar's former president (9 March 2026) is that centralising localised distribution usually raises corporate cost rather than lowering it. Management's position is coherent: lean trading companies lose scarce application engineers if cut, and the gross margin those engineers protect is worth more than the SG&A they cost.

What the demonstrated actions delivered, dated. The 200 head reductions (announced through 2025, completed by the Q4 2025 call in January 2026) at an average cost of perhaps SEK 0.8m each are worth SEK 150 to 160m a year, about 50 basis points of sales, but they were taken in units whose revenue was also falling, so the net margin effect was to hold rather than lift the group margin through 2025; the benefit is that the cost base entering 2026 was about 0.5 points leaner than it would have been. The divestments (Scanmaskin, Plooijer Zaandam SEK 93m, Novisol SEK 50m, Modernum SEK 17m) removed roughly SEK 200m of revenue at low or negative margin, which is the main reason Infrastructure & Construction's margin rose from 10.5% to 12.2% in Q2 2026 on -3% organic sales; that is a one-time structural gain of about 25 basis points of group margin, already in the run-rate, with the chief executive stating in January 2026 that no further divestment agenda exists. The UK write-downs (SEK 125m in Q4 2025) were a cost of control failure, not a cost reduction; management says the projects are in their final phases, no recurring write-downs are expected, and recovery "will take time". The removal of the responsible managers eliminates the cause but does not restore the segment's margin until the project companies either return to contribution or are pruned.

When each remaining item reaches the line items.

  • Technology & Systems Solutions recovery: the area's EBITA margin fell to 13.7% in Q2 2026 from 14.4%; the UK project subsidiaries are a small group within a SEK 4.8bn area. A return to 14.5% by H2 2027 and 15% by 2028 adds about SEK 40 to 60m of EBITA, 10 to 20 basis points of group margin, phased over six to eight quarters. The new area head (Peter Laveson, an acquirer and turnaround operator by background) was appointed in September 2025 and has had a year; the first evidence will be the area's margin in Q3 and Q4 2026.
  • Infrastructure & Construction: the restructuring is done; the remaining improvement is cyclical (European building recovery deferred to 2027) and the new head (Håkan Svensson, August 2026) has a turnaround background. A segment margin of 13% by 2028 on recovering volume adds about SEK 40m, 10 basis points of group.
  • SG&A absorption: this is the operating-leverage item of P-01 and I do not double count it here.
  • Working-capital efficiency through the segment layer: the structured monthly scorecards and the subsidiary boards are the mechanism; the 2025 inventory reduction of 5% was the result and management says further releases are marginal. I model working capital flat at about 26% of revenue and treat any improvement as upside.

Against peers: what Indutrade does not have. Lagercrantz's benchmarking programme asks subsidiaries for 2 points of margin improvement toward a 20% group target; Addtech weeds out low-margin trading accounts; Lifco mandates post-acquisition price rises. Each is a central efficiency lever that Indutrade has declined, and the refusal to raise the 14% margin target is the policy expression of that. The cost of the choice is a structurally lower margin ceiling (about 15.5% by 2028 against 17 to 20% at peers); the benefit is the deal velocity that produced nine acquisitions in H1 2026 and the seller reputation that keeps entry multiples at 6 to 8x. For the next four years, the Consumer should assume the choice holds: no programme, no step, and margin improvement that is 80% volume and mix and 20% the roll-off of 2025's problems.

What would change this. Three signals would mean a cost lever is being pulled after all: a central function announced for purchasing or logistics (none is planned); a second round of divestments (ruled out in January 2026); or an explicit margin target above 14% at a Capital Markets Day (the next is on the three-year cadence, 2028). A fourth signal would mean the opposite, that cost is growing into the recovery: SG&A rising faster than sales for two consecutive quarters while organic growth is positive, which is what happened in H2 2024 and is the pattern to watch in Q3 and Q4 2026.

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years

C55

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Price contributes 1.5 to 2.5 points of the 3 to 4.5% organic growth in the forecast and holds the gross margin flat at 35.5% on the company's basis; it is a defence of the margin, not a source of expansion, and the defence is strong in the specified and regulated half of the group and weakening at the standardised margin. The 80 basis points of company-basis gross margin gained from 2023 to Q2 2026 (34.6% to 35.6%) was roughly one-third price discipline through the inflation years, one-third mix (Life Science and proprietary products growing faster than trading) and one-third krona-driven purchasing gains in Swedish import-and-resell subsidiaries; of those three, the first is now spent, the second continues at 10 to 20 basis points a year, and the third reverses as the krona weakens (EUR/SEK 11.20 in October 2026 against about 10.9 to 11.0 a year earlier). Netting them, the gross margin is flat to down 10 basis points a year in the base case, which is why I carry no gross-margin expansion and put all the EBITA margin improvement below the gross profit line.

The evidence for what price has done and can do. Three facts establish pricing power at the margin line. First, the gross margin rose through a seven-quarter volume decline in which freight, metals, semiconductor and component costs rose, which cannot happen under cost-plus pass-through alone; the company attributes it to value-based pricing and managing directors "protecting the gross margin under all circumstances". Second, the contract structure is built for it: most customer contracts have an expected duration of a year or less with point-in-time revenue recognition, currency clauses are standard, and framework contracts in technical service carry indexation (with lags that wage inflation exposes); subsidiaries repriced freight and semiconductor increases within quarters in 2021 to 2022. Third, the peers behaved the same way through the same downturn (Addtech's gross margin up 330 basis points from 2022 to 2025, Lagercrantz above 39%, Bufab's twelve consecutive quarters of gross-margin expansion to 33.2%, Bossard 34.4%), which says the pricing conduct is an industry structure, not a company habit. The AlphaSense pricing report's conclusion is that 35.5% is a defensible floor through a renewed downturn or an input-cost shock, with medium-to-high confidence, and I accept that with one refinement: the floor is defensible at the group level because Life Science (18% EBITA margin, regulatory lock-in) and flow technology cross-subsidise it; it is not defensible in every part.

Where it is weakest, and what that costs. The standardised part of Technology & Systems Solutions (catalogue-type electronics and automation components) has the lowest switching costs and faces three pressures at once: digital price transparency and AI-assisted procurement (Morgan Stanley's October 2026 distributor survey says the near-term moat erosion is limited and 39% of distributors see no measurable effect from AI yet, but the direction is clear); principals such as SKF, Danfoss and Bosch going direct or pushing list prices that distributors must accept; and the Q3 2026 automation-channel evidence that suppliers are recovering only 2 to 3% of 3 to 4% cost inflation as availability restores customer leverage. Against that, the Flowserve (3 to 6% price, aftermarket 4 to 8%), Munters (3 to 5%) and Sika (20% price with 20% volume) expert evidence of October 2026 says critical and specified components still price freely. The portfolio consequence is a gross-margin dispersion that the company does not disclose: perhaps 40%-plus in Life Science and proprietary niches, perhaps 28 to 30% in standard trading. The bear case I carry is 20 to 50 basis points of gross-margin erosion in the trading half (about 10 to 30 basis points at group level) over three years, which the 14% EBITA floor can absorb.

Price by operating condition over the forecast horizon. (1) Volume recovery with normalised supply (2026 to 2027, the base case): price contributes about 2% of organic growth, slightly below cost inflation of 3 to 4%, so the gross margin is held by mix rather than by price; this is the condition in which customer bargaining power returns and is the one the Q3 2026 expert evidence describes. (2) A weak krona (the current path): Swedish trading subsidiaries buying in euros and dollars lose the purchasing gain that lifted Q1 and Q2 2026 gross margins; the company's history (2014 to 2016, when a weak krona compressed trading margins under fixed-price contracts until repricing caught up) says the lag is two to four quarters and the group effect is partly offset by the translation gain on foreign earnings. I carry a 10 to 20 basis point drag on the company-basis gross margin in 2027 from this source. (3) An energy-led raw-material shock to plastics, metals and freight: the 2021 to 2023 record says pass-through within two quarters with a transient 20 to 40 basis point dip (Lagercrantz's 20 basis point fall in Q1 2026/27 on raw materials is the live example); a drag on one or two quarters, not on the year. (4) A prolonged construction recession: Infrastructure & Construction (15% of sales) faces the intense price competition Lindab describes in Germany, France and the Netherlands; that area's gross margin is the one most likely to fall, and its 12.2% EBITA margin has less cushion. (5) Digital transparency: slow, confined to standard components, a 2028-plus issue for the model.

Against Lifco, and what the comparison says about the ceiling. Lifco mandates 3 to 7% price rises in the first year of ownership and holds about 43% gross margins; Indutrade's hands-off model leaves pricing to the managing director and achieves 35.6%. The gap is mostly mix (Lifco's dental and demolition-robot franchises against Indutrade's 58% trading share) and partly playbook. The playbook part, perhaps 1 to 2 points of gross margin over several years, is available to Indutrade only if it changes its model, which management has declined to do; the mix part closes only through acquisitions, at 10 to 20 basis points a year. The pricing power is therefore real, adequate to hold the margin, and not a lever the forecast can pull.

For the model. Organic growth of 4.5% in H2 2026, 3.5% in 2027, 3% thereafter, of which price is about 2%, 2%, 1.5 to 2%; company-basis gross margin 35.5% in 2026, 35.4% in 2027, 35.4 to 35.5% thereafter (FactSet basis 33.3 to 33.5%); no gross-margin contribution to EBITA margin expansion; all expansion from operating leverage, mix and area recoveries as in P-01 and P-03.

P-05

Reinforcing and balancing feedback loops: how they shape the main line items over the next 18 months to four years

C57

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Four reinforcing loops are running and two of them (deal sourcing and operating leverage) have visibly re-engaged since H1 2026; three balancing loops (scale, acquisition pricing, complexity) are running underneath them and will not tip the balance within 18 months but will cap the compounding rate at 9 to 11% EBITA growth from 2028 rather than the 12 to 15% the loops delivered in 2017 to 2023. The net effect on the line items is that revenue and EBITA growth are front-loaded (2026 to 2027 above the algorithm, 2028 to 2030 at it), the margin rises mechanically by 10 to 20 basis points a year from mix on top of whatever leverage delivers, and the free-cash-flow loop that funds the deals is intact because the balance sheet is unlevered. The observable tipping indicators are the deal count (below 15 a year would mean the sourcing loop has stalled), the realised acquisition multiple (above 9x all-in would mean the arithmetic loop is being offset), working capital above 27.5% of revenue at a year-end, and SG&A growing faster than sales in a quarter of positive organic growth.

The deal-sourcing loop: running, and the most important for the four-year numbers. Mechanism: 35 segment leaders, each a former managing director, each chairing the boards of 6 to 8 companies and each tasked with at least one acquisition a year from a mapped network of up to 1,500 candidates; 75% of acquired companies come from the internal pipeline (senior vice president of acquisitions, 4 November 2025), which is the same proprietary share Addtech reports (80% from organic relationships). Evidence it is running: nine deals adding SEK 1.5bn of annual sales in H1 2026 against 13 deals and SEK 1.3bn in all of 2025; Controlin (SEK 130m) in July and Euromed (Poland, conditional) in October; acquired contribution to EBITA back to 7 to 8% from 4 to 5%; and the Addtech precedent that a reorganisation into more sub-divisions (2021, and again to 15 subdivision heads in late 2025 with a 15 to 20 deal target) raised both organic and acquired growth. What would stall it: the Lifco former-acquisition-head critique that the segment layer builds silos and centralises, which would deter the family sellers who come for autonomy; the evidence against that critique is the H1 2026 count itself. The loop is self-reinforcing in a specific sense: each closed deal adds a managing director to a segment's network of potential sellers, which is why the best compounders' proprietary flow rises with size. For the model it supports SEK 2.0 to 2.5bn of acquired sales a year (16 to 20 deals), rising toward SEK 3bn by 2029 to 2030 as the count climbs toward the 20 to 30 the structure was built for.

The arithmetic loop: running, with its return compressed by the price paid. Mechanism: free cash flow after dividends of about SEK 2.3 to 3.2bn a year buys EBITA at 5 to 8x initial consideration plus about 0.4x sales of earn-outs (an all-in median of 8.6x for 2023 to 2025 on DNB Carnegie's table, against 6.6 to 6.8x at Addtech, Lagercrantz and Lifco); the acquired EBITA at 16 to 19% margins compounds the group, and the group trades at 19x 2026 EV/EBITA. The arithmetic at my base case: SEK 2.4bn of cash in 2027 at 8.5x all-in buys about SEK 280m of EBITA, 4.9% of the 2027 base; add the full-year effect of the prior year's deals and acquired EBITA growth is 6 to 7% a year, which is DNB Carnegie's 7% average to 2031 and the company's ten-year norm. Where it turns balancing: the return on each krona is 11.8% pre-tax at 8.5x against 15% at 6.7x, so Indutrade needs about 25% more cash than its peers to buy the same EBITA growth; and the base grows faster than the cash, so holding 6 to 7% acquired growth requires the cash deployed to rise from SEK 2.3bn to 3.0bn by 2030, which the cash flow supports (P-02) but the organisation must source. The loop reverses only at the multiples (10x and above) the new platforms and private equity pay for larger deals, and Indutrade's sweet spot (SEK 30 to 250m of sales) sits mostly below the EUR 20 to 50m line where that competition starts; Tången buys at 4 to 6x, Auroora at Finnish discounts, Salix at a 6.7x weighted average, which says disciplined buyers still find the price. The larger recent deals (Valveco SEK 345m, Createc 250m) are the ones to watch for multiple creep.

The operating-leverage loop: re-engaged in Q2 2026, and the one that funds the next turn of the flywheel. Mechanism: organic growth absorbs the pre-built cost base, EBITA rises 2x faster than sales, cash conversion of that EBITA is high (SEK 882m of Q2 2026 operating cash flow, +20%, with comparable inventories flat), and the cash funds deals that enter at higher margins. The loop's weak link is working capital: receivables rose with the Q2 invoicing surge, net working capital reached 27.9% of trailing revenue at June 2026 against 24.2% in March 2025, and management said in 2025 that further inventory releases would be marginal. Redeye's long-run model has working capital absorbing SEK 6bn through 2033. My reading: about 26 öre of each incremental organic krona is absorbed, the H1 2026 build (SEK 950m from December to June) is two-thirds seasonal and acquired-company balances, and the year-end ratio returns to about 26%; the loop therefore converts about 90% of incremental EBITA into cash, not 100%. The precedent that the loop can run without organic growth is Addtech (fifth consecutive year of margin expansion to 16.0% on 2% organic growth, return on working capital 81%, nine deals funded from cash flow), which is the pattern Indutrade would fall back to if the cycle relapses.

The mix loop: running mechanically, and capped by policy. Life Science (23% of sales, 18.0% margin, organic +13% in Q2 2026, consensus SEK 7.5bn to 10.1bn from 2025 to 2028) and acquired companies (19.5% and 17.6% margins in Q1 and Q2 2026 against group 13.3% and 14.7%) lift the group margin by 10 to 20 basis points a year with no operating improvement. Lagercrantz is the precedent (proprietary share 77 to 78%, margin toward 18% and a 20% target); Addtech weeds out low-margin trading accounts. The cap is management's decision not to raise the 14% target so that segment leaders can keep buying lower-margin, high-return trading businesses; DNB Carnegie notes that acquisitions have historically entered 2 to 3 points above the group margin, so the cap is soft in practice, but it means the loop lifts the margin by mix only as fast as deals tilt the portfolio, and not by a programme. Durability of entry margins after one to three years is the unverified assumption; Lifco's stable 23% says it holds in a hands-off model, and the UK case says it can fail locally.

The balancing loops, ranked by the damage they can do over four years. (1) Acquisition pricing: already visible at 8.6x against 6.7x, it does not stop the loop but lowers its return; each full turn of multiple on SEK 2.5bn of annual deployment costs about SEK 30m of EBITA a year, cumulative. (2) Scale: each deal is 0.4% of sales at SEK 35bn and 0.3% at 49bn; the structure was built for 20 to 30 deals a year precisely to offset this, and the loop stalls if the count stalls, as 2024 to 2025 showed (acquired contribution 4 to 5%, EBITA flat two years). (3) Complexity and control: 220 companies, no internal audit, a SEK 125m concealment undetected for several quarters; the balancing effect is episodic (SEK 100 to 300m of EBITA in a quarter, 5 to 10% of a quarter's EBITA) rather than continuous, and the sector precedents of full reversal (Storskogen's debt-funded roll-up, Volati at 4.5x net debt to EBITDA, Bergman & Beving's forced cost-cutting) all involved leverage or integration that Indutrade does not have. (4) Currency: SEK 17.0bn of unhedged foreign net investments and about SEK 18m of net profit per 1% krona move; this is the loop that moves reported numbers without moving the business, in either direction, and it currently points the right way. (5) The seller-reputation loop running in reverse if centralisation is perceived: the critique exists, the evidence does not yet.

How the loops shape each line item, 2026 to 2030. Revenue: 6 to 7% acquired plus 3 to 4.5% organic, with the acquired share rising as the sourcing loop scales and the organic share falling as the cycle normalises. EBITA margin: mix and acquisitions add 10 to 20 basis points a year on top of leverage, giving 15.2 to 15.4% by 2028 to 2030; the first set's 15.0 to 15.5% range is confirmed and I sit in its lower half because of the trading-margin erosion and the weaker krona purchasing effect. EBITA growth: about 14% in 2026 (easy base, leverage, acquired contribution), 12 to 13% in 2027, 10 to 11% in 2028 to 2030. Free cash flow: funds the deals with nothing to spare, leverage drifting down. Return on capital employed: stays at 18 to 19% as each year's deals enter below the group average at 8.5x; the loop that would lift it (lower entry multiples) is the one not running.

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and in which direction to lean

C50

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement. The forecasts for the next 18 months deserve moderate weight at the group level (revenue and EBITA margin reliable within about 1.5 points and 60 basis points respectively over a year) and low weight at the quarterly level (5 to 10% of a quarter's EBITA is routinely unguided noise), and they should be leaned in three different directions at once: slightly below consensus on 2026 to 2027 revenue and pre-tax profit, in line on the EBITA margin, and above consensus on 2028 and beyond. The lean is small in 2026 (about 1 to 2% on EPS), larger in 2027 (2 to 3% below on EPS because of the interest line, offset by unannounced deals), and reverses in 2028 (3 to 5% above on EBITA because consensus models only completed deals). The weight rises materially if Q3 2026 (tomorrow) and Q4 2026 (January 2027) deliver organic growth of 3% or more with a margin at or above the prior-year quarter; it falls to low if either print shows SG&A growing faster than sales.

The record, read for what it predicts. The company's record is strong on the things that do not change with the cycle and weak on the things that do. Reliable across 2019 to 2025: gross margin (32.0 to 33.4% on FactSet's basis, never down more than 30 basis points in a year); the 14% margin floor (reported EBITA margin never below 13.3% in a quarter, including the quarter with the write-down); the 5 to 8x entry multiple; the absence of equity issuance; the progressive dividend; and the acquired contribution to EBITA when the organisation is not distracted (7 to 8% in eight of the last ten years). Unreliable: organic growth (a decade average of 4% with a range of -4% to +13% by quarter); the SG&A ratio in the year after a demand turn (it lagged in both directions in 2023 to 2026); and quarterly EBITA against consensus (three misses in four quarters to Q1 2026, then an 11 to 12% beat; the misses were 5 to 14% and concentrated in cost absorption and one control failure, the beat was in volume and leverage). The pattern is mechanical: the company beats when volume arrives and misses when it does not, because the cost base does not flex. That means the forecasts' reliability is the reliability of the organic growth assumption, and the margin forecast is a derivative of it through the P-01 rule.

Why lean below on 2026 revenue and 2027 pre-tax profit. Consensus 2026 revenue of SEK 35,364m requires H2 2026 growth of 13.9%; with acquired growth of 6 to 7%, organic 4 to 5% and currency neutral to slightly positive, H2 lands at 11 to 12%, so 2026 revenue is 1 to 1.5% high (my SEK 34.9bn, +8.4%). The margin consensus (14.5% EBITA in 2026, 15.0% in 2027) is where I sit; the P-01 rule produces it without stretch. The pre-tax line is where consensus is most exposed: it assumes net financial items falling by about SEK 120m into 2027 on rate cuts that the October 2026 regime has removed, and that gap is worth about SEK 0.30 of EPS. The 2027 free cash flow consensus (SEK 4,046m on FactSet's after-dividend definition, +58% on 2026) is too high only in its phasing; on a consistent definition the recovery is real but spread over 2026 to 2028.

Why lean above from 2028. Consensus revenue growth of 5.0% in 2028 and the EPS "fall" in 2029 (one broker) are coverage artefacts: brokers model completed deals only, and the sector digest's finding is that unannounced acquisitions are typically two-thirds of a top compounder's earnings growth. At SEK 2.4 to 2.8bn of annual deployment, the unmodelled acquired contribution is 4 to 5% of EBITA a year from 2028, which is why my 2028 EBITA (SEK 6.3bn) is about 5% above consensus EBIT (6,039m) and my 2028 reported EPS (SEK 10.54) sits above the reported-basis brokers (10.20 to 10.38) while my adjusted EPS (12.90) sits level with DNB Carnegie and Nordea (12.80 to 13.00), whose M&A scenarios do include a 7% acquired contribution.

The direction of surprise over the next 18 months, by event. Q3 2026 (23 October): consensus has organic growth of roughly 3 to 4% and an EBITA margin near 15%; the base is the strongest quarter of 2025 (14.6%), the krona has turned from headwind to neutral since the Q1 print, orders led sales by 3 points in Q2 and the backlog was 8% higher, so the probability of an in-line-to-better print is above 60%, but the share price at a 52-week high and 18.8% above its 200-day average prices that in. Q4 2026 (late January 2027): the easiest comparison of the cycle (0% organic, 13.3% reported margin with the write-down), so reported growth will look strong whatever the underlying run-rate; the test is the underlying margin against 14.9% and the 2026 deal count against Redeye's 18. Q1 2027 (April): against 0% organic and 13.3%, still easy; the first quarter under the new chief financial officer. Q2 2027 (July): the first hard comparison (+5% organic, 14.7%), and the quarter in which a 2027 relapse would show; this is where the cone is widest and where I would put the largest probability of a consensus cut if the rates regime has bitten. The record says the company will not pre-warn and will not guide, so each of these is a binary event of 5 to 15% share-price magnitude.

The weight in numbers. At the group level over 18 months: revenue within plus or minus 1.5% of my base case with 60% probability, EBITA margin within plus or minus 40 basis points with 60% probability, reported EPS within plus or minus 5%. At the quarterly level: EBITA within plus or minus 8% of any sensible forecast with 60% probability. The 2024 to Q1 2026 record would justify lower weights than these; the Q2 2026 print and the structural facts (deal engine restored, cost base pre-built, orders leading sales, destocking complete) justify the moderate weights I give. Two more quarters of delivery would raise them; one miss of the Q1 2026 kind would lower them for a year.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C52

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. The items below EBITA are, in order of size, amortisation of acquired intangibles (SEK 656m in 2025, growing to about SEK 1.0bn by 2030 with the deal pace), net financial items (SEK -437m, drifting to about -495m), tax (23 to 23.5% of reported pre-tax profit, drifting up with the geographic mix), earn-out remeasurements and one-offs (a net positive SEK 208m in 2025's operating income, a net negative so far in 2026, unforecastable and best modelled at zero), and currency (unhedged, currently a mild tailwind). The share count is fixed at 364.3m and will stay fixed; dividends rise about 10% a year at a 40% payout of reported earnings. Taken together, these items convert EBITA growth of 12 to 14% in 2026 to 2027 into reported EPS growth of about 18% and 14%, and EBITA growth of 10 to 11% in 2028 to 2030 into reported EPS growth of 10 to 11%, with adjusted EPS growing 1 to 2 points slower than reported because amortisation grows faster than profit. Against the last six years, the below-EBITA lines are a smaller drag than in 2022 to 2024 (when net interest rose fivefold) and a slightly larger one than in 2019 to 2021 (when amortisation was SEK 400 to 450m and interest about 120m). Free cash flow after leases converts at 95 to 110% of reported net income, a correction upward from the first set's reading.

Amortisation of acquired intangibles: the bridge between the two EPS bases. SEK 656m in 2025 (of 706m total intangible amortisation), straight-line over 5 to 20 years on customer relationships, agencies and trademarks, growing with every deal: about SEK 720m in 2026, 790m in 2027, 860m in 2028, 930m in 2029 and 1.0bn in 2030 on my acquisition path (roughly 40% of consideration allocated to amortisable intangibles over about ten years). It is non-cash, largely non-deductible (share deals), and it is the whole difference between the reported-basis brokers (Pareto 8.46, Goldman Sachs 8.71, two restricted brokers at 8.40 and 9.84 for 2026) and the adjusted-basis brokers (DNB Carnegie 10.40, Nordea 10.30), which add it back in full with no tax effect. The FactSet median EPS of 10.01 blends the two and must not be used as a series. For the Financial Analyst's model, which carries no acquired-amortisation line, the model's earnings will be on the adjusted basis; reported EPS is about SEK 2.0 per share lower in 2026, rising to 2.75 lower by 2030. The economic question (whether acquired customer relationships decay faster than the accounting life) is the one DNB Carnegie raises and the one a short-seller would raise; the evidence (gross margin and organic growth of the acquired base) says they do not decay quickly, but it is a point of exposure for the adjusted basis.

Net financial items. Covered in P-02: SEK -410m in 2026, -450m in 2027, -465m in 2028, -480m in 2029, -495m in 2030, which includes about SEK 60 to 80m a year of lease interest and small earn-out discount unwinds. Consensus is about SEK 120m more favourable by 2027. Each 100 basis points on policy rates is SEK 61m pre-tax, SEK 0.13 of EPS.

Tax. The effective rate was 23.1% in 2025 (SEK 770m on SEK 3,332m), 24% in H1 2026, against a Swedish statutory 20.6% and a weighted national rate of 21.8%; the difference is non-deductible amortisation, withholding taxes and the mix of higher-rate jurisdictions (UK, Germany, Netherlands, Italy, Spain) in which the recent acquisitions sit. Pillar Two was SEK 6m in 2025 (Ireland) and the transitional safe harbours run to 2026; after that the exposure is procedural, not financial. I model 23.5% of reported pre-tax profit in 2026, 23.3% in 2027 and 2028, 23.4% in 2029 and 23.5% in 2030. Expressed against the model's pre-amortisation profit (EBITA less net financial items), because that is the base the Financial Analyst's model will apply it to, the same tax charge is 19.9%, 19.8%, 19.9%, 20.0% and 20.2%; I enter those figures in the form so that the model's net income lands on the brokers' adjusted basis, and I explain the conversion in Part 4.

Earn-outs, one-offs and the quarterly noise. Contingent-consideration remeasurements flow through operating income (SEK 208m positive in 2025, SEK 15m more in net financials; SEK 59m positive in Q2 2025 and SEK 14m negative in Q2 2026); a positive remeasurement means an acquired company is underperforming its earn-out, so it is bad news dressed as income, and the 2025 figure says a meaningful fraction of the 2022 to 2024 vintage missed its targets. Project write-downs (SEK 125m in Q4 2025) are the other recurring "non-recurring" item. Peers differ (Addtech books earn-out revaluations in operating profit and put-option changes in equity; Lifco books put-option changes in equity; Diploma publishes a full bridge). For the model I set both to zero and carry the noise as a quarterly variance of 5 to 10% of EBITA; across a year the items have netted to between -1% and +5% of EBITA since 2023. The cash side of earn-outs is small: SEK 237m paid in 2025 for earlier acquisitions, a maximum of SEK 436m on the H1 2026 deals over one to three years; I include about SEK 300m a year inside the acquisitions line.

Currency. Unhedged translation on SEK 17.0bn of foreign net investments (EUR 9.0bn of it); a 1% krona move is SEK 243m of sales, 18m of net profit (0.6%) and 181m of equity. Translation was +4% on sales in 2023, -2% on EBITA in 2025, -3% on EBITA in Q1 2026, neutral in Q2 2026, and with EUR/SEK at 11.20 in October 2026 it is neutral to slightly positive for H2 2026 and H1 2027. Transaction exposure is managed locally with currency clauses and matching (net exchange losses of SEK 32m in operating profit in 2025). I model zero translation; a 5% krona swing in either direction is SEK 90m of net profit, SEK 0.25 of EPS, 2.5 to 3%, and is the largest single unmodelled item in the 18-month numbers.

Share count, dividends, buybacks. 364.3m shares for four years, incentive programmes hedged with equity swaps so no creeping dilution, no buyback ever, none planned, and no reason to expect one while deals are available at 6 to 8x against a 23x P/E. Dividend SEK 3.10 for 2025 (44% of reported net income, 39% five-year average); the chief executive has said dividends will rise in absolute terms every year; I model SEK 3.40 for 2026, 3.80 for 2027, 4.20 for 2028, 4.65 for 2029 and 5.10 for 2030, about 40% of reported EPS. Consensus is SEK 3.30, 3.80, 3.80. Peers: Lifco 34% payout and no buybacks; Addtech 45% with 1% treasury shares; Lagercrantz 43% with 1.5% treasury shares. Indutrade's framework is the simplest in the cohort and will not change under this chief executive and this anchor shareholder.

Working capital, leases and the free-cash-flow bridge. With the correction in the reader's note, the bridge from EBITDA to free cash flow is: EBITDA (brokers' basis) less cash tax (about 95% of the charge), less net interest paid, less organic working-capital absorption (about 26 öre per incremental organic krona; the acquired companies' working capital comes inside the acquisition price), less about SEK 350m of other operating items (provisions, earn-out remeasurement reversals, the gap between FactSet's and the company's cash-flow classifications, which was about SEK 380m in 2025), less capital expenditure including intangibles (1.6% of sales, SEK 544m in 2025), less lease payments (SEK 565m in 2025, growing to about 800m by 2030 with the subsidiary footprint). That gives free cash flow after leases of about SEK 2.9bn in 2026, 3.5bn in 2027, 4.0bn in 2028, 4.4bn in 2029 and 4.9bn in 2030: 96%, 102%, 105%, 105% and 105% of reported net income, and 78 to 87% of adjusted net income. Against 2019 to 2025 (pre-dividend operating cash flow less capex and leases of about 114% of reported net income in 2025, 2023 and 2024 similar, 2022 at about 40% in the inventory build), the forecast conversion is a little lower than the inventory-release years and far better than 2022, which is where the Consumer should put the bear-case working-capital risk: a 2022-style build (inventory rising ahead of demand in a supply scare) would halve a year's free cash flow without touching EPS.

What it means for the three line items, 2026 to 2030, versus 2020 to 2025. Reported net income: SEK 3.0bn, 3.4bn, 3.8bn, 4.2bn, 4.6bn (compound growth 12.6% from 2025's 2,560m) against 8.9% a year from 2020 to 2025 and a decline in 2024 and 2025. Reported EPS: SEK 8.30, 9.45, 10.54, 11.57, 12.71; adjusted EPS: 10.28, 11.62, 12.90, 14.12, 15.46. Free cash flow after leases: as above, growing 14% a year, with none of it available for buybacks because the deals consume it. The below-EBITA items subtract about 1 point a year from EPS growth over the period (amortisation and interest growing slightly faster than EBITA, tax rate drifting up), which is the opposite of the first set's expectation that falling interest would add a point; the difference is the rates regime.

P-09

The net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C58

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. The next four years return the company to its historical algorithm with a front-loaded first 18 months and a slightly lower ceiling than the 2017 to 2023 version of it. Against the last six years (2020 to 2025: revenue compound growth 10.9%, EBITA margin averaging 14.4%, reported EPS compound growth 8.9%, two years of EPS decline at the end), the next four deliver revenue growth of 8.5 to 9.5% a year, an EBITA margin rising from 14.1% underlying to 15.3 to 15.4%, EBITA compound growth of about 11.5%, reported EPS compound growth of about 12.5% and adjusted EPS compound growth of about 11.5%, free cash flow after leases growing about 14% a year, and leverage falling. Against the last four years only (2022 to 2025: revenue growth 10.4% compound but reported EPS growth about 5% with the margin falling 170 basis points from peak to trough), the change is almost entirely in the margin and in the acquired engine. Nothing in the next four years is new in kind; what is different is that both engines run at once for the first time since 2023, and the cost base built for the 2023 to 2025 demand that never arrived now gets absorbed.

The line-by-line comparison, 2020 to 2025 against 2026 to 2030.

  • Revenue: SEK 19.2bn to 32.2bn (10.9% compound, of which acquired about 7%, organic about 3%, currency about +1%) against 32.2bn to about 49.0bn (8.8% compound, of which acquired 6 to 6.5%, organic 3 to 3.5%, currency zero). The growth rate is lower because the base is larger and the 2021 to 2023 post-pandemic restock and krona weakness do not recur; the composition is the same.
  • Gross margin: FactSet basis 32.0% to 33.4% (rising); forecast flat at 33.3 to 33.5%. The rise is over; the defence holds.
  • EBITA: SEK 2.6bn (2020, 13.5%) to 4.45bn (2025, 13.8%; 14.1% underlying), with a peak of 15.0% in 2023; forecast 5.08bn (2026, 14.55%), 5.73bn (2027, 15.0%), 6.33bn (2028, 15.2%), 6.91bn (2029, 15.3%), 7.55bn (2030, 15.4%): compound growth 11.1% against 11.3% over 2020 to 2025 (which included the 2021 to 2023 boom and the 2024 to 2025 stall). The margin exceeds the 2023 peak from 2027.
  • EBITDA (brokers' basis): 17.1% to 18.5%, against 16.5 to 17.9% over 2020 to 2025.
  • Net financial items: SEK -123m (2020) to -437m (2025), with a fivefold rise in 2022 to 2024; forecast -410m to -495m, a slow rise. The interest shock of the last cycle does not repeat.
  • Pre-tax profit (reported): SEK 2.1bn to 3.3bn (9.2% compound over 2020 to 2025) against 3.3bn (2025) to 6.05bn (2030), 12.7% compound, the acceleration coming from the margin recovery and the end of the interest drag.
  • Tax rate: 22 to 23% against 23.3 to 23.5%; a small negative.
  • Reported EPS: SEK 4.60 to 7.03 (8.9% compound over 2020 to 2025) against 8.30 to 12.71 (12.6% compound from 2025's 7.03; 11.2% from 2026 to 2030). Adjusted EPS: 8.83 (2025) to 15.46 (2030), 11.9% compound.
  • Dividend: SEK 1.80 to 3.10 (11.5% compound, from a suspended 2019 base) against 3.40 to 5.10 (10.5%).
  • Free cash flow after capex and leases: about SEK 2.9bn in 2025 (3,990 less 502 less 565) against 2.9bn to 4.9bn, 11% compound from 2025; conversion falls from 114% of reported net income to about 100 to 105% because the inventory-release years are over, but the first set's 80 to 90% was a reading of the wrong line.
  • Net debt: SEK 7.3bn (end-2025, FactSet basis) to about 8.3 to 9.2bn, with leverage from 1.3x to about 1.0x; net debt to equity from 45% toward 30%.
  • Return on capital employed: 18% toward 19%, not 20%; each year's deals enter at 8.5x all-in and take three to five years to reach the group average.
  • Share count: unchanged.

What drives the difference, ranked by size. (1) The acquired engine restored to 6 to 7% of sales a year from 4 to 5% in 2024 to 2025 (worth about 2 points of annual EBITA growth against the last two years, nothing against the six-year average). (2) Operating leverage on the pre-built cost base (worth about 100 basis points of margin over 2026 to 2027, 1.5 points of EBITA growth a year over two years, then fading). (3) Organic growth returning to its 3 to 4% norm from -2% to 0% (worth 3 to 5 points of annual revenue growth against the last two years). (4) Mix and area recoveries (Life Science, acquired margins, Infrastructure & Construction and Technology & Systems Solutions normalising; 40 to 60 basis points of margin over four years). (5) The absence of an interest shock (worth about 1 point of EPS growth a year against 2022 to 2024). Against these, three drags: amortisation growing faster than EBITA (minus 0.5 point of reported EPS growth a year), the tax rate drifting up (minus 0.2 point), and acquisition pricing at 8.6x rather than 6.7x (which does not reduce growth but holds return on capital employed below target).

The concentration of the drivers, for the third set. Two of the five drivers (the acquired engine and operating leverage) account for about two-thirds of the difference between the next four years and the last two, and both depend on the same organisational fact: 35 segment leaders sourcing deals and holding costs while volume returns. The third (organic growth) is exogenous and cyclical, and the rates regime has turned against it since October 2026. The difference is therefore moderately concentrated: not in one product or customer, but in one organisational capability and one macro variable. That is the honest basis for the third set's predictability test.

P-10

The base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years

C58

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. The base case is a restored compounder running at the lower end of the cohort's historical algorithm: revenue growth of about 9% a year (6 to 6.5% acquired, 3 to 3.5% organic, currency neutral), an EBITA margin that recovers to 15.0% in 2027 and reaches 15.4% by 2030, EBITA growth of 14%, 13%, 10%, 9% and 9%, reported EPS of SEK 8.30, 9.45, 10.54, 11.57 and 12.71 (adjusted 10.28, 11.62, 12.90, 14.12, 15.46), a dividend rising about 10% a year, free cash flow after leases that exactly funds dividends plus a SEK 2.3 to 3.0bn annual acquisition programme, and leverage falling to about 1.0x EBITDA by 2030 with no buyback and no issuance. The 18-month path is front-loaded: H2 2026 and Q1 2027 run against the easiest comparisons of the cycle with organic growth of 4 to 5% and year-on-year margin gains of 60 to 100 basis points per quarter; from Q2 2027 the comparisons harden, organic growth normalises toward 3% and the margin gains shrink to 20 to 40 basis points. The skew around the base case is negative over twelve months on the organic input (the rates regime) and positive over four years on the acquired input (the balance sheet could fund 50% more deals than I assume); the cone is roughly symmetric on EPS in 2026 (plus or minus 5%) and widens to plus or minus 12% by 2028.

The operational drivers.

  • Organic sales growth: H2 2026 +4.5% (Q3 +4%, Q4 +4.5 to 5%), 2027 +3.5% (Q1 +4%, then +3%), 2028 +3%, 2029 +3%, 2030 +3%; price about 2 points of each. By area for 2026: Life Science +9%, Process, Energy & Water +5%, Industrial & Engineering +3.5%, Technology & Systems Solutions +2%, Infrastructure & Construction -3%; for 2027: +7%, +5%, +3%, +3%, +2%.
  • Acquired sales: SEK 2.0 to 2.2bn of annual sales acquired in 2026 (nine H1 deals SEK 1.5bn, Controlin 130m, Euromed and others), SEK 2.3bn in 2027, 2.5bn in 2028, 2.8bn in 2029, 3.0bn in 2030; 16 to 20 deals a year rising toward 22 to 25; acquired contribution to sales 6.5% in H2 2026 and 2027, 6% thereafter; acquired EBITA margins 16 to 18%; all-in multiple 8 to 8.5x including earn-outs; cash deployed SEK 2.3bn, 2.4bn, 2.6bn, 2.8bn, 3.0bn.
  • Currency: zero translation effect; the swing is SEK 18m of net profit per 1%.
  • Gross margin: 35.5% company basis in 2026, 35.4% in 2027, 35.4 to 35.5% thereafter.
  • SG&A: growing 7 to 8% in 2026 (wage inflation plus acquired companies), 7% in 2027, 7 to 8% thereafter, so the ratio falls from 22.5% of sales to about 21.6% by 2028.
  • Deal count and backlog are the two leading indicators: the backlog was +8% at June 2026 and the book-to-bill 105%; a count below 15 in a year or a book-to-bill below 100% for two quarters would move me off the base case.

The 18-month phasing. Q3 2026: revenue +11% (organic +4%, acquired +7%, currency 0), EBITA margin 15.2% (EBITDA 18.3%), EBITA about SEK 1,325m (+15%). Q4 2026: revenue +11.5%, EBITA margin 14.8% (EBITDA 17.9%), EBITA about 1,360m (+24% reported, +11% underlying). Q1 2027: revenue +10.5%, EBITA margin 14.2% (EBITDA 17.3%), EBITA about 1,265m (+18%). Q2 2027: revenue +9%, margin 15.0% (EBITDA 18.1%), EBITA 1,470m (+11%). Q3 2027: +9%, 15.5% (18.6%), 1,470m (+11%). Q4 2027: +9%, 15.2% (18.3%), 1,520m (+12%).

Against consensus, line by line. Revenue: 1.2% below in 2026, 0.5% below in 2027, 3.3% above in 2028. EBITA (consensus EBIT): 1% below in 2026 (5,083 against 5,127), 0.7% below in 2027, 5% above in 2028. EBITA margin: in line (14.5%, 15.0%, 15.2% against 14.5%, 15.0%, 15.0%). Pre-tax: 1% below in 2026, 4.5% below in 2027 (interest), 3% below in 2028 on the reported basis. Reported EPS: 1 to 5% below the reported-basis brokers in 2026, in line to 4% below in 2027, above in 2028; adjusted EPS: 1 to 2% below DNB Carnegie and Nordea in 2026 and 2027, level in 2028. Dividend: above in 2026 (3.40 against 3.30), level in 2027, above in 2028. Free cash flow: not comparable to FactSet's after-dividend line; on a like basis I am close for 2026 and below for 2027's 58% jump.

The cone. Bull (one in five): organic growth of 5 to 6% through 2027 as the PMI lead indicator plays out and construction recovers early, 20-plus deals a year at 7.5x, krona weaker: 2027 EBITA about SEK 6.2bn (15.6%), reported EPS SEK 10.5, adjusted 12.7. Bear (one in four): organic growth relapses to 0% in 2027 under the rates regime, SG&A grows into the recovery, one more SEK 100 to 200m control failure, deals continue at 15 a year: 2027 EBITA about SEK 5.1bn (14.0%), reported EPS SEK 8.2, adjusted 10.3, roughly flat on 2026. The bear case does not break the 14% floor or the dividend; it breaks the re-rating. Neither tail changes the 2028 to 2030 algorithm much, because the acquired engine runs in both.

The three things I would change my base case on. (1) The Q3 2026 and Q4 2026 prints: organic growth below 2% in either, or SG&A growth above sales growth in either, moves me to the bear path for 2027. (2) The 2026 deal count and the realised multiples disclosed with them: a count below 15 or a median all-in multiple above 9x takes 1 to 2 points off 2027 to 2028 EBITA growth. (3) The Riksbank and ECB path: three hikes by mid-2027 is in my interest line; more than that, or a Swedish housing-led slowdown, is the organic relapse.

FactSet's segment consensus for 2026 is Life Science 8,630, Industrial & Engineering 8,896, Process, Energy & Water 8,257, Infrastructure & Construction 4,717, Technology & Systems Solutions 4,839; I am below it on Industrial & Engineering and Life Science (slower organic growth in the short-cycle base and a lower acquired contribution in Life Science until Euromed completes) and slightly above on Infrastructure & Construction (acquisitions offset the organic decline). The Life Science margin assumes mix within the area continues to shift toward hospital and laboratory products; the Technology & Systems Solutions margin assumes the UK project companies stop losing money in 2027 and return to contribution in 2028.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue growth is built as organic growth plus acquired growth plus currency. Organic: H2 2026 +4.5% (Q3 +4% against a -1% base, Q4 +4.5 to 5% against a 0% base), 2027 +3.5% (Q1 +4% against 0%, then +3% as the comparisons harden from Q2 2027), 2028 to 2030 +3%, the decade norm; price is about 2 points of each year's organic growth. Acquired: annual sales acquired of SEK 2.0 to 2.2bn in 2026 (nine H1 deals adding SEK 1.5bn, Controlin SEK 130m in July, Euromed in Poland conditional on competition approval), SEK 2.3bn in 2027, 2.5bn in 2028, 2.8bn in 2029 and 3.0bn in 2030, which is 16 to 20 deals a year rising toward 22 to 25, giving an acquired contribution of 6.5 to 7% of sales in H2 2026 and 2027 and about 6% thereafter. Currency: zero translation effect at EUR/SEK 11.20 (ING, 12 October 2026); a 1% krona move is SEK 243m of sales and SEK 18m of net profit, unhedged.

EBITDA margin is the EBITA margin plus depreciation of 3.1 to 3.2% of sales (property, equipment, right-of-use assets and non-acquired intangibles; SEK 1,065m in 2025, 3.3%). The EBITA margin path (14.55% in 2026, 15.0% in 2027, 15.2% in 2028, 15.3% in 2029, 15.4% in 2030, from 14.1% underlying in 2025) is built from four pieces: operating leverage on a pre-built cost base, at one-third of a margin point per point of organic growth above a 2% break-even rate (about +40 basis points in 2026, +40 in 2027, +20 in 2028, +10 thereafter); mix from Life Science and acquired companies entering at 16 to 19% margins (+10 to 20 basis points a year); the recovery of Technology & Systems Solutions from 13.7% and Infrastructure & Construction from 12.2% (+20 to 30 basis points over 2027 to 2028); and a flat company-basis gross margin of 35.4 to 35.5%, with krona-driven purchasing gains fading as price discipline and mix offset the loss. The quarterly phasing in Part 3 reflects the easy second-half and Q1 2027 comparisons (Q3 2025 margin 14.6%, Q4 2025 13.3% reported and 14.9% underlying, Q1 2026 13.3%) and the hard comparison from Q2 2027 (14.7%); Q1 is seasonally the weakest margin quarter and Q3 the strongest.

Net financial items rise in absolute terms from SEK -437m in 2025 because net debt is about SEK 2bn higher after the H1 2026 deals and the Riksbank and ECB are leaning toward hikes (SEK 61m of pre-tax cost per percentage point); they fall as a share of EBITA from 9.8% to 6.6%. The line includes lease interest and small earn-out discount unwinds. Tax: see the bridge below. Dividend per share: 40% of reported EPS, rising every year in absolute terms as the chief executive has stated. Share count: 364.3m, unchanged for four years, incentive programmes hedged with equity swaps.

Capital spending is 1.6% of sales (SEK 544m in 2025 including intangibles). Working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables, FactSet's definition) was 25.7% of trailing revenue at December 2025, 26.6% at December 2024 and 27.9% at June 2026 (seasonal peak plus the Q2 invoicing surge); I hold the year-end ratio at 26.2% for 2026 and 2027 and 26.0% thereafter, because inventory releases are exhausted (management, 2025) and acquired companies bring similar ratios. A note for the model: part of each year's increase in the working-capital balance arrives with acquired companies and is paid for inside the acquisitions line, so a model that absorbs the full balance change through operating cash flow overstates the cash absorbed by about SEK 400 to 650m a year; my cross-check below counts only the organic absorption. Lease payments were SEK 565m in 2025 and grow with the subsidiary footprint. Acquisitions less disposals is cash consideration including earn-out settlements (SEK 237m in 2025; a maximum of SEK 436m on the H1 2026 deals over one to three years); disposals are nil (no divestment agenda, chief executive, January 2026). No buybacks, consistent with the company's whole listed history.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

The company gives no numerical guidance for any period. Its through-cycle financial targets (reconfirmed at the November 2025 Capital Markets Day, not raised): average annual sales growth of at least 10%; EBITA margin of at least 14% over a cycle; return on capital employed of at least 20%; net debt to equity normally below 100%; a dividend payout of 30 to 50% of net profit. Against them, my inputs give sales growth of 8.4 to 9.3% a year (below the target; the target was met only in 2021 to 2023 and requires 20-plus deals a year at this size, which I treat as the upside case); an EBITA margin of 14.55% rising to 15.4% (above the floor every year, as it was on average over 2021 to 2025 at 14.6%); return on capital employed of about 18 to 19% (below target, because each year's acquisitions enter at an all-in multiple of about 8.5x and take three to five years to reach the group average); net debt to equity falling from 45% toward 30%; and a payout of about 40%. The qualitative commentary since July 2026 (backlog +8% with deliveries extending through Q4, an "exceptionally strong" M&A pipeline, costs not expected to rise proportionately with revenue, Life Science demand broadening, Infrastructure & Construction bottoming, no recurring UK write-downs expected) is consistent with my H2 2026 inputs and gives no information beyond them.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

The company reports net sales, EBITA (before amortisation of acquired intangibles), operating profit (EBIT, after it), net financial items, profit before tax, net profit and basic EPS. FactSet's consensus EBITDA and EBIT are on the brokers' adjusted basis: consensus EBIT (SEK 5,127m for 2026) is effectively the company's EBITA, because consensus depreciation and amortisation (SEK 1,075m) excludes acquired-intangible amortisation (SEK 656m in 2025, about 720m in 2026); consensus EBITDA (SEK 6,204m, 17.5%) is EBITA plus that depreciation. FactSet's history line for EBITDA (operating, SEK 5,499m for 2025) is within 0.3% of the brokers'-basis actual (5,515m), and its history EBIT (adjusted, SEK 3,774m) is after acquired amortisation, so the history EBIT margin (11.7%) is not comparable with the consensus EBIT margin (14.5%). My EBITDA input is on the brokers' basis: FY2025 17.1% (5,515m), FY2026 17.7%.

The FactSet consensus EPS (median 10.01, mean 9.57 for 2026) blends two bases and must not be used as a series: Pareto (8.46), Goldman Sachs (8.71) and two restricted brokers (8.40, 9.84) forecast reported EPS after acquired amortisation; DNB Carnegie (10.40) and Nordea (10.30) forecast adjusted EPS, which adds the amortisation back in full with no tax effect (the amortisation is largely non-deductible). FY2025 adjusted EPS on that basis is SEK 8.83 (net income 2,560m plus amortisation 656m, over 364.3m shares), so like-for-like consensus EPS growth for 2026 is about 17 to 24% on either basis, not the +42% the FactSet series implies. The consensus net income line (median 3,060m, five brokers) is on the reported basis and implies reported EPS of about SEK 8.40.

The Financial Analyst's model carries no acquired-amortisation line, so its earnings are on the adjusted basis (EBITDA less depreciation equals EBITA; less net financial items; less tax). To make the model's net income land on the brokers' adjusted basis, the tax rate in Part 1 is the reported tax charge (23.5% of reported pre-tax profit in 2026, 23.3% in 2027 and 2028, 23.4% in 2029, 23.5% in 2030, on an effective rate of 23.1% in 2025 and 24% in H1 2026, drifting up with the mix of higher-rate jurisdictions) expressed as a percentage of the model's pre-amortisation profit (EBITA less net financial items): 19.9%, 19.8%, 19.9%, 20.0%, 20.2%. The model's EPS should therefore be read against DNB Carnegie and Nordea, and reported EPS is the model's EPS less acquired amortisation per share: SEK 1.98 in 2026, 2.17 in 2027, 2.36 in 2028, 2.55 in 2029 and 2.75 in 2030. Earn-out remeasurements (SEK 208m positive in 2025's operating income, negative so far in 2026) and project write-downs (SEK 125m in Q4 2025) are set to zero in every year; they add a quarterly variance of 5 to 10% of EBITA that is not forecastable.

FactSet's "free cash flow" line in the financials block (SEK 2,397m for 2025) is operating cash flow less capital expenditure less cash dividends (3,990 less 502 less 1,091); the consensus "FCF estimate" for 2026 (2,566m) is on the same after-dividend basis. My cross-check free cash flow below is on a different and stated basis.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Earnings per share below is on the adjusted basis the model produces (reported net income plus acquired-intangible amortisation, over 364.3m shares), to compare like with like against the model; the corresponding reported EPS is SEK 8.30, 9.45, 10.54, 11.57 and 12.71. Free cash flow is operating cash flow after interest and tax, less capital spending including intangibles, less lease payments, before acquisitions, earn-out settlements and dividends; it absorbs only the organic growth in working capital, because the acquired companies' working capital is paid for in the acquisitions line. It equals about 96%, 102%, 105%, 105% and 105% of reported net income, and funds dividends of SEK 1.13bn to 1.7bn plus the SEK 2.3bn to 3.0bn acquisition programme with net debt roughly flat at SEK 8.3bn to 9.1bn (FactSet basis, including leases) and net debt to EBITDA falling from 1.35x at end-2026 to about 1.0x by 2030.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Indutrade AB (INDT-SE), a Swedish decentralised industrial group that acquires and owns more than 220 technical-trading and niche-manufacturing companies in more than 30 countries, reporting in five business areas: Industrial & Engineering, Life Science, Process Energy & Water, Infrastructure & Construction, and Technology & Systems Solutions.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Lifco (LIFCO.B-SE; the longest-running version of the same decentralised acquisition model, with EBITA margins of about 22 to 23% and a stated 2:1 dependence on acquired over organic growth); Addtech (ADDT.B-SE; technical trading, a fifth consecutive year of EBITA margin expansion to 16.0% in FY2025/26 on about 2% organic growth, and a plan to lift its deal pace to 15 to 20 a year after adding subdivision heads in late 2025); Lagercrantz (LAGR.B-SE; a proprietary-product mix shift toward 80% and a 20% EBITA margin target within two to three years); Diploma (DPLM-GB; acquisition-led growth with full-year guidance raised three times in 2026); OEM International (OEM.B-SE; a newly activated acquisition engine under EQT ownership and a centralised logistics cost model); Bufab (BUFAB-SE; a trading-heavy peer that returned to organic growth in 2026 with twelve consecutive quarters of gross-margin expansion); Beijer Ref (BEIJ.B-SE; a precedent of a serial acquirer that launched a SEK 100m warehouse-consolidation programme in October 2025); Storskogen and Bergman & Beving (precedents of decentralised roll-ups whose growth stalled or reversed); and the new Nordic platforms InstallatørGruppen, Tången and Auroora as competing bidders for the same private targets. Operating conditions: a European short-cycle industrial recovery from a two-year trough with purchasing managers' indices above 50 in Sweden and the eurozone from mid-2026; a rising-rate regime from October 2026 (a Riksbank hike expected in November 2026, markets pricing up to three ECB rises by mid-2027, the US ten-year yield at about 5.1%); a weak krona (EUR/SEK about 11.20 in October 2026) after a period of krona strength in 2025 and early 2026; a European construction and building-products recession expected to recover only in 2027; strong demand in healthcare and medical technology, electrification, grid, water and data-centre cooling; and a Nordic M&A market with more serial acquirers and private-equity platforms bidding for small private technical companies. Inputs: The expected change: a return to the company's own historical algorithm after two years in which organic sales were flat to negative (organic sales growth of -4%, -1%, 0%, 0% from Q2 2025 to Q1 2026, then +5% in Q2 2026 with organic order intake +8%, a book-to-bill of 105% and an order backlog 8% higher year on year, per the Q2 2026 interim report of 16 July 2026), the reported EBITA margin fell from 15.0% in 2023 to 13.8% in 2025 (14.1% excluding SEK 125m of UK project write-downs) and then recovered to 14.7% in Q2 2026, and the acquired contribution to EBITA fell to 4 to 5% in 2024 to 2025 before returning to 7 to 8% in H1 2026 on nine acquisitions adding SEK 1.5bn of annual sales. the precedents at Indutrade in 2010 to 2011 and 2021 to 2023 and at peers; (2) whether the 35-segment structure introduced in 2025 (each segment leader tasked with at least one acquisition a year from a mapped network the company says covers up to 1,500 candidates; the Addtech precedent of its 2021 and 2025 reorganisations; (3) whether the company can hold acquisition multiples at its stated 5 to 8x EV/EBITA on initial consideration (DNB Carnegie's peer table of 24 September 2026 records Indutrade's 2023 to 2025 median at 8.6x against 6.6 to 6.8x for Addtech, Lagercrantz and Lifco) while raising its deal count, and what happened to peers that raised the count; (4) whether the gross margin of about 35.5% on the company's definition (record levels in Q1 and Q2 2026) is a demonstrated strength that holds as the krona weakens and customer bargaining power returns, or depended on krona-driven purchasing gains that the chief financial officer acknowledged in late 2025;
