Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

The Investment Analyst's heading The fourth set: How far our assessment is reflected in company guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative

The fourth set as a whole ❌ D46 written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The gap between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect is small, and where it exists it runs against the investment case over the next 18 months and for it beyond. Company guidance is the model's own definition (10% growth, 14% margin, 20% return) and will neither be raised nor reduced inside the horizon; the margin floor is likely to be lifted to 15% at a 2028 Capital Markets Day if 2027 delivers, which would confirm what consensus already assumes. Consensus for 2026 and 2027 sits where our forecast sits on revenue and margin and above it on the below-EBITA lines, because the only published model lets net debt fall by not buying companies and assumes falling rates; the near-term revisions will be nudged higher by deals and currency through January 2027, held in April, and tested at the July 2027 print, where the sell-side's organic assumption (about 5% against a +5% base) is the most exposed number in the files; the revision breadth is at its two-year peak and can only fade. Consensus for 2028 and beyond is structurally too low by 3 to 6% because it models completed deals only, and the sell-side knows it, so the favourable gap there is between the published numbers and the published views rather than between the market and us. The narrative articulates almost everything prominently (the organic turn, the leverage, the deal pace and the 8.6x multiple, the margin-target refusal, Life Science, the yield-driven multiple, the control question, the earned-or-opportunity discount, the cohort tiering) and leaves unsaid the interest line under the rates regime, the krona's forecast reversal, the specific July 2027 test, and the understatement of cash conversion and deal compounding in the standard lines. With the share price at a 52-week high, 19% above its 200-day average, a 70% buy share, sell-side momentum rated A, three Holds held on valuation at targets equal to the price, and a template fair multiple that the research in all four sets does not support, the setup is one in which our forecast is about right, is already in the price for 18 months, and is under-priced only in the out-years and only if the deal engine runs at its 2026 pace; that is a modestly unfavourable gap now and a modestly favourable one for an investor whose horizon is three years.

Guidance

1 question
I-0148
I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the earnings events over the next 18 months, and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

❌ D48

Question asked How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

Judgement. The company gives no numerical guidance, so there is nothing to raise or reduce at any of the six to seven earnings events in the next 18 months (Q3 2026 today; Q4 2026 on 27 January 2027; Q1 2027 in April; Q2 2027 in July; Q3 2027 in October; Q4 2027 in January 2028; possibly Q1 2028 in April 2028), and the only quantity that will be "met" or not is the set of through-cycle targets, which will be met on margin, probably just missed on 2026 sales growth and clearly missed on return on capital. What moves at each event is the qualitative tone, and the record in the files says the tone is a reliable one-quarter lead on the direction of the next print: the shift from "market uncertainty" and "customer hesitation" (Q1 2025) to "cautiously optimistic" (Q1 2026) preceded the Q2 2026 turn, and the company does not pre-warn. On that basis I expect the tone to be held constructive through the Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 events (easy comparisons, closed deals, a backlog that "extends through" the following quarters, no recurring UK write-downs), with a probability of about 65% that it is held, 20% that it strengthens (a statement that costs will keep lagging sales, or that the pipeline supports a higher deal count) and 15% that it softens; and I expect the Q2 and Q3 2027 events to carry the first real risk of a softer tone (the hard comparison from Q2 2026, the rates regime, the krona-recovery path), with about 40% softer, 45% held, 15% stronger. Secondarily, the through-cycle targets reconfirmed in November 2025 will not change before the next Capital Markets Day on the three-year cadence (late 2028, outside the 18 months and inside the three years), and at that event a raise of the margin floor from 14% to 15% is the single most plausible change (about 40%), because the company has raised the floor at each of the last two Capital Markets Days (10% to 12% in 2018, 12% to 14% in 2022) and the forecast has the margin at 15.0 to 15.2% through 2027 and 2028; a raise of the growth or return targets is implausible (both are currently missed), and a reduction of any target is very unlikely (the floors are below delivery).

What each outcome would need, event by event.

  • Q3 2026 (today). Consensus needs organic growth of about 5 to 6% and a margin of about 15.1 to 15.2% (DNB Carnegie and Infront); the second set's inputs need 4% and 15.2%. A print at or above consensus with SG&A growth below sales growth would strengthen the tone ("a larger improvement in Q3" was the company's own phrase in July); organic growth of 3 to 4% with the margin at or above 15% would hold it; organic growth below 3% or a margin below 14.6% (the base) on positive organic growth would soften it. The five Q3 lines that reached the files (operating cash flow SEK 1,216m, net debt down sequentially, net financial items flat, contingent consideration up with thirteen deals closed) are consistent with a solid quarter and say nothing about the margin. The peers' Q3 prints (short-cycle names strong, Addtech and Lifco modest) raise the probability of an in-line-or-better organic print to about 60%.
  • Q4 2026 (27 January 2027). The easiest comparison of the cycle (0% organic, 13.3% reported margin with the write-down, 14.9% underlying). Reported growth will look strong whatever the run-rate; the tone will be set by the underlying margin against 14.9%, the 2026 deal count (fifteen to sixteen on the current pace against Redeye's eighteen; the company has never guided a count), the dividend proposal (DNB Carnegie models SEK 3.30; the second set 3.40; the chief executive has said the dividend rises in absolute terms every year, so anything from 3.20 up "meets"), and the first statement about 2027's backlog. Probability of a held-or-stronger tone about 85%.
  • Q1 2027 (April). Still an easy comparison (0% organic, 13.3%); seasonally the weakest margin quarter; the first report presented by Anna Vilogorac. The tone risk here is definitional rather than operational: a new chief financial officer from a listed investment company may add disclosure (a backlog figure, a price-volume comment) or change the "underlying" conventions, and either would be read as guidance even though it is not. Held about 70%.
  • Q2 2027 (July). The first hard comparison (+5% organic, 14.7%, +8% orders). DNB Carnegie's path has organic growth of 5.1% and a 15.1% margin in this quarter; the second set has 3% and 15.0%. If the quarter prints organic growth of 2 to 3% against the +5% base, which is my base case, the company will describe it as normalisation and the market will read it as the end of the recovery; this is the event at which a softer tone is most likely (about 40%) and at which the sell-side's own organic assumption is most exposed.
  • Q3 and Q4 2027. Hard comparisons again; the interest line visibly higher if the Riksbank and ECB have hiked; the krona possibly recovering. The tone will depend on the deal count (the forecast needs SEK 2.3bn of acquired sales in 2027) more than on organic growth, and the company has been consistent in describing the pipeline as strong; held about 55%, softer 30%, stronger 15%.

The targets, "met" or not, over 18 months. Sales growth of at least 10%: 2026 will land at about 8.5 to 10% (the second set 8.4%; consensus 9.7%; DNB Carnegie 10.0%; the October deals and neutral translation push toward the top of that range), so "met" is a coin toss on the reported figure and "missed" on an organic-plus-acquired constant-currency basis; 2027 at about 9% is a miss by the forecast and by consensus. EBITA margin of at least 14%: met in every quarter of the forecast and in every quarter since Q1 2026 except Q1 2026 itself (13.3%). Return on capital employed of at least 20%: 18% at June 2026; DNB Carnegie's 16% in 2027 and 17.4% in 2028 on its definition; not met in the horizon, and the sell-side uses this to justify the discount. Net debt to equity below 100% and the 30 to 50% payout: met with room. The company will present the record as "two of three financial targets met, the third improving", which is accurate and is how it presented 2025.

The secondary question: 18 months to three years. Three plausible changes at a 2028 Capital Markets Day, in order of likelihood: a margin floor of 15% (about 40%, conditional on 2027 printing at or above 15.0%; the mechanics that lift the margin, mix and leverage, do not require a policy change, and the company's stated reason for not raising it in 2025, protecting deal flexibility, weakens once the acquired companies themselves average 16 to 18%); a formal organic-growth or proprietary-share ambition of the Lagercrantz kind (about 20%; management has declined it twice and the culture resists central programmes); a change to the 20% ROCE target, either a redefinition or a lower figure (about 15%; it would be read as a concession). A raise of the 10% growth target is implausible at SEK 40bn of sales. The company could also lift the 14% floor earlier at a quarterly event, but it has never done so and the cadence argues against it. For the investment case, the favourable gap is that the forecast already sits above the margin floor by a full point and the market knows it, so a raise would confirm rather than surprise; the unfavourable gap is that there is no guidance to beat, so the upgrade cycle, if it comes, will be the sell-side's and the narrative's, not the company's.

How this compares with our assessment. Our forecast sits inside what the targets already permit (margin above the floor, growth below the target, returns below the target). There is no gap between our assessment and company guidance to exploit in either direction, because the company's guidance is the model itself. The only informational gap is the tone, which has been constructive since July 2026 and which we expect to stay so for two events and to be tested at the third and fourth.

Back to contents

Sell-side estimates

1 question
I-0250
I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (18 months): upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

C50

Question asked How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

Judgement. The consensus will most likely be nudged higher over the next two events and held-to-cut over the following three, with the net over 18 months a hold on FY2027 (within plus or minus 3%) and an upgrade on FY2028 and beyond (3 to 6%), and with the breadth of revisions, now at its most positive reading in two years (sell-side momentum rated A; FY2027 EPS +6% in six months; target prices +11%; buy share up 30 points to 70%), fading toward neutral by mid-2027 and turning negative for a quarter or two if the Q2 2027 comparison bites. The reason for the near-term nudge is mechanical and already visible: three acquisitions announced in the week of 19 to 23 October (Euromed SEK 230m, Sinmed 130m, Schraner 90m) add about 1.3% to 2027 sales that the completed-deal models will now book, the krona at 11.1 to 11.2 has turned translation from headwind to neutral-or-tailwind and DNB Carnegie already lifted 2026 to 2028 by 1% for it on 9 October, and the next two comparisons are the easiest of the cycle. The reason for the later hold-to-cut is that consensus for 2027 contains three assumptions our assessment does not share: organic growth of about 5% (DNB Carnegie's 5.1%; FactSet's implied 2027 composition per the T-I1 report) against our 3.5%; net financial items falling to about SEK -330m against our -485m (DNB Carnegie models a 5% interest rate but applies it to net debt falling to SEK 5.6bn by 2027 and 2.9bn by 2028, because its base case models completed deals only and therefore lets the cash pile up; our model keeps net debt at about SEK 10bn because it keeps buying); and a 2027 free cash flow jump of 58% on FactSet's definition. Those three are worth about 3 to 5% of 2027 EPS on the adjusted basis against the deals and currency worth +2 to +3%, which is why the net is a hold with a negative lean, and the lean is concentrated at the July 2027 print.

Where consensus sits against the run-rate and against us, by line. FY2026: revenue SEK 35,364m (requires H2 +13.9%; the second set has +11 to 11.5% and SEK 34,936m, -1.2%); EBIT (effectively EBITA) 5,127m (the second set 5,084m, -0.8%); adjusted EPS, DNB Carnegie 10.40 and Nordea 10.30 against the model's 10.28 and my corrected 10.20; reported EPS, Pareto 8.46 and Goldman Sachs 8.71 against the second set's 8.30. FY2027: revenue 38,375m (the second set 38,185m, -0.5%); EBIT 5,768m (5,727m, -0.7%); DNB Carnegie adjusted EPS 11.80 against the model's 11.62 and my 11.55 (-2%); reported-basis brokers 9.50 to 10.22 against 9.45. FY2028: revenue 40,308m (+5.0%) against the second set's 41,622m (+3.3% above); EBIT 6,039m against 6,327m (+4.8%); DNB Carnegie and Nordea adjusted EPS 12.80 to 13.00 against the model's 12.89. FY2029 rests on one or two brokers and the FactSet EPS "fall" to 10.70 is a coverage artefact. The shape of our difference is therefore: slightly below for 18 months, above thereafter, and the sell-side's own M&A scenarios (DNB Carnegie's 7% acquired EBITA growth to 2031 valued at SEK 281; Redeye's 18 deals a year and 9% sales growth to 2034) agree with our out-years while their base cases do not include them.

Revisions by event, with probabilities.

  • Q3 2026 (today). Upgrade (small, 1 to 3% on 2027 from deals, currency and an in-line-or-better print) about 55%; hold about 30%; cut (organic below 3% or the margin below 14.6%) about 15%. The breadth stays positive: the restricted brokers at Buy and the three Holds (DNB Carnegie, Pareto, Goldman Sachs) have targets of SEK 260 to 265 against a price of 262 to 267, so a beat would move targets, not ratings. Note that DNB Carnegie trimmed its target to SEK 260 on 5 October on yield spreads with unchanged estimates, which shows the yield channel can cut targets while estimates rise.
  • Q4 2026 (27 January 2027). Upgrade about 45%, hold 40%, cut 15%. The drivers are the deal count and the underlying margin; the dividend is a non-event; FactSet will roll the base year, which removes the "+42%" artefact and resets the headline growth rate to the like-for-like 17 to 24% for 2026 and about 10 to 13% for 2027.
  • Q1 2027 (April). Hold 50%, upgrade 30%, cut 20%; a seasonally weak margin against an easy base; the new chief financial officer; the first visible quarter of higher interest cost if the Riksbank has hiked in November and February.
  • Q2 2027 (July). Cut 40 to 45%, hold 40%, upgrade 15 to 20%. This is the event at which the consensus organic assumption (about 5%) meets the +5% base; the second set's 3% would be read as a deceleration, and the T-I1 report's own precedent (2022 to 2023: 12% organic and a record 15.2% margin followed by 0% and 14.6%) is the pattern. A 2% organic print at a 15.0% margin would take 2 to 3% off 2027 consensus EPS and would turn the revision breadth negative for the first time since April 2026.
  • Q3 and Q4 2027. Hold 45%, cut 30%, upgrade 25%; the interest line and the krona path on the downside, unannounced deals on the upside; the 2028 estimates start to absorb deals as they are announced, which is where the structural upgrade stream lives.

The breadth, and why it is at its peak. The dashboard's sell-side momentum score of 13 and the A rating reflect six-month revisions (EPS +6%, EBITDA +6%, sales +6%, target +11%, buy share +30 points) that were the rebuild from the April 2026 trough; one- and three-month revisions are already down to +1%, and the buy share has not moved in three months. The same pattern holds across the cohort (OEM International A, Diploma A, Beijer Ref B) and the names with A ratings are the ones whose share prices are at highs. From here the breadth can stay positive only on deals and currency until July 2027; it cannot rise much because the Holds are held on valuation (the earnings-yield spread to the swap rate), not on estimates, and a yield-driven regime cuts targets without cutting estimates.

The 18-month-to-three-year question. The consensus for 2028 and 2029 is structurally low because it models completed deals only (2028 revenue growth of 5.0%; DNB Carnegie's base-case net debt falling to SEK 2.9bn by 2028, which is the same fact seen from the balance sheet), and because the FactSet EPS series blends two bases and rests on one broker in 2029. Each year of SEK 2.3 to 3.0bn of acquired sales adds 4 to 5% to the following year's EBITA that is not in the base case until announced, so the 2028 consensus should be upgraded by 3 to 6% over the next 18 months if the deal pace holds (probability about 65%), held if the count falls to about fifteen a year at the larger size (25%), and cut only if the engine stalls as it did in 2024 to 2025 (10%). This is the one place where our assessment is clearly above consensus and where the gap is favourable; it is also the place the sell-side itself acknowledges (DNB Carnegie's SEK 281 M&A scenario against its SEK 260 target; Redeye's explicit modelling of unannounced deals), so the gap is between the base-case numbers and what the brokers believe, not between the brokers and us.

What would change this answer. A Q3 print today with organic growth of 6% or more and a margin above 15.5% would convert the near-term "nudge" into a round of 3 to 5% upgrades and would push the Holds toward Buy on estimates rather than on valuation; a print with organic growth below 3% would bring the Q2 2027 cut forward by three quarters. A Riksbank path that stops at 2.00% would remove most of our interest-line difference; a krona at 10.8 by mid-2027 would add a 2 to 3% translation cut to the consensus that nobody has modelled yet (every bank forecast in the files expects krona recovery; no broker model in the files has applied it).

Stock market narrative

1 question
I-0344
I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary: how well known is each to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

❌ D44

Question asked How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

Judgement. The sell-side narrative articulates almost every dynamic in our assessment, prominently and with views that mostly match ours; the investor narrative, as far as the files can see it, is narrower and concentrated on two things (the cohort's multiple against yields, and the control question after the UK write-down and the Lifco episode). The dynamics that are widely known and in the price are: the Q2 organic inflection and the debate over whether it is cyclical or structural; operating leverage on the 2023 to 2025 cost base; the restored deal pace; the 8.6x entry multiple and the 20% discount it helps justify; the margin-target refusal; Life Science's breadth beyond Novo Nordisk; the Process, Energy & Water backlog; the multiple's dependence on the swap rate; and the tiering of the cohort into proven and newly activated engines, with Indutrade placed firmly in the proven tier at a discount. The dynamics known to one or two commentators and not widely are: the two EPS bases and the blend distortion (DNB Carnegie publishes both; the FactSet consensus and the screens that use it do not); the structural under-modelling of 2028 and beyond (DNB Carnegie and Redeye know it and say so; the consensus numbers do not reflect it); and the cash drain below operating cash flow from leases and earn-outs (Redeye and DNB Carnegie on their definitions; the company's own 130% conversion figure is what the narrative repeats). The dynamics that no one in the files articulates are: the interest line under the October rates regime (the only published model, DNB Carnegie's, still has net financial items falling to SEK -330m in 2027 and -210m in 2028, and the T-I1 report itself contains a sentence asserting a rate-cutting cycle); the krona-recovery path in 2027 as a translation headwind (every bank forecasts it; no broker has modelled it); the Q2 2027 hard comparison as the specific event at which the organic assumption is tested (DNB Carnegie's path has 5.1% organic growth in that quarter, which is the opposite of an articulation of the risk); and the working-capital double count between acquired and organic balances that makes the standard free-cash-flow lines understate cash by about SEK 200m a year. For the investment case, the narrative gap is small and mostly adverse over 18 months (the things nobody has said are mostly negative for 2027 earnings) and modestly favourable from 18 months to three years (the deal stream, the balance-sheet capacity, and the resilience of organic earnings through Life Science and energy that retail commentary ignores).

The coverage the files can see, and its limits. The T-I2 report identifies DNB Carnegie and Redeye as the houses whose own documents it cites, with Deutsche Bank, Rothschild & Co Redburn, Morgan Stanley and Jefferies cited on peers; it claims coverage of about 85% of the research AlphaSense holds. The broker-definitions report (OWN-5) found no recent published models from Pareto, Nordea, Goldman Sachs, SEB, Jefferies, ABG, Kepler Cheuvreux, Handelsbanken or Danske in the database, so their views are known only from FactSet's numbers (Pareto Hold 265, Goldman Sachs Hold 261, Nordea Buy 322, two restricted Buys at 290 and 295) and from the T-I1 report's note that SEB upgraded to Buy in June 2026. Investor commentary is the weakest part of the evidence: the T-I2 report's statements about Avanza and Placera forums and about Redeye's community are sourced to DNB Carnegie and company documents rather than to the forums themselves, and there is nothing from X, Substack or podcasts. I therefore credit the sell-side findings with confidence and the investor findings with low confidence, and I write a research request for the latter.

Dynamic by dynamic: who, how prominently, since when, with what view.

  1. The Q2 2026 organic inflection. Everyone. DNB Carnegie (Q2 update 16 July, preview 5 October): the quarter "sorted out key concerns" but the comparison was easy and the recovery is cyclical until proven; Redeye (24 July): the order book outpacing sales makes it a runway into H2; Jefferies on the cohort: scope for re-rating if organic growth converges with demand. Retail (per the files): read as a cyclical bounce in a rate-sensitive market. The structural-versus-cyclical split is the most-discussed question in the narrative and it matches our own framing (cyclical turn with structural strands). Fully known.
  2. Operating leverage on the 2023 to 2025 cost base. DNB Carnegie's central thesis since July 2026 ("the SG&A ramp-up will bring good operational leverage as volumes return"); Redeye on the gross margin as the enabler; the risk that SG&A grows into the recovery was raised by Redeye in May 2025 and by DNB Carnegie after the Q1 2026 miss. Widely known; the specific rule we use (one-third of a point per point of organic growth) is not stated by anyone but is implicit in DNB Carnegie's margin path.
  3. The restored deal pace and the price paid. DNB Carnegie's 8.6x median against 6.6 to 6.8x at peers (24 September) is the single most repeated number in the sector commentary and the stated justification for the 20% discount; Redeye disputes the inference (multiples "have not risen significantly over time"; 7x initial, 9x with maximum earn-outs); the company's own nine-month aggregate of 1.22x sales is in the interim report and nobody has yet cited it. Known and contested; our view (structure and size explain most of the gap; discipline intact on EV/sales) is closer to Redeye's.
  4. The margin-target refusal. Discussed at and after the November 2025 Capital Markets Day by DNB Carnegie and Redeye and in the Nordic press; framed as pragmatic (protects deal flexibility) and as the reason the margin profile is structurally below Lagercrantz's 20% target and Lifco's 22 to 23%. Known; the history that the company raised the floor in 2018 and 2022 and so could raise it in 2028 is noted in the T-I1 report but not in any broker view.
  5. Life Science beyond Novo Nordisk, and the PEW backlog. DNB Carnegie (16 July: Life Science "can grow organically despite less support from pharma"; 5 October: Q3 organic growth led by Life Science and PEW); Redeye (24 July: PEW orders +16%, long lead times); the AddLife initiation (18 September) on the Polish Life Science market. Known to the sell-side; the T-I2 report says retail forums overlook the shift from pharma manufacturing to hospital medtech. This is one of the two places where the investor narrative under-appreciates a positive.
  6. The below-EBITA items. The two EPS bases: DNB Carnegie publishes reported 8.36 and adjusted 10.40 side by side; the OWN-5 report documents that the vendor consensus blends them and that sophisticated investors bypass the blend; no investor commentary in the files mentions it. The interest line: not articulated under the rates regime by anyone (see the judgement). Currency: DNB Carnegie updated for the weak krona (9 October) and the T-I1 report describes translation turning to a tailwind; the 2027 reversal is in the bank forecasts and in no broker model. Partly known; the 18-month negative (interest, krona) is the least-known item in the whole assessment.
  7. The control question and short-seller exposure. DNB Carnegie (30 January: "not a bad report when adjusting for one-offs"), Redeye (29 January: the write-down was large relative to a subsidiary's sales), and the governance report's absence of an internal audit function; the T-I2 report finds the sell-side "largely overlooks" decentralised control risk while retail forums raise it and argue 220 companies cannot be overseen without internal audit; the Lifco episode (16% fall on The Analyst's note) is cited by DNB Carnegie as the cohort's exposure. Known differently by the two audiences; our view (plausible recurrence at SEK 100 to 300m, the gap unchanged) is nearer the retail view than the sell-side's.
  8. The 20% discount: earned or opportunity. DNB Carnegie: earned (lower organic growth, ROCE, cash conversion, multiples paid; a 20% discount applied explicitly to reach 17.5x); Redeye: opportunity (19% below the stock's own two- and five-year medians; a 34x historical median P/E implies SEK 342); the T-I2 report finds retail focused on the historical multiple rather than the peer relative. Known; our view (earned, and closable only with standards management has declined) sides with DNB Carnegie.
  9. The cohort tiering. DNB Carnegie's "proven Swedish compounder group" (Addtech, Indutrade, Lagercrantz, Lifco at an average 21 to 22x EV/EBITA) against the unproven (14x) and the newly activated engines (OEM International at 25x under EQT); the sector digest's preference for "newly lit engines" over "mature engines that need the re-rating". Known; Indutrade is placed in the proven tier at the bottom of it, which is also our placement.

What the narrative does not contain that our assessment does, ranked by consequence for 18 months. (1) The interest line: a SEK 100 to 150m gap between DNB Carnegie's 2027 net financial items and ours, worth 2 to 3% of EPS, with every macro source pointing to hikes. (2) The Q2 2027 test: the consensus path puts 5.1% organic growth against a +5% base; our 3%. (3) The krona's forecast recovery in 2027. (4) The dispersion finding that quarterly outcomes widen in the first year of a recovery (the D2 report), which the narrative treats as having narrowed after Q2. (5) On the positive side for 18 months: the cash-conversion correction (about 100% of reported net income after leases, not the 63 to 69% of EBITA that the sell-side quotes), and the balance-sheet capacity to fund 50% more deals than the base case. (6) For 18 months to three years: the deal stream missing from 2028 consensus, which the sell-side knows and the consensus numbers do not carry.

How well known the dynamics are to the two audiences, summarised. To the sell-side covering the company: nearly everything, with views that match ours on the mechanics and diverge from ours on two timing points (2027 organic growth; the interest line) and one valuation point (DNB Carnegie's discount is "earned", Redeye's is "opportunity"; ours is earned). To investors in the company and the sector: the multiple's yield dependence (very well known, and the reason the cohort paused in October), the control risk (known to retail, discounted by institutions), and the organic turn (known, debated); not well known are the resilience of the structural strands, the deal stream's compounding effect beyond 2027, and the below-EBITA lines. The narrative's blind spots are mostly on the downside for the next 18 months and on the upside beyond, which is the configuration in which the gap is slightly unfavourable now and modestly favourable later.

Note for the Financial Analyst, no input changed: the gap between the second set's cross-check free cash flow (SEK 2,900m, 3,500m, 4,020m, 4,410m, 4,880m) and the model's (2,805m, 3,296m, 3,788m, 4,134m, 4,548m) is a definitional one. No input is wrong on its own definition, earnings per share are unaffected, and the model's year-end 2026 net debt (SEK 9.7bn on the original inputs) is close to the company's SEK 9.6bn at 30 September, so I leave the working-capital percentage unchanged rather than distort the balance-sheet ratio to correct a cash-flow classification.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Indutrade AB (INDT-SE), a Swedish decentralised industrial group that acquires and owns more than 220 technical-trading and niche-manufacturing companies in more than 30 countries, reporting in five business areas: Industrial & Engineering, Life Science, Process Energy & Water, Infrastructure & Construction, and Technology & Systems Solutions.

Request: OWN-1

Question: What did Indutrade AB (INDT-SE) report for the third quarter of 2026 in its interim report of 23 October 2026 (net sales, organic growth for the group and for each of the five business areas, order intake and book-to-bill, the order backlog, EBITA and EBITA margin for the group and each area, the gross margin, selling and administrative costs against sales, contingent-consideration remeasurements and any one-off items, operating cash flow and working capital, net financial items, net debt, acquisitions completed and announced), what did management say on the earnings call about demand by customer segment and geography, pricing, costs, the UK project-oriented companies, the acquisition pipeline and the fourth-quarter outlook, how did each line compare with the pre-report forecasts of DNB Carnegie (its preview of 5 October 2026 modelled net sales of SEK 8,896m and adjusted EBITA of SEK 1,348m) and Redeye and with the Infront or FactSet consensus (about SEK 8,827m and SEK 1,342m), how did the share price react on the day and over the following five trading days relative to Lifco, Addtech, Lagercrantz, OEM International, Bufab and the Carnegie Swedish small-cap index, and how did DNB Carnegie, Pareto, Nordea, Goldman Sachs, SEB, Redeye and any other covering broker change their estimates for 2026 to 2028, their ratings and their target prices in the days after the report?

Request: OWN-2

Question: What are investors, as distinct from sell-side analysts, saying about Indutrade AB (INDT-SE) and about the Swedish serial-acquirer cohort (Lifco, Addtech, Lagercrantz, OEM International) since the Q2 2026 report of 16 July 2026 and after the Q3 2026 report of 23 October 2026: in Swedish retail and professional investor forums (Avanza and Placera discussions, Redeye's community, Börssnack), on X and Substack, in podcasts covering Nordic compounders, and in the published letters and holdings disclosures of institutional holders (including Lundbergföretagen, AMF, Fidelity and Swedish small-cap funds); which topics dominate (the organic recovery, operating leverage, the acquisition pace and the prices paid, the November 2025 decision not to raise the 14% EBITA margin target, the SEK 125m UK project write-down of Q4 2025 and the absence of an internal audit function, The Analyst's January 2026 short-seller report on Lifco, the discount to Lifco, Addtech and Lagercrantz, the sensitivity of the cohort's multiples to Swedish and euro-area interest rates, and the weak krona); what views are expressed and by whom; how retail ownership and short interest in Indutrade have moved in 2026; and which of these topics the investor commentary discusses that the sell-side does not, and the reverse?

Request: OWN-3

Question: At what forward P/E and EV/EBITA multiples did Indutrade AB (INDT-SE) trade at comparable points in its previous cycles (the recovery from the 2009 downturn through 2010 to 2011, the 2016 to 2017 upturn, and the 2020 to 2021 recovery), how many quarters after the first quarter of positive organic growth did the multiple and the share price peak in each, what did the multiple do in the subsequent period when year-on-year comparisons hardened, and how did the same sequence play out for Lifco, Addtech and Lagercrantz in the same cycles and in their most recent recoveries; what target multiples did DNB Carnegie, Pareto, Nordea, Goldman Sachs, SEB and Redeye apply to Indutrade at each of those points and how did they justify them; and what do brokers and investors say now, after the Q3 2026 reporting season across the cohort, about the multiple range they consider fair for Indutrade over the next one to three years given the Riksbank and ECB rate paths, the ten-year euro swap rate, and the company's discount to its proven-compounder peers?
